TT Electronics · TTG-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the TT Electronics memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on TT Electronics was written: 51,170 words, against 10,980 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 24,767 words · written 22 October 2026, 16:54 to 17:17 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
TT Electronics (TTG-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of an investment case

Written 2026-10-22 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (the second set).

Orientation: the base the Consumer models from (information, condensed)

  • Reporting: December year end, semi-annual. Last reported period is H1 2026 (six months to 30 June 2026, reported 2 September 2026); I confirmed this against the latest Earnings Review, so the caution in the instruction is resolved. Next results: FY2026 on 10 March 2027. From April 2026 the group reports three product-led divisions (Power, EMS, Components) in place of three regions; every pre-2026 regional and end-market figure below sits on the retired basis.
  • FY2025 (as reported): revenue £481.4m (-7.6% statutory, -2.7% organic); adjusted operating profit £37.2m (7.7% margin, up 60bp); adjusted EPS 6.9p on a 57.1% adjusted effective tax rate (25.4% if US deferred tax assets were recognised); statutory operating loss £28.2m after £65.4m of adjusting items; cash conversion 150% (£14.8m inventory release); net debt ex leases £50.3m, covenant leverage 1.1x; dividend nil. Restated FY2025 divisions: Power £206.3m revenue / £33.5m adjusted operating profit (about 16%); EMS £195.8m / £9.6m (about 5%); Components the balance (about £79m) loss-making before central costs. FY2025 end markets: Aerospace and Defence £152.8m (+12% organic), Automation and Electrification £140.1m (-13%), Healthcare £107.8m (-4.3%), Distribution £80.7m (-4.7%).
  • H1 2026: revenue £228.1m (-2.7% organic; +4% excluding the Plano closure and the Suzhou-to-Kuantan transfer); adjusted operating profit £18.5m (+37%, margin 8.1%, +230bp); adjusted EPS 5.7p (tax 36.3%); cash conversion 42% (£12.7m working-capital build), free cash flow -£0.4m; net debt ex leases £52.0m, 1.1x; order book about £550m (+20%), book-to-bill 1.12x. Divisions: Power £97.3m flat, profit £13.8m (-8%), margin 14.2% (-120bp), book-to-bill 1.14x; EMS £94.1m (-8.3%), margin 7.9% (+390bp); Components £36.7m (+5.8% organic), profit £1.0m versus a £1.9m loss, margin 2.7% (+820bp).
  • Guidance: FY2026 adjusted operating profit "ahead of market expectations" (the company-compiled consensus range before the upgrade was £32.6m to £38.5m); FY2026 cash conversion 70% to 80%; FY2026 adjusted tax rate mid-to-high 30s%; £3m net cost savings in FY2026 rising to over £6m annualised from FY2027; medium-term targets of 4% to 6% organic growth, a 12% adjusted operating margin (undated), mid-to-high-teens ROIC, 90%+ cash conversion, leverage below 1.5x; dividend decision deferred to March 2027.
  • Consensus (FactSet median, 22 October 2026, four brokers): revenue £482m / £503m / £525m / £535m for FY2026 to FY2029; EBIT £40.0m / £45.0m / £50.0m / £52.9m (8.3% to 9.9%); adjusted EPS 11.9p / 15.9p / 17.8p / 19.9p; free cash flow £9.0m / £18.9m / £26.7m / £30.4m; capex £12.5m to £14.0m; dividend nil then 2.5p, 3.1p, 4.3p. Ratings: Investec Buy 205p, Stifel Buy 155p, Jefferies Hold 175p, RBC Hold 190p; consensus target 185p to 190p.
  • Price 186p (22 October 2026), market capitalisation £347m, enterprise value £402m, 52-week range 93p to 199p, +95.6% over twelve months, relative-strength percentile 98; 24-month-forward P/E 10.5x, the 93rd percentile of its five-year range (median 7.2x) and roughly its ten-year median (9.8x). Average value traded about $1m a day. DBAY Advisors holds 29.04% (TR-1, August 2026) after blocking Cicor's recommended 150p offer in January 2026.

External change forces

C6010 questions
C-0166C-0248C-0358C-0462C-0545C-0664C-0740C-0865C-0950C-1060

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external picture is a moderate, broadening tailwind arriving at a company that spent the last eighteen months facing nothing but headwinds. The structural force (European and NATO rearmament reaching the qualified-component tier where TT's Power division lives) is the most reliable and has two-plus years of visibility; the cyclical force (electronics restocking, semiconductor capital equipment) is real, is already in TT's orders and Components growth, and is time-limited to six to eighteen months; the regulatory force adds demand and compliance cost in roughly equal measure; suppliers are tightening modestly; competition is rising only at the commodity edge. The one large external negative is outside the income statement: a risk-off, high-discount-rate regime that punishes small, illiquid, recently re-rated stocks, which is what TT now is. For the model: external forces add roughly 3 to 5 points of organic growth across H2 2026 and FY2027 (defence plus restocking plus the lapping of self-inflicted removals) and 2 to 4 points in FY2028 (defence continuing, restocking plateauing); sterling is a mild reported tailwind; input costs trim gross margin percentage for two quarters at a time; and the multiple, not the earnings, is where the external risk sits. The answers to C-01, C-06 and C-08 carry the growth mechanics; C-07 and C-10 carry the caution.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and visibility

C66

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement: demand is turning up for the company as a whole, but through three mechanisms with very different visibility, and the Consumer should model them separately rather than as one growth rate. (1) Aerospace and Defence, about a third of sales and two thirds of Power, is a structural, funded, multi-year upswing: European defence budgets (France's EUR 36bn programme to 2030, the UK's 2.5% of GDP by 2027, the EU AGILE and European Defence Fund money), a European book-to-bill of 135% in 2025 against a sustainable 100% to 115%, and post-period lifetime and framework awards (Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement, MBDA letter of intent, the $10.3m Collins Aerospace classified programme, Tempest feasibility work). Visibility here comfortably exceeds two years because platforms (Boxer, CV-90, Challenger 3, Typhoon) run 15 to 30 years and the parts are designed in; this is the one end market that qualifies as a cyclical-plus-structural swing with two-plus years of sight. (2) Short-cycle industrial, semiconductor equipment and distribution (Automation and Electrification plus Distribution, about 46% of FY2025 sales) are at the start of a cyclical restocking turn with poor visibility: components order visibility is about twelve weeks, the sector digest dates the restocking pulse at six to eighteen months and expects it to plateau by the fourth quarter of 2026, XP Power's semiconductor orders rose 116% and discoverIE's organic orders 31%, and TT's own Components division returned to 5.8% organic growth in H1 2026 "supported by stronger distributor markets". This is a refill-and-use recovery, not yet proven as use, and it is the segment where consensus revenue growth of 4% in FY2027 and FY2028 will be won or lost. (3) Healthcare (22% of sales) is stabilising after US research-grant cuts and OEM destocking (-4.3% in FY2025, -6% at the trough); management calls it "stabilising", Volex's medical sales still fell 11% in its FY2026, so I treat it as flat-to-low-single-digit for 18 months with no visibility beyond the order book. Why this matters for the model: the H1 2026 headline organic decline of 2.7% masks underlying growth of about 4% once the two self-inflicted volume removals (Plano, Suzhou) are stripped out; those removals annualise during H2 2026, so reported organic growth should flip positive in H2 2026 even without any demand improvement, and the £550m order book (about 13 months of revenue at the H1 run rate) covers consensus FY2027 revenue of £503m almost entirely. The skew of twelve-month paths is to the upside on volume (order book plus restocking plus defence), but the cone widens sharply in months 12 to 24 if the restocking pulse fades before use demand arrives.

C-02

Competitive intensity: extent and direction of change

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising modestly at the commodity edge of TT's perimeter and is flat-to-easing in its qualified core, so the net effect on the group is slightly negative and concentrated in the two divisions (Components and EMS) that carry the lowest margins. Evidence of rising intensity: Cicor has become the number four European EMS provider (6% pro forma share) partly by buying TT's own former sites and is explicitly acquisitive; Volex "bids aggressively, squeezes intermediate suppliers over the subsequent 18 months, and raises prices at renewal" and wins without five-year contracts (a former TT business-development director's account); XP Power's FLXPro silicon-carbide and gallium-nitride range targets the same high-voltage specialist applications; Indian and other Asian EMS producers enjoy subsidies that widen their cost advantage; and in tight supply larger players secure component allocations that sub-scale providers cannot. Evidence of stability: the 70% to 80% of TT's sales that are low-volume, high-specification, regulated product sit behind 18-to-24-month requalification and site-level accreditations (AS9100D, FDA, ISO 13485), the mega-scale EMS groups avoid these niches, and tier-two specialists "maintain pricing discipline in specialised programmes while avoiding volume-led price wars". The important nuance for the model is that intensity has not been visible in TT's own win rates (Cleveland won three new customers and six products in H2 2025 after a three-year drought; Power's book-to-bill is 1.14x) but is visible in the margin structure: the group's EMS and Components margins (7.9% and 2.7%) are where price-taking shows, and the Power division (14.2%) is where it does not.

C-03

Disruption forces: strengthening or disproved, and direction

C58

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: the disruption force most relevant to TT, platform electrification and the move to wide-bandgap (silicon-carbide) power conversion, is strengthening and acts in TT's favour over a five-to-ten-year horizon, but it is too early and too small to enter an 18-month-to-four-year model as revenue. TT has delivered first prototype high-power-density silicon-carbide modules using silver sintering to a major aerospace prime, launched the AX-FORCE family and the Altitude DC architecture (co-developed with the Aerospace Technology Institute), and holds early-stage work on Tempest, uncrewed platforms and eVTOL. The customer-side trend toward buying pre-integrated subsystems rather than components also favours a box-build house. Against that, three disruption vectors act against the smaller parts of the business: software-defined architectures and centralised compute that commoditise discrete sensors (relevant to Components), advanced packaging that moves interconnects inside the semiconductor package, and the capital-intensity barrier of lights-out automation at Asian giants. Adoption of solid-state transformers and 800V data-centre architectures is slow (cost premiums, certification delays past 2028), so the upside from TT's roadmap is real but back-end loaded; the honest modelling treatment is zero incremental revenue from it before 2028 and an unquantified option thereafter. The one thing that would be disproved is the bear line that TT's technology is being left behind: it is not.

C-04

Political and regulatory change, and how it bears on the company

C62

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: political and regulatory change is net positive for demand and net negative for cost and agility, with the demand effect larger. Positive: European rearmament is the single largest external tailwind in the file (UK 2.5% of GDP by 2027, France EUR 36bn to 2030 with munitions up 53%, EU AGILE EUR 115m for 2027 inside the EUR 7.3bn European Defence Fund), and TT is designed into the land and air platforms that this funds; the US CHIPS Act and customer demands for China-plus-one supply chains drove the Suzhou-to-Kuantan transfer and are unlocking new healthcare and aerospace orders at the Malaysian site. Negative: US Executive Order 14415 (July 2026) ends critical-minerals waivers from 1 January 2027 and requires an indentured bill of materials within 180 days, so TT's US defence work (Kansas City, Minneapolis) carries new supply-chain audit cost and a tail risk of a non-compliant tier-three supplier; NIST SP 800-171 scoring acts as a gate to future US awards; UK Autumn Budget employer costs; and the UK National Security and Investment Act review that applied to the Cicor scheme raises the bar for any foreign acquirer, which matters because a bid is part of the equity's history. Tariffs are a wash at the operating-profit line because contracts carry co-terms that pass duties to customers (management: "it's the customer that bears those costs"), but they slowed Asian ordering in 2025 (Asia organic -9.2%) and that drag is now easing. The watch item is timing, not direction: the sector digest's evidence (Solid State's revenue swinging £163m to £125m to £154m on award cadence; 150 US programmes delayed by stopgap budgets) says defence revenue follows procurement timing, so a backlog is safe and a half year is not.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: net headwind or tailwind

❌ D45

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement: a modest and tightening headwind for the next six to twelve months, mostly to margin percentage rather than gross profit pounds, with a second-order benefit that extending lead times make customers order earlier and inflate book-to-bill. The evidence of tightening: memory (DRAM) contract prices have risen sharply since 2024 and are still rising on high-bandwidth-memory allocation; European PCB capacity is booked into 2028 and PCB lead times have stretched from 8 to 20 weeks; Avnet reports lead-time extensions in over half its tracked categories; bespoke connectors are constrained; mature-node and power-management IC lead times run 40 to 52 weeks. TT's defences are contractual and historical rather than structural: indexed pass-through clauses recover 80% to 90% of input inflation with a three-to-six-month lag (shorter-cycle fixed-price contracts lag six to twelve months), management recovered £40m of input inflation in the 2021 to 2023 cycle, and Project Dynamo consolidated freight providers. The structural weaknesses are scale (sub-scale EMS providers "are forced to rely on sourcing partners" for allocations) and a history of stock-outs cited by industry experts. Supplier concentration is not a group-level problem (diverse vendors) but is a product-level one (limited alternatives for certain proprietary technologies). For the model: assume gross margin percentage gives back 30 to 60bp in a period of fast input inflation and recovers it two quarters later, and recognise that the H1 2026 inventory build (£12.7m) was partly deliberate insurance against exactly this.

C-06

Where the company's own revenue and demand cycle is, and whether it is turning

C64

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement: the company is at the bottom of a three-year revenue decline (£617m in FY2022 to £481m in FY2025, roughly -11% compound including disposals) and the cycle is turning, but the turn is so far better evidenced in orders and in underlying growth than in reported revenue. Reported organic growth was -2.7% in FY2025, -4.8% over the first four months of 2026 and -2.7% for H1 2026; underlying growth excluding Plano and Suzhou was +4% in H1 2026; the order book is £550m (+20%) at a 1.12x book-to-bill, up from 1.09x in FY2025 and 1.02x in FY2024; Power's book-to-bill is 1.14x and finalised customer agreements are expected to lift H2 Power revenue; Components has already returned to growth; management expects "a return to organic revenue growth in H2". The two self-inflicted drags annualise: Plano's revenue is gone from the base after H2 2026 and the Kuantan site is through its transfer and is expected at full rate by year end. Consensus revenue of £482m for FY2026 (flat) therefore implies H2 revenue of about £254m, +4% on H2 2025's £244m, which is exactly what the order book and the lapping of the drags would produce without any help from end markets; FY2027's +4.4% then requires end markets to contribute. The persistence question is whether the restocking element fades in 2027; the structural element (defence) does not.

C-07

Macro force: on the company, sector and industry, and where it bites hardest

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: the macro force bites hardest at the sector and company level through the discount rate and the small-cap fragility channel, not through TT's own demand, and it is the main reason the equity's path over twelve months is wider than its earnings path. The portfolio's environment reading (16 October 2026) is a discount-rate shock with the US ten-year at about 5.1%, hiking paths pulled forward, risk-off led by AI financing and Asia, defensives bid for four weeks, Fragility Double Underweight and a market that "refuses re-ratings". TT is small (£347m), thinly traded (about $1m a day), 1.1x levered, has a 29% blockholder, and has just re-rated 95% in twelve months to the 93rd percentile of its own five-year forward P/E range: it is the archetype the regime punishes. On demand, higher rates cost about 0.6% of developed-market output per 100bp, which would weigh on the 46% of sales in short-cycle industrial and distribution, but no house forecasts recession and manufacturing PMIs (UK 51.7, Eurozone 52.7, Germany 54.3) are expanding. Currency is a second macro channel: 79% of sales are outside sterling, a 1 cent move in GBP/USD moves operating profit by about £0.1m, consensus expects sterling to drift weaker toward $1.31 to $1.34, so FX is a mild tailwind to reported figures in 2027 after three years of cumulative £38.1m revenue headwinds. Energy-led inflation would hit the furnace-intensive sites and the fixed-price portion of contracts. Net: the macro cone is wider than the company's own, and the environment is an argument for the Consumer to lean forecasts on the discount rate and the multiple rather than on the order book.

C-08

A key industry or sector driver accelerating the company's demand

C65

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: three sector drivers are live and TT is exposed to all three, but the exposure to the strongest one (defence) is the widest and the exposure to the fastest (semiconductor capital equipment) is the narrowest, so the group's demand acceleration will be slower and steadier than the headline sector moves suggest. Defence: the qualified-component tier is receiving rearmament money with one-and-a-half-to-four-year qualification keeping incumbents in place; TT's Power division (two thirds A&D) is where this lands and is already the group's profit engine (about 16% margin on 43% of revenue). Semiconductor equipment: wafer-fabrication demand is in "blow-growth mode", XP Power's orders rose 116% and its book-to-bill reached 1.81x, and TT supplies Edwards Vacuum PCB assemblies and box-builds, but semiconductor customers are only about 10% of the Automation and Electrification segment (roughly 3% of group), so this adds perhaps 1 point of group growth at most. Electronics restocking: book-to-bill is above 1.0 across the cohort (discoverIE 1.15, Huber+Suhner 1.19, TT 1.12), industrial distributors say stock is too low (28% of them), and lead times are back at 12 to 16 weeks; this lifts Components and distribution-channel sales for six to eighteen months. The modelling insight is that these drivers are additive in H2 2026 and FY2027 (defence plus restocking plus semi) and then diverge in FY2028 (defence continues, restocking plateaus), which argues for front-loading the growth in the forecast rather than spreading 4% evenly.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

C50

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement: three other external dynamics are material. (1) Corporate control: TT has been the subject of two unsolicited approaches (Volex at 135.5p to 139.6p in late 2024, withdrawn rather than overpay) and one recommended offer (Cicor at 150p to 155p, lapsed January 2026 after DBAY voted it down), and the sector digest records that a cleaned-up mid-cap in this sector "often ends in a sale to private or strategic capital"; the share price at 186p is now above both approaches, so the bid floor is below the price and the bid dynamic is no longer a support, it is a memory. (2) Currency: sterling is forecast weaker, which helps reported numbers, but the hedge programme (75% of expected exposures over 12 months, 50% over 12 to 24) leaves translation unhedged, so the Consumer must model sterling as its own line (the Geography digest's instruction). (3) The AI data-centre wave: TT has essentially no exposure, so it has missed the tailwind that lifted Volex 92% in twelve months and it will not suffer when that financing cracks; this is why TT's relative-strength percentile of 98 is a turnaround re-rating, not a theme trade, which matters for how the shares behave if the AI chain sells off further. A fourth, smaller dynamic is the sell-side's coverage rebuilding from two active brokers to four (RBC, Investec, Stifel now alongside Jefferies), which widens the audience just as the price has caught up with targets.

C-10

Each thematic driver in turn, then the whole: headwinds and tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and the overall external picture

C60

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement, driver by driver. Value-chain level: demand is a tailwind (defence structural, restocking cyclical, healthcare neutral), competition a mild headwind at the commodity edge, suppliers a mild and tightening headwind, regulators a net tailwind with a compliance cost, disruption a long-dated tailwind. Industry level (industrials and capital goods): the market is paying for scarce capacity and proven conversion, not for channel or unfenced incumbents; TT is a conversion story (backlog into invoiced revenue and cash) and that is the right side of the industry trade for the next twelve months. Sector level (engineered components and electronics manufacturing): the sector digest's rules are that electronics destocking is over, only designed-in parts reprice, a clean-up re-rates in two steps (the event, then the first clean margins), defence margins hold but shares follow award timing, and a bid sets a floor only where the market had not already priced the label. TT has had step one of the re-rating (the event: H1 2026 beat and upgrade) and the second step (clean margins without adjusting items and with cash conversion restored) is still ahead. Overall external change picture: moderately positive and improving, with tailwinds that are broad but individually modest and headwinds that are easing rather than gone. The structural tailwind (defence) is the most reliable and the restocking tailwind the most time-limited; the macro and discount-rate headwind is the largest and sits outside the company's control. Comparing the next six months with the last twelve to eighteen: eighteen months ago every external force was adverse (destocking, tariffs deferring Asian orders, grant cuts, sterling strength, CEO departure); today three of five are supportive. The Consumer should model external forces as adding roughly 3 to 5 points of organic growth in H2 2026 to FY2027 and 2 to 4 points in FY2028, and should hold the multiple, not the earnings, as the external risk.

Internal change forces

C6613 questions
C-1170C-1266C-1362C-1466C-1570C-1655C-1755C-1847C-1952C-2056C-8642C-8750C-2166

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Internal change is the strongest part of TT's case and the part the Consumer can model with most confidence. In eighteen months the company has replaced its CEO, CFO, Chair and audit leadership, abolished its regional structure, closed two loss-making sites and transferred a third, fixed its worst factory, substantially completed a cost programme, halved leverage, removed a going-concern uncertainty, re-cut incentives toward profit and cash, run a disposal review to a saleable conclusion, and raised guidance for the first time in three years; seven of the eight corporate-change markers are present and dated. The changes are strong (the margin has already moved from 5.8% to 8.1% half-on-half), broad (people, structure, footprint, cost, incentives, portfolio, balance sheet), and adequately resourced for their purpose, which is repair rather than growth: capex, R&D and M&A are deliberately starved, so the programme delivers margin and cash, not revenue. The negatives inside the programme (42% H1 cash conversion, Power's 120bp margin slip, the unfinished Kuantan ramp, a new team not yet tested together, a 29% blockholder with a veto) are execution and sequencing risks rather than strategic ones. For the model: the internal items explain the EBIT path from £37m to about £45m to £47m by FY2027 without needing demand (Plano gone, Cleveland profitable, £6m of savings, Kuantan restored, Power phasing, lower interest), the Components disposal is a dated scenario worth about 100bp of margin and £35m to £40m of proceeds, and the tax asset is the one internal item that could move EPS discontinuously. Nothing in the internal programme supplies growth above the order book.

C-11

Change in chief executive, key leaders and key actors, and what it signals

C70

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: the leadership has been rebuilt in full within eighteen months and the rebuild signals a shift from growth rhetoric to operational and financial control, which is the right signal for a company whose last five years were defined by factory-floor failure and a prior-period restatement. Peter France left with immediate effect on 9 April 2025 after 18 months; Eric Lakin (joined as CFO Designate in January 2025, ex Smiths Interconnect CFO, Ceres Power CFO, chartered engineer and management accountant) became acting CEO the next day and permanent CEO on 12 August 2025 after an external search; Mark Hoad retired as CFO after ten years and Richard Webb bridged fourteen months as interim until Ian Ashton (ex SIG plc CFO) started on 29 June 2026; Warren Tucker stepped down as Chair after six years and Phil Swash (BAE Systems, Airbus, GKN) took over on 15 May 2026; Mary Waldner (ex Ultra Electronics and Senior plc finance) became Senior Independent Director and Audit Chair on 27 August 2026; Karina Rigby (Eaton, Siemens) joined in October 2025; investor relations moved to an external agency; the three regional heads were brought onto the executive committee, then the regions were abolished in favour of product divisions with rebuilt site leadership at Cleveland. The other key actor is DBAY Advisors at 29.04%, which blocked the Cicor sale and whose prior advisory relationship with the new Chair was reviewed and cleared. What it signals: a finance-and-restructuring executive backed by an aerospace-industrial Chair and an audit-grade SID, chosen to deliver margin and cash rather than top-line ambition, with a blockholder that evidently believes the equity is worth more than 150p. The risks are a CEO with no prior standalone P&L role, a CEO and CFO both under two years in post, and thin internal successors.

C-12

Change in strategy or priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

C66

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: the strategy has changed from "grow and acquire toward 12% by 2026" (the April 2024 Capital Markets Day) to "stabilise, simplify, self-help, then grow", and it is clearly articulated and adequately, though not generously, resourced. The four stated priorities are divisional realignment (completed April 2026: Power, EMS, Components, with Fairford moved to Power and Sheffield to Power), cost reduction (£3m net in FY2026, over £6m run-rate from FY2027, self-funded with about £3m of restructuring cost in H1), sales transformation (upgraded business-development talent and pipeline management, credited with the 1.12x book-to-bill and the Rolls-Royce and MBDA awards), and portfolio optimisation (the Components strategic review completed and a divestment being evaluated against "encouraging indications of interest", strictly subject to value). The articulation is good: each priority has a measurable output and management has already withdrawn the undated 12% target from 2026 rather than defend it. The resourcing is modest: capex at 1.8% of sales in FY2025 against peers near 3.5%, R&D at 3.8% of sales against XP Power's 12%, the cost programme deliberately "administrative" to preserve engineering, and growth capital reserved for after deleveraging. The judgement for the model is that this is a credible operating plan with a margin and cash pay-off that can be forecast, and a growth plan that cannot yet be: the strategy buys margin from 7.7% toward 9% to 10% with high confidence and buys growth above 4% with low confidence.

C-13

Guidance raised, mid-term targets set or raised

C62

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: FY2026 guidance was raised once, in September 2026, from "in line with consensus" (£34.8m at the March midpoint) to "ahead of market expectations", which Jefferies read as a high-single-digit consensus upgrade to £37m to £39m and consensus has since moved to £40.0m EBIT; this is the first upgrade in at least three years and it came after a 12% beat of compiled consensus at H1. Medium-term targets were not raised: the 12% margin was reiterated but pushed from "by 2026" to an undated medium term; 4% to 6% organic growth, mid-to-high-teens ROIC and 90%+ cash conversion were maintained; and the dividend decision was deferred to March 2027 rather than reinstated. No capital-markets-day ambition has been set under the new team. For the Consumer: the raise is real but small (about £3m to £5m on £35m), it was triggered by self-help rather than demand, and the most important forward number (FY2027) carries no guidance at all, so consensus of £45m EBIT rests on brokers' extrapolation of the savings run-rate and the order book. The direction of travel of guidance is now up for the first time since 2022, which is the fact that matters for the narrative.

C-14

Improving standards in execution, operations, governance and cost discipline

C66

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: standards are improving measurably from a low base, and the improvement is the best-evidenced internal change in the file. Execution: Cleveland productivity rose from 57% in Q3 2024 to 78% in Q4 2025 and 81% by January 2026 (target), rework fell from 12% to 6% to 5%, the site broke even in Q4 2025 and was consistently profitable through H1 2026, and it won three new customers and six products in H2 2025; Plano was closed within two months of the decision; the Suzhou-to-Kuantan transfer completed on plan at a £10m temporary revenue cost. Cost discipline: £3m of net savings on track, restructuring self-funded, freight consolidated, bids and pricing now subject to "rigorous bid and pricing discipline" with business turned away below margin mandates. Governance: a 71% independent board with a new audit-grade SID, a 50% non-audit fee cap (stricter than the 70% statutory limit), malus applied to 2023 awards, zero bonus and zero LTIP vesting for 2024, STIP re-weighted to 50% adjusted operating profit and 25% free cash flow, and a Provision 29 readiness project. The caveats are that Deloitte still runs a fully substantive audit with zero reliance on internal controls because of decentralised systems, that the £5.7m 2023 restatement came from "inappropriate direction from senior finance staff" at Cleveland, and that the standards are those of a competent mid-cap, not an outlier. The modelling implication is that the North American profit drag (a £2.7m regional loss in 2024, Plano's £5.7m loss) has been removed and is unlikely to recur in the forecast period.

C-15

Reducing perimeter or composition

C70

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: the perimeter is being cut purposefully and this is the most financially consequential internal change still ahead. Done: Project Albert (three low-margin sites to Cicor, Q1 2024, £70.2m of sales removed, a 50 to 70bp margin uplift), Plano closed (late 2025, a £5.7m to £6m annual loss removed at about £4m cash cost), Mexicali closed (H1 2026, £1.0m restructuring and £3.8m impairment), Suzhou refocused on Asia-for-Asia. In progress: the Components divestment, with indications of interest received and the division restored to a small profit (£1.0m in H1 2026, 2.7% margin, 5.8% growth) to strengthen the negotiating hand; Jefferies' sum-of-the-parts values it at £38.2m enterprise value (5.0x EBITA). The arithmetic the Consumer should carry: Components is about £75m to £80m of revenue at roughly 2% to 3% margin, so its removal takes FY2026 group revenue to about £405m and lifts the group margin by roughly 100bp (from about 8.3% to about 9.3%) before any use of proceeds; proceeds of £35m to £40m would take net debt ex leases from about £45m to under £10m, remove roughly £2m of interest, and bring a dividend forward. The sale also removes the division with the highest operating leverage and shortest visibility (12-week orders, distributor channel), so predictability improves as well as margin. The risk is that it is sold cheaply into a restocking upturn or not sold at all, leaving a sub-scale division in a consolidating market.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement: the company is not entering new markets in any material way; it is relocating within existing ones, and that is sensible for a business in this condition. The moves are the Kuantan expansion (new SMT lines and warehousing to receive a major EMS customer from Suzhou and to serve customers that need a non-China footprint, now unlocking healthcare and aerospace orders), US defence depth (the $10.3m Collins Aerospace programme from Kansas City, ITAR-compliant facilities), and the technology roadmap (SiC modules, AX-FORCE, Altitude DC) into the aerospace customers it already serves. Nothing is being attempted in data centres, EV or consumer, where peers have grown fastest, and the new-market option that matters (a US antenna or power footprint beyond Kansas) is not on the table while the balance sheet is being repaired. For the model this means growth is in-market share gain and mix, not addressable-market expansion, which caps the organic growth ceiling near the 4% to 6% target.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement: real pricing is partly real and partly latent, and it is concentrated in Europe and Power. Evidence of real action: the commercial sales transformation "identified underperforming legacy contracts for active price renegotiation" and the renegotiation of low-margin European contracts contributed to the 240bp European margin expansion to 15.3% in FY2025; management "turned away business that did not meet margin mandates"; Components' H1 2026 return to profit was attributed partly to "strategic self-help pricing actions". Evidence of nominal-only pricing: the 80% to 90% indexed pass-through recovers cost, not margin, and dilutes percentage margins; fixed-price multi-year OEM contracts cap recovery; tariff co-terms are pass-through. Latent and increasingly noted: the sector digest's finding that only designed-in custom parts can raise prices while standard parts pass cost through late applies to TT's mix exactly, so pricing power exists in roughly the 70% to 80% that is custom and is absent in the rest; management has started to say "profitable growth, not just top line". For the model, assume 1 to 2 points of real price in Power over two years as legacy contracts renew (the aftermarket parts precedent of up to 4x initial pricing shows the headroom), zero in Components and EMS, and pass-through everywhere.

C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions: sensible, disciplined, integrated

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: the company is deliberately not stepping up M&A, and given its record that restraint is the disciplined choice, but it leaves TT as the only name in its peer set without an inorganic growth leg. The record is poor: Stadium (2018) led to a £32.5m write-down and the disposal of Hartlepool and Dongguan; Torotel and Ferranti added integration complexity without sustained margin gains; Volex's public critique was that acquisitions "failed to drive sustained margin expansion despite significant capital deployed". Current posture: management is "beginning to build and assess a selective pipeline of potential medium-term opportunities to strengthen core businesses", net debt reduction takes priority, and the sell-side notes leverage restricted bolt-ons. Meanwhile discoverIE deployed £95m at 9x EBIT, Volex plans $300m of acquisitions toward $2bn, Volex and Cicor have both tried to buy TT, and Cicor recovered 84% of its Albert purchase price within nine months, which says more about what TT sold than what Cicor bought. For the model: zero acquisition spend through FY2027, and an option of small bolt-ons from FY2028 funded by Components proceeds; TT is far more likely to be acquired than to acquire.

C-19

Use of the balance sheet, shareholder returns, or mooting either

C52

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement: the balance sheet is being repaired rather than used, returns are suspended, and management is openly mooting their reinstatement, which makes the dividend a dated catalyst rather than a present support. Net debt ex leases fell from £126.2m (FY2023) to £80.1m to £50.3m (FY2025), covenant leverage from 1.8x to 1.1x, the RCF was cut to £105m and extended to June 2028 with only £8.3m drawn, and £75m of 3.65% private-placement notes run to 2028 and 2031. The policy is leverage below 1.5x, organic investment first, dividend "under review" with an update promised in March 2027; Jefferies models dividends from FY2027 and consensus carries 2.5p for FY2027 and 4.3p by FY2029; Jefferies projects net debt of £43m at FY2026, £18m at FY2027 and net cash of £8m by FY2028 before any Components proceeds. No buyback has been mooted and executive ownership is too thin for one to be a personal priority. The honest reading is that the balance sheet has headroom (covenant 3.0x) that management chooses not to use, the pay-off from that choice is a lower interest line (£8.8m in FY2025 to about £5.4m annualised in H1 2026) and the removal of the going-concern uncertainty, and that the first use of the balance sheet (a dividend of 2p to 3p, about £4m to £5m a year) is small relative to the £347m market capitalisation.

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible

C56

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement: four further positive forces are present or plausible and two of them are under-modelled. (1) Tax: £282.4m of gross unrecognised tax losses (£225.1m in the US) mean cash tax is low while the adjusted tax rate is 36% to 39%; as US profits recover (Cleveland profitable, Plano gone), recognition of US deferred tax assets would drop the adjusted rate toward the 25% underlying rate and add roughly 15% to adjusted EPS at a stroke, and even before recognition the gap between adjusted EPS and cash earnings flatters free cash flow relative to the P&L. (2) The Kuantan ramp: the £10m of revenue lost to safety-stock building returns in H2 2026 at an EMS site now carrying fixed cost for it, so its incremental margin should be well above the 7.9% divisional average. (3) Incentives: the LTIP requires 12% constant-currency EPS compound growth and 95% cash conversion for full vesting and the STIP is 25% weighted to free cash flow, so management is paid for exactly the two lines the market doubts. (4) Sell-side coverage is rebuilding (four brokers from two) and the compiled consensus mechanism makes beats visible. Plausible but unproven: a return of the group to its FY2019 EBITDA margin of 12% (FactSet basis) would put consensus FY2029 within reach a year early.

C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible

❌ D42

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement: six negative developments are live, and the first three are the ones a model should carry as explicit risks. (1) Cash: H1 2026 conversion fell to 42% and free cash flow was -£0.4m on a £12.7m working-capital build; the full-year 70% to 80% target requires an H2 inflow of roughly £15m to £20m, and the risks family's warning that a return to under 80% conversion is a re-pre-mortem trigger means the March 2027 cash number is the single most important print ahead. (2) Power: the profit engine's margin fell 120bp to 14.2% on customer-driven delays, and the division's revenue was flat; management expects H2 phasing benefits, but a second half of Power margin erosion would undermine the mix argument that underpins the 12% ambition. (3) Kuantan has not reached full-rate production and remains "a key execution task" with customer-delivery and margin risk. (4) Leadership integration: a CEO, CFO and Chair all new within fourteen months, working together for the first time. (5) DBAY at 29% can block any future transaction and has shown it will. (6) Translation: the FY2026 tax rate stays mid-to-high 30s%, and if US performance disappoints the £225m of US losses stay trapped permanently. Plausible negatives beyond these: a Components sale that fails or is struck cheaply, a relapse at Cleveland (the site's productivity flattening below target is the named leading indicator), and a renewed restatement from decentralised controls that Deloitte still does not rely on.

C-87

Controlling actors indicating scope for change in strategy or approach

C50

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement: DBAY Advisors (29.04%, an Isle of Man activist private-equity firm) has indicated clearly that it sees scope for more value than a 150p sale delivered, but has not indicated what strategic change it wants, and that ambiguity is itself a factor. DBAY made three unsolicited proposals up to 130p that the board rejected as having "a different agenda", then voted against Cicor's 150p recommended offer and withdrew its own rival interest ahead of the Takeover Panel deadline; it has since increased from 24.5% to 29.04% (TR-1, 5 August 2026), just below the 30% mandatory-bid line. The new Chair worked with DBAY in the past and was cleared as independent. What this implies: the blockholder is aligned with the self-help plan (it blocked a sale and has bought more while the plan delivered), it can block any special resolution, and at 29% it retains the option of a bid itself or of selling to a bidder at a higher price. Other holders (Artemis 5.21%, Steel Partners 3.01%) are passive. For the Consumer: there is no activist push for a different strategy, but there is a shareholder able to decide the company's end state, and that compresses the range of outcomes at the top (a sale above 186p needs DBAY's assent) and at the bottom (DBAY's own interest sets an informal floor well below the current price, around the 130p it last offered).

C-21

Taken together: strength, breadth and resourcing of positive corporate change

C66

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: the positive corporate change under way is strong, unusually broad for a company of this size, and adequately resourced for its margin and cash objectives, but it is a repair programme rather than a growth programme, and most of its measurable pay-off lands in FY2026 and FY2027. Breadth: new CEO, CFO, Chair and SID; a new divisional structure; two site closures and one transfer completed; a factory turnaround delivered; a cost programme substantially complete; a disposal in process; incentives re-cut toward profit and cash; guidance raised for the first time in three years; leverage halved. Strength: the changes have already moved the margin from 5.8% to 8.1% half-on-half and the North American drag from a £2.7m regional loss to profit, so the first £5m of the improvement is banked. Resourcing: the programme is self-funded from cash flow, which is a strength for the balance sheet and a weakness for the growth engine (capex and R&D are at the bottom of the peer set). The sequencing the Consumer should model: FY2026 benefits from Plano removal, Cleveland profitability and £3m of savings; FY2027 adds the second £3m of savings, Kuantan at full rate, Power phasing recovery and a lower interest line; FY2028 adds the Components disposal mix effect and possibly the tax asset; growth beyond the order book is the piece that corporate change does not supply. Against the eight corporate-change markers, seven are present and dated and only the ones that need growth (M&A, new markets) are absent.

Industry and Value Chain

C5216 questions
C-2438C-2540C-2656C-2738C-2850C-2952C-3150C-8458C-3262C-3358C-3447C-3555C-3660C-3760C-3855C-3942

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The value chain gives TT a protected place but not a privileged one. The protection is qualification: design-in lifecycles of 15 to 30 years, 18-to-24-month requalification, site accreditations and now rising sovereign-supply rules keep customers in place and make substitution and entry slow in the 70% to 80% of the business that is custom and regulated. The lack of privilege is scale and position: TT is sub-scale in a consolidating industry, sits in the thin middle of an hourglass profit distribution where fragmentation denies pricing discipline, is prey rather than predator in the consolidation, and is losing relative ground each year to Volex, discoverIE and Cicor, which compound from higher margins with growth legs TT lacks. Demand is turning in TT's favour on three fronts (defence structurally, restocking cyclically, semi capital equipment narrowly), and the order book already covers consensus FY2027 revenue, but conversion timing is in customers' and procurement agencies' hands and suppliers are tightening. The precedents say the outperformance available here is the second step of a clean-up re-rating (clean margins and cash) and, at the end, a sale, not a compounding path; the underperformance precedents (profit warnings on execution, impairments on North American acquisitions) are TT's own history. For the model: hold Power's margin in the mid-teens on structure, cap EMS in the high single digits, treat Components as a disposal, model organic growth below the market's 5% to 7% (4% is the right central case), and carry working capital as a use of cash and the component-supply chain as an unquantified delivery risk. The industry is supportive enough for the margin repair to hold and not supportive enough to supply growth beyond the order book.

C-24

Market share rank and distance from number one or two

❌ D38

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: TT holds no number-one or number-two position in any market it reports, and the distance to the leaders is large in every one of its three divisions; the business is a collection of defensible niche positions inside markets led by others, which is why it earns its returns on qualification rather than on share. Power (military and aerospace power conversion, about £206m): a "strong tier-two position" behind engineering-led specialists such as XP Power (5% share of a $6.4bn precision power market), Advanced Energy and MKS, and behind the primes' own captive capacity; TT's share of its addressable power market is in the low single digits. EMS (about £196m): a mid-tier high-mix low-volume contract manufacturer in a global market of over 15,000 providers and a European market of over 2,000, where Cicor's purchase of TT's former European sites made Cicor number four in Europe with 6% share and left TT smaller; Volex is at $1.24bn and Kitron, Scanfil and Cicor all larger than TT's EMS division. Components (about £79m): a sub-scale participant in commoditised passives against Vishay, Bourns and TE Connectivity, routinely awarded sub-20% second-source allocations. The implication for the model is that TT cannot set price or lead consolidation, so its margin ceiling is set by its qualification barriers, not by share; the counterweight is that in its specific design-in positions (Boxer, CV-90, Challenger 3, Rolls-Royce, the Edwards Vacuum relationship of over 15 years, Abbott and Medtronic micro-coils) it is effectively the sole source for the life of the platform.

C-25

Market concentration and the steepness of the share distribution

❌ D40

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement: the markets TT competes in are fragmented at the level TT operates and concentrated at the levels above and below it, which is the worst combination for pricing discipline and the best for the availability of niches. In EMS the top five automation and robotics providers hold 28% of global share and the European EMS landscape has over 2,000 providers with tier-three players unable to hold price; the top five therefore hold under 30% and the distribution below the mega-scale groups (Foxconn, Flex, Jabil, under 4% margins) is flat rather than steep. In connectors and precision power the opposite holds: the top ten connector firms' share has risen from 38% to 61% (TE Connectivity 17.8%, Amphenol 8.8%), and upstream bare PCB, OSAT and semiconductors are concentrated enough to capture 35% to 40% gross margins and to squeeze mid-stream assembly during input inflation (the "hourglass" chain shape). The consequence for TT is structural: it sits in the thin middle of the hourglass where fragmentation keeps industry prices soft, and its profit depends on carving out designed-in positions where the relevant market is a single platform and concentration is total. The Consumer should not expect industry-wide price discipline to help margins; mix toward designed-in work is the only route.

C-26

Stability of market shares and share taken by new entrants

C56

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: shares are highly stable within TT's designed-in programmes and unstable in its catalogue and commodity work, and new entrants have taken meaningful share only in the latter over the last three to five years. Stability evidence: historical customer churn fell from 30% to about 10% as engineering complexity rose; all top-ten customer relationships exceed ten years and several exceed twenty; Ultra PCS is a 35-year relationship; management confirmed zero major customer losses through the 2024 North American crisis, with customer-side effects limited to schedule deferrals; requalification takes 18 to 24 months and a single mould requalification costs over $20,000. Instability evidence: distribution sales fell 27% organically in 2024 and a further 17% in H1 2025 as distributors liquidated stock and shifted allocations; Chinese and subsidised Indian EMS capacity (Syrma SGS, Kaynes) is taking commodity assembly share in Asia; second-tier suppliers (Volex, Luxshare) win secondary-source status by pricing 5% to 8% below first-tier players. The honest reading for the model is that TT's revenue decline from £617m to £481m was overwhelmingly disposals, destocking and self-inflicted site exits, not share loss in its core; the share TT has lost to entrants is in the part of the business it is now trying to sell.

C-27

Consolidation toward the largest and most advantaged, and whether the company is among them

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement: the industry is consolidating toward scale and toward the most advantaged operators, and TT is on the wrong side of both vectors: it is a consolidation target, not a consolidator. The evidence is explicit: Cicor is rolling up European EMS (number four with 6% share after buying TT's sites, and it tried to buy all of TT); Volex has done twelve acquisitions at 5.2x EBITDA and tried to buy TT; discoverIE deployed £95m in six months at 9x EBIT; Halma invested a record £515m across six deals; scale is "the defining competitive differentiator in securing critical component allocations". The "most advantaged" are those with low-cost flexible footprints (Volex's Türkiye, Batam and Mexico hubs; XP Power's Vietnam at 10% of US labour cost) and those with proprietary design-in IP at 40% gross margins (discoverIE). TT has neither the scale (sub-£500m, revenue per site in the bottom quartile) nor a low-cost manufacturing advantage (its Malaysian and Mexican sites are tariff and nearshoring responses, not cost leaders), and its former Chair said in the Cicor offer document that "insufficient scale has structurally restricted" the company. The only consolidation vector TT is on the right side of is qualification: in defence power the market consolidates toward whoever is already qualified on the platform, and there TT is among the advantaged. The modelling consequence is that TT's standalone growth should be assumed below the industry's (its 4% to 6% target against a 5.6% to 6.8% market), and that the end state of the equity is more likely a sale than a compounding path.

C-28

Competitors' attitude to competition

C50

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: competitors in TT's qualified niches put margin first and are benign; competitors at its commodity edge put volume first and are willing to price for share; and in neither case do industry prices fall with volume in the way they do in commodity manufacturing, because contracts are fixed-price or indexed. Margin-first evidence: XP Power exited RF power and closed its China plant to protect returns; discoverIE prices to technical value and "rarely trades on price"; tier-two specialists avoid volume-led price wars; the mega-scale EMS groups do not bid for low-volume regulated work at all. Volume-first evidence: Volex "bids aggressively" and wins without long contracts, then recovers margin over eighteen months; Chinese appliance-cable makers are pricing aggressively into Europe; Asian EMS capacity is expanding 40%+ on subsidies; distributors withhold design-in pricing from contract manufacturers. On the price-volume relationship: through the 2024 to 2025 destocking, TT's own experience was volume loss with margin held (Europe's margin rose 240bp to 15.3% while Asia's fell 180bp on under-absorption), which says that the industry's volume downturn transmitted to profits through fixed-cost absorption, not through price cuts. For the model: price is not the swing variable in a downturn; utilisation is.

C-29

Stability of that attitude over eighteen months to three years; "Michael O'Leary" instability and shark-attack risk

C52

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: competitor attitudes have been stable in TT's core and have become more aggressive only at the corporate level, where the shark attack has already happened and taken the form of takeover approaches rather than price. Over the last three years no named competitor has launched a pricing attack on TT's designed-in programmes; the attacks were Volex's two proposals at 129p to 139.6p (late 2024) with a public campaign on TT's "65% share price erosion", and Cicor's recommended 150p offer, both exploiting operational weakness rather than pricing weakness. Volex is the one competitor with O'Leary characteristics (reactive, opportunistic, willing to bid low and renegotiate later, walked away rather than overpay), and it remains the most plausible attacker if TT stumbles again; Cicor is a disciplined acquirer; XP Power and discoverIE are margin-led. What would provoke a pricing shark attack is a visible loss of qualification or a delivery failure at a key programme, because that is when a second source can be awarded; the 2024 Cleveland scrap and yield crisis was exactly such a window and no customer was lost through it, which is reassuring on the depth of the design-in. Attitude stability is therefore high for the next 18 months with the caveat that corporate, not commercial, aggression is the form competition takes toward TT.

C-31

Taken together: how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support

C50

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: the industry structure is moderately supportive in Power, neutral in EMS and unsupportive in Components, and the support is fragile in the specific sense that it rests on qualification barriers that only hold while the company executes. The supportive elements are durable: regulated high-reliability end markets growing 4% to 6% through the cycle (the European high-mix low-volume subsegment at 6.8% a year), outsourcing rates in Europe (40%) below the global 50%, design-in lifecycles of 15 to 30 years, and tier-one primes with 95% of engineering tied up in sustaining work who will not insource. The unsupportive elements are structural: fragmentation that denies pricing discipline, an hourglass profit distribution that squeezes the middle, scale advantages in procurement that TT lacks, and a consolidation dynamic in which TT is prey. The fragility is that the support is conditional on operational standards: when Cleveland and Kansas City failed in 2024, the structure did not protect margins (North America fell to a loss) even though it protected customer relationships. The Consumer should model structure as holding the Power margin in the mid-teens, capping EMS in the high single digits and leaving Components at the mercy of the distribution cycle; the group margin is then a mix and execution question, not a structure question.

C-84

Historical precedents, rules of thumb and empirical evidence on outperformance in this sector and industry, and the company's fit

C58

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: the sector's own rules say that share-price outperformance here comes from four sources, and TT fits two of them well, one partially and one not at all. (1) The clean-up re-rating "in two steps, at the event and at the first clean margins", often ending in a sale to private or strategic capital: TT has completed step one (the H1 2026 beat, upgrade and 60% target-price increase lifted the shares 95% in twelve months) and step two (a year of clean adjusted-to-statutory reconciliation and cash conversion above 90%) is still ahead; Volex is the sector's canonical precedent (margins 2.8% to 10.2% over nine years, shares up many-fold), XP Power's margin recovery from 4.3% to 7.9% in a year is the nearer one, and the Senior and Goodwin cases show the end state is often a transaction. (2) The restocking pulse of six to eighteen months: TT fits (book-to-bill 1.12x, Components back in growth), but the digest's warning is that restocking does not produce design wins, and discoverIE at 18.5x shows the quality names have already priced it while TT at 10x has not. (3) Qualified defence components keeping margins while shares follow award timing: TT fits, with the caveat that reported defence revenue is lumpy and a half year can disappoint while the backlog is intact (Solid State's swing from £163m to £125m to £154m). (4) Being on the right machine (data centre, copper and optics) and relabelling: TT does not fit, has no AI exposure, and will not earn the multiple that Volex earns from it. The empirical finding most relevant to the Consumer is the Industry note of 17 August 2026: through the destocking downturn, reported cash and margins at several component makers were flattered by inventory release and deferred spending, the strongest-looking cash conversion came with falling volume, and the reverse is expected as volumes return. TT is the named example (150% conversion on -2.7% organic growth) and is now living the reverse (42% conversion as volume rebuilds); the model must carry working capital as a use of cash through the recovery, as at Volex and AQ Group. Underperformance precedents fit TT's own history exactly: profit warnings on site execution (late 2023, April 2024, November 2025), goodwill impairments on North American acquisitions (£93.6m over two years), and consensus EPS cut 57% in a year.

C-32

Customer demand: support for the thesis and fragility

C62

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis in Power strongly, in Components modestly and in EMS not yet, and the fragility is concentrated in the timing of conversion rather than in the existence of demand. Support: the order book is £550m (+20%) with a 1.12x group book-to-bill and 1.14x in Power; European defence book-to-bill was 135% in 2025; the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement and the MBDA letter of intent are post-period; the Cleveland site has capacity to take more volume; Components returned to 5.8% growth on stronger distributor markets; healthcare is stabilising and wafer-fabrication orders are growing in Asia. Fragility: the EMS division's revenue fell 8.3% in H1 2026 and its "underlying trading continues to be impacted by macroeconomic uncertainty and customer caution"; Power's revenue was flat because customer contract finalisations slipped; the risks family's forensic point is that book-to-bill can be inflated by deferrals (£10m of Cleveland revenue was deferred from 2025 into 2026) and that the primary early-warning signal is the gap between twelve-month order cover and realised ninety-day revenue; the Asian pro forma book-to-bill was 0.91x in FY2025; order visibility in components is twelve weeks. The strongest statement I can make is that demand exists to deliver consensus FY2027 revenue of £503m from the order book alone, and the weakest is that whether it arrives in FY2026 or FY2027 is in the customers' hands, not TT's.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and fragility

C58

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: the route to market helps and is robust for 83% of revenue, and hinders and is fragile for the remaining 17%. Direct sales to tier-one OEMs are 83% of group revenue, involve co-engineered solutions with 10-to-30-year lifecycles, and make disintermediation implausible; this is the channel through which the order book and the design-in moat operate. The distribution channel (17% of sales, down from 20% to 27% historically; 35.8% of North American revenue against 2.1% in Asia) is the source of the group's short-cycle volatility: it fell 27% organically in 2024, 17% in H1 2025 and 4.7% across FY2025, distributors hold inventory seven days above the ten-year median, and expert evidence says distributors "frequently withhold design-in pricing discounts from ECM providers" and squeeze payment terms. The Components disposal would remove most of the distribution exposure and therefore most of the channel fragility; until then the channel is the part of the business that will reflect the restocking pulse first and fastest, up in H2 2026 and FY2027, and down when the pulse ends. Downstream, OEM payment terms are expected to extend, which is a modest working-capital hindrance the model should carry.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: help or hurt, and the fragility of the company's position

❌ D47

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement: existing competitors are hurting TT at the margins through investment and expansion rather than through price, and TT's position is fragile where it is sub-scale and secure where it is qualified. Volex is expanding capacity (five Centres of Excellence, doubled Tijuana, new San Luis Potosí), has raised its margin to 10.2% and its targets to $2bn and 12%, and wins aggressively; discoverIE has added capacity in India and Thailand and three defence-weighted acquisitions; XP Power has moved to Vietnam, cut its margin drag and launched wide-bandgap products aimed at TT's specialist applications; Cicor is consolidating Europe and has stated TT lacks scale; Kitron and Scanfil are growing with European rearmament. None of this has shown up as lost programmes at TT, and TT's H1 2026 margin expansion of 230bp exceeded every one of these peers' moves in the period. The hurt is relative: these competitors are compounding from higher margins (Volex 10.2%, discoverIE 13.8%) with growth legs TT lacks, so the gap in scale and returns widens each year TT spends repairing itself, and the sector's "whose machine is in demand" rule means their multiples (14.5x to 18.5x) stay above TT's (10x). The fragile element is the scale disadvantage in component allocations as supply tightens again.

C-35

New entrants: adjacent, standard and orthogonal, and the threat of each

C55

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: standard entrants are the only material threat and only to the commodity third of the business; adjacent and orthogonal entrants are threats to the industry but not to TT's niches within the horizon. Standard entrants: subsidised Indian EMS (Production Linked Incentive expansion, Electronic Component Manufacturing Scheme), Chinese appliance and cable makers pricing into Europe, and Asian EMS output growing 40%+; these take commodity assembly and catalogue component volume and are the reason Components and parts of Asian EMS are structurally price-taking. Adjacent entrants: hyperscale EMS groups moving up into thermal and power specialisation for data centres (Flex, Luxshare, Delta), which is not where TT plays; semiconductor and software firms dealing directly with OEMs in software-defined architectures (automotive, not TT's markets); XP Power into TT's power niches with SiC/GaN products, the one adjacent entry that matters. Orthogonal entrants: AI design automation and configurators that could commoditise application engineering support in distribution (relevant to Solid State's model more than TT's). The barriers protecting TT's core are specific and dated: 18-to-24-month qualification, AS9100D and FDA site accreditations, ITAR compliance in Kansas, 20-year production guarantees in aerospace, and critical-minerals and NIST compliance that are now rising, which raises the entry bar further for anyone not already in the US defence chain.

C-36

Substitutes and alternatives, and how that is changing

C60

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: substitution is a low and stable threat to TT's designed-in products and a moderate, rising threat to its standard components, and the most important substitute (customer insourcing) is receding. Insourcing by primes is rated unlikely over five to ten years because 95% of OEM engineering is sustaining work and specialised high-mix manufacturing is capital-intensive; the pre-integrated subsystem trend is a substitution in TT's favour (customers prefer bought-in assemblies because integration costs exceed the price); standardisation of power modules across next-generation aircraft is the named downside that would commoditise TT's custom assemblies. In Components, centralised compute, integrated SoCs and alternative sensor roadmaps (FMCW LiDAR, co-packaged optics) substitute discrete parts over time, which is another reason to sell the division. Pass-through contracts and the cost of requalification mean that even a cheaper substitute rarely displaces an incumbent part mid-platform. The change over the last eighteen months is positive: nearshoring and sovereign-supply mandates have made TT's regional footprint a reason to be chosen, not a cost to be substituted.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: helping or hurting, and fragility

C60

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement: regulators and political actors are net helpful to TT's demand and net costly to its operations, and the helpful part is less fragile than the costly part is predictable. Helpful: NATO and European rearmament budgets flow to platforms TT is designed into; sovereign-supply and critical-minerals rules (EO 14415) reward incumbents with transparent chains and raise barriers for entrants; CHIPS Act re-shoring drives Kuantan and US demand; tariffs are contractually passed through. Costly: EO 14415's indentured bill-of-materials requirement within 180 days and the 1 January 2027 waiver cessation, NIST SP 800-171 scoring, carbon and plastics reporting, UK employer tax rises, and the administrative overhead of multiple customs regimes that the H1 2026 report flags could offset the £3m cost programme. Political actors specific to the equity: the UK NSI Act review applies to any foreign acquirer of TT's defence assets (it applied to Cicor), and UK fiscal uncertainty under the new government is a sterling risk. The fragility is in timing: US stopgap budgets have delayed over 150 procurement programmes and a slipped UK Defence Investment Plan has delayed British awards, so the regulatory tailwind arrives in lumps.

C-38

On whom disruption forces act, how, and how fragile they leave the company

C55

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: disruption forces act on four actors in TT's chain, and the net effect leaves TT's position moderately robust because the forces that act on its customers favour it while the forces that act on its competitors and suppliers are neutral to mildly adverse. On customers (demand): electrification of aircraft and vehicles, autonomy and edge computing raise power density and reliability requirements, which pushes OEMs toward pre-integrated, qualified power subsystems and toward wide-bandgap modules, exactly TT's roadmap (SiC, silver sintering, AX-FORCE, Altitude DC); laboratory automation and robotic surgery drive advanced PCBA and micro-coil sensor demand. On competitors: wide-bandgap products from XP Power and others raise the technology bar in specialist applications, and automated lights-out factories in Asia raise the capital bar in volume assembly. On regulators and political actors: sovereign-supply and critical-minerals mandates are reshaping sourcing in TT's favour. On suppliers: AI demand is absorbing memory and mature-node capacity, extending lead times and raising input costs for everyone downstream. The fragility is a reinvestment question: TT spends 3.8% of sales on R&D against XP Power's 12%, so it can keep its design-in positions but is unlikely to lead the next technology transition; if aircraft programmes standardise power modules or certification of high-voltage platforms slips past 2028, the roadmap earns nothing in the forecast period.

C-39

Suppliers: support or source of risk

❌ D42

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement: suppliers are a modest and rising source of risk, not a support. The evidence: memory contract prices rising sharply, European PCB capacity booked into 2028, lead times extending across semiconductors and electromechanical parts, bespoke connectors constrained by raw-material shortages, limited alternatives for certain proprietary technologies, days payable flat-to-shrinking (little leverage over foundries), and a documented history of stock-outs. TT's mitigants are process-based (a Procurement Code of Conduct, digital supplier risk rating, monthly regional procurement reviews, a Supply Chain Council, targeted safety stock for critical inputs) and contractual (80% to 90% pass-through, tariff co-terms). The structural disadvantage is scale: in allocation, larger EMS groups are served first and smaller ones rely on sourcing partners. For the model, two consequences: inventory will not fall back to the FY2025 trough (net working capital 12.6% of revenue) because safety stock is now policy, so free cash flow conversion should be modelled at the 70% to 80% guided level rather than at 90%+ until lead times normalise; and the risk of a delivery miss on a defence programme through a single component shortage is real but unquantifiable.
