Company - Competitive advantage

C507 questions
C-4158C-4235C-4362C-4455C-4552C-4630C-4750

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

TT's competitive advantage is narrow, real and conditional. It is narrow because it exists only where the company is designed in and qualified (Power, regulated EMS), which is roughly 70% to 80% of sales, and is absent in Components and standard assembly. It is real because it is measured in behaviour that cannot be faked: churn of about 10%, relationships of ten to thirty-five years, no customer losses through a factory crisis, continued design wins from Rolls-Royce, MBDA and Collins, and requalification barriers of 18 to 24 months. It is conditional because the same advantage did not protect margins in 2024: the moat holds the customer while the company earns whatever its execution allows, and the gap between what TT's assets earned under TT and what the disposed sites earned under Cicor is evidence that the assets have been under-exploited rather than that the moat is weak. Scale is the structural deficit, pricing power is of the cost-recovery kind with margin pricing only beginning, and the market is large enough that growth is a question of winning rather than of room. For the model: the competitive position supports a group margin in the 9% to 10% range once self-help completes and the mix shifts, supports organic growth near 4%, and supports neither a premium multiple nor an acceleration beyond the market's growth. The direction of travel is positive (regulatory barriers rising, Components leaving the perimeter, Power widening its technical edge), and the Consumer should treat the moat as a floor under revenue and relationships, not as a source of margin.

C-41

Sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats: which, how strong, widening or narrowing

C58

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: TT holds real moats of two kinds and lacks the other three, and the moats it holds are widening in Power and narrowing in Components. Customer-related (strong, stable): co-engineered design-in at platform definition that makes TT the specified part for 15 to 30 years; measured by customer churn falling from 30% to about 10% as complexity rose, all top-ten relationships over ten years, several over twenty, Ultra PCS at 35 years, no customer above 10% of sales, and zero major customer losses through the 2024 crisis. Regulatory or political (strong, widening): site-level AS9100D, FDA, ISO 13485 and ITAR accreditations, 18-to-24-month requalification, 20-year aerospace production guarantees, and now sovereign-supply and critical-minerals rules that reward incumbents with transparent chains; widening because rearmament and US compliance mandates raise the bar for anyone not already qualified. Production-related (moderate, widening in Power): proprietary test engineering, silver-sintering and silicon-carbide module capability at Bedlington, ultra-fine-wire micro-coil winding for surgical navigation, and the dual position as both component maker and assembler that an industry expert called "unique" and "difficult for pure-play EMS peers to replicate"; narrowing in Components, where products are commoditised passives. Scale-based: none; TT is sub-scale and the business-foundations work states that advantage-widening "relies on disciplined execution rather than mechanical network effects". Culture or knowledge: engineering depth exists (NPI centres produce prototypes in two weeks) but was damaged by 500 redundancies and the Cleveland control failure and is being rebuilt. Net: the moats protect share and relationships, not revenue timing or margin, which is exactly what the 2024 to 2025 record showed (customers stayed, profits fell).

C-42

Scale relative to competitors, and what it confers

❌ D35

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: TT is sub-scale against every listed peer that matters and its scale confers nothing beyond the minimum needed to hold a global footprint; this is the most persistent structural weakness in the file and the stated reason the former Chair recommended a sale. Revenue of £481m (FY2025) compares with Volex at $1.24bn with 23 sites in 25 countries, discoverIE at £443m but at a 13.8% margin on a design-only model with 41 sites, Cicor growing 19% to 28% a year by acquisition, and Solid State at £154m. The business-foundations work puts minimum viable scale for a global footprint at £250m of revenue, which TT exceeds, but its revenue per site is low (eight UK manufacturing facilities alone), its purchasing power is weak (smaller EMS groups "rely on sourcing partners" for allocations), and its capex of £8m and R&D of 3.8% of sales cannot fund technology leadership against XP Power at 12% of sales. Headcount is not disclosed in the file beyond the 500-plus redundancies. What scale confers negatively is important for the model: fixed overhead per site is high relative to volume, which is why under-absorption drove North America to a loss in 2024 and why the same sites now show sharp incremental margins as volume returns; the Components sale and the Suzhou refocus reduce the site count without adding scale to what remains.

C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and mid-term growth allowed

C62

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement: the addressable market is many times larger than the company and growing at or above the company's own target, so market size is not a constraint on the forecast and the growth rate the model should use is the lower of the market's and the company's ability to win. Figures: the high-reliability high-mix low-volume subsegment is $21.4bn (40% of European EMS) growing 6.8% a year to 2030; global EMS grows 5.6% a year from $648.5bn to $853.1bn; the specialised high-mix PCB market is $25bn to $35bn; precision power control is $6.4bn (XP Power's definition); the European outsourcing rate of 40% against 50% globally is a secular conversion tailwind; the through-cycle growth of TT's end markets (defence, medical automation, industrial electrification) is 4% to 6%, which is management's own target. TT at under £500m holds low-single-digit shares of each. The constraint is therefore not the market but the company: it targets 4% to 6%, its peers grow 5% to 14%, and its three-year record is -11% a year. For the model I would allow 4% to 5% organic growth over the mid term, with upside to 6% if the Power order book converts on time, and would not model share gain beyond that.

C-44

Surplus customer utility against competing offerings: leading, or demonstrably not inferior

C55

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement: TT's offering is demonstrably not inferior in Power and in regulated EMS, is unproven in standard EMS, and is inferior in Components. The disproof of inferiority in the core rests on behaviour, not claims: customers kept TT through the 2024 scrap, yield and delivery crisis (schedule deferrals, no losses); new design wins continued through the crisis (Cleveland's three new customers and six products in H2 2025 after a three-year drought; the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement; the MBDA letter of intent; Collins Aerospace's $10.3m classified programme; the five-year European land-platform HMI award; the £8m six-degree-of-freedom sensor contract; Edwards Vacuum at over 15 years); expert testimony from a former competitor called TT "unique" in combining engineering, development and contract manufacturing; and the group's own 2024 problems were internal ("yield, the cost of poor quality, and inventory management"), not product rejection. The counter-evidence that should temper the reading: Cicor recovered 84% of what it paid for TT's three European sites within nine months and doubled margins at acquired sites, which says those assets were run below their potential under TT; Volex wins work from TT's markets with aggressive bidding; and Volex's own 65%-share-price-erosion critique was accepted by enough shareholders to make the Cicor bid viable. In Components, multi-sourcing with sub-20% allocations and distributor behaviour say the product is a price-taker. The honest summary is "not inferior where it is designed in, and not differentiated where it is not".

C-45

Pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility, shown by application

C52

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement: TT has demonstrated pricing power of the cost-recovery kind and has begun to demonstrate pricing power of the margin kind, but only in Europe and Power, and the evidence of application is thinner than the evidence at the best peers. Applied: £40m of input inflation recovered through contractual adjustments and surcharges over the 2021 to 2023 cycle; tariffs passed through under customer co-terms (Malaysian duty at 24% borne by the customer); legacy underperforming European contracts renegotiated, contributing to a 240bp European margin gain to 15.3% in FY2025; business turned away below margin mandates; Components' return to profit attributed partly to pricing actions; aftermarket cable-harness parts at up to 4x initial contract pricing. Limits: indexed pass-through of 80% to 90% with a three-to-six-month lag protects gross pounds and dilutes percentage; short-cycle fixed-price contracts delay recovery six to twelve months; no price/volume split is disclosed; hyperscale and industrial buyers award secondary suppliers on price; and Volex's model of bidding low and raising at renewal shows the market's tolerance for the opposite behaviour. Compared with discoverIE (customers accept 3% to 5% annual increases because its parts are 3% to 5% of system cost), TT's pricing evidence is weaker because its box-build and EMS content is a larger share of the customer's cost. For the model: real price of 1 to 2 points a year in Power as contracts renew, zero elsewhere, with pass-through protecting gross profit in pounds.

C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers

❌ D30

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement: TT does not share scale economies with customers, has no scale economies to share, and does not position itself that way; its value proposition is engineering, qualification and reliability at a price that recovers cost. Nothing in the strategy, messaging or culture references passing efficiency gains to customers; the sales transformation runs in the opposite direction (pricing discipline, turning away low-margin work); and the contractual architecture (indexation, tariff co-terms) is designed to pass costs up, not savings down. This is appropriate for a sub-scale specialist and not a criticism, but it means the business cannot build the customer-growth flywheel that scale-sharers (Volex's low-cost hubs, the Walmart-style model) use, and it confirms that growth must come from design wins one programme at a time.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces improving competitive advantage and financial outputs each period, versus heroic dynamism

C50

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement: roughly half of the forecast improvement in TT's financial outputs over the next three years is mechanical and would occur with ordinary execution, and half is heroic and depends on management doing difficult things well; this is a better ratio than TT's own history but a worse one than its compounding peers. Mechanical forces: (1) mix, because Aerospace and Defence grows at double digits with a mid-teens margin while the rest grows slowly at single-digit margins, which lifts the group margin by roughly 30bp to 40bp a year without any action; (2) the order book, which is an annuity of designed-in programmes converting on platform schedules; (3) the cost programme annualising from £3m to over £6m in FY2027; (4) the interest line falling as debt is repaid; (5) the lapping of Plano and Suzhou removals in H2 2026; (6) the potential recognition of US deferred tax assets as US profits recur, which is an accounting consequence of profitability rather than an action. Heroic elements: Cleveland staying profitable and winning volume, Kuantan reaching full rate without customer disruption, the sales transformation converting pipeline, selling Components at value, holding Power's margin while customers defer, and integrating a new executive team. Peer comparison: discoverIE's improvement is mostly mechanical (acquired businesses maturing, mix to defence), Volex's is a mix of structural demand and execution, Solid State's is almost entirely contract timing; TT sits between discoverIE and Solid State. The modelling consequence is that the margin path to about 9% to 9.5% is largely gravitational, and the path from there to 12% is heroic.

Company - Economic strength

C5011 questions
C-4960C-5042C-5150C-5246C-5356C-5435C-7550C-7664C-7748C-7862C-7942

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

TT's economic strength is that of a repairable business, not a strong one: margins, returns and cash are all recovering from a trough and all remain below the peer range, the earnings base is unclean on three counts (adjusting items, an inflated adjusted tax rate, and non-recurring items inside adjusted profit), and the perimeter is wide. What makes the recovery modellable with confidence is that it is driven by identifiable internal items of known size (Plano removal, Cleveland profitability, £6m of savings, the interest line, Kuantan, Power phasing) that together explain the path from 7.7% to about 9.5% without needing demand; what makes it uncertain is that the final leg to 12% and the EPS leg from tax recognition depend on North American profitability recurring for several years, on a disposal being executed at value, and on volume arriving on the order book's schedule. Operating leverage is high and now works upward, financial leverage is comfortable and shrinking, and the scope for improvement beyond cost cuts is the best feature of the economics: four or five big areas, peer-proven, congruent with strategy, arithmetically sufficient to reach the target. For the model: EBIT of £40m, £45m to £47m and £50m to £53m for FY2026 to FY2028 on 0%, 4% and 4% growth is a sound central case with upside from Kuantan and Power phasing; the adjusted tax rate is the explicit assumption that most changes EPS (37% versus 27% is a 16% EPS difference) and must be stated, not inherited from consensus; free cash flow should be modelled at 70% to 80% conversion in FY2026 and about 90% thereafter, with restructuring cash, working capital at 17% of revenue and capex at £12.5m carried explicitly; and the Components disposal should be modelled as a dated scenario with a 100bp margin step and £35m to £40m of proceeds, not blended into the base.

C-49

EPS growth, revenue growth and durability

C60

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement: the company is generating very high adjusted EPS growth on near-zero revenue growth, and the growth is durable to FY2027 because its sources are internal and already in motion, but it is a recovery from a depressed base rather than compounding: consensus adjusted EPS of 11.9p (FY2026, +75% from 6.8p), 15.9p (FY2027, +34%), 17.8p (FY2028, +12%) and 19.9p (FY2029) only regains the FY2023 level of 19.0p by the fourth forecast year. Revenue grows 0.1%, 4.4%, 4.4% and 1.9% on consensus. The bridge the Consumer should carry: FY2026 EPS growth comes from the margin step (7.7% to about 8.3%: Plano removal, Cleveland profitable, £3m savings), a lower interest line (£8.8m FY2025 net interest on FactSet's basis to about £5.5m) and the lapping of H1 2025's weak base; FY2027 growth comes from the second £3m of savings, Kuantan at full rate, volume on the order book at a high incremental margin, further interest savings and, critically, an implied fall in the adjusted tax rate from the high 30s toward about 30% (consensus EPS of 15.9p on consensus pre-tax profit of £39.0m and about 180m shares implies a tax rate near 27% to 30%, which only happens if US losses start to shelter US profits); FY2028 growth is volume. Durability: the self-help sources are durable; the tax step is a forecasting assumption the Consumer must own explicitly; the volume source depends on the restocking pulse lasting into FY2027. Peer comparison: Volex grows EPS 11% to 22% on 6% to 14% revenue growth, discoverIE 6% to 11% on 4% to 11%, so TT's EPS growth is the highest and its revenue growth the lowest in the set, the signature of a margin recovery.

C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital at a high return for three to five years, and what it means for potential and the fair multiple

❌ D42

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement: the scope to deploy capital at a high incremental return is limited and mostly already identified, which caps the fair multiple at a recovery multiple rather than a compounder's. The identified high-return uses are small: capex guided up from £8m to about £12.5m (2.6% of sales) for Kuantan SMT lines and warehousing, silicon-carbide capability at Bedlington and precision-winding automation; working capital to support the order book; and the cost programme, whose £3m of restructuring returns £6m a year (a payback inside a year). The best "deployment" is actually a withdrawal: the Components disposal, which would release £35m to £40m of enterprise value from a division earning 2% to 3%, and debt repayment, which earns the 3.65% to RCF-rate cost of debt plus the removal of covenant risk. Beyond that there is no reinvestment runway: no acquisition pipeline in execution, no new market entry, R&D at 3.8% of sales, and Cleveland with spare capacity that needs orders, not capital. For the fair multiple: the sector's quality names with reinvestment runways trade at 14.5x (Volex) to 18.5x (discoverIE) while TT trades at about 10x, and the absence of a reinvestment engine is the right reason for most of that gap; the gap can close partly on clean margins and cash (step two of the clean-up re-rating) but not on growth optionality. The mid-to-long-term potential is therefore a 9% to 10% margin business growing 4% with a modest dividend, worth a low-double-digit multiple of earnings, unless it is sold.

C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital

C50

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement: returns are recovering from a trough to the middle of the peer range and are flattered by low capital intensity and by the tax losses; they are not yet high. The company reports post-tax ROIC of 13.3% in FY2025 (from 10.0% in FY2024 and 12% in FY2023) and the H1 2026 report cites 17.7%; the target is mid-to-high teens. Peers: Volex ROCE 21.0%, discoverIE ROCE 15.2% and return on tangible capital 45.1%, Oxford Instruments 18%. On a FactSet view, FY2026 consensus EBIT of £40m taxed at 25% on invested capital of roughly £250m (equity £165m plus net debt and leases of about £85m) is about 12%; on tangible capital the return is far higher because £93.6m of North American goodwill has been written off and much of the remaining goodwill relates to older acquisitions, so return on tangible capital employed is plausibly in the 25% to 35% range (hypothesis; the file does not give the tangible base). Return on new invested capital is high where capital is deployed (the cost programme, Kuantan, the Cleveland fix) because the amounts are small and the pay-offs large, which is the characteristic of a repair phase. For the model: carry ROIC rising toward 16% to 18% by FY2028 on the margin path and the Components disposal, and note that cash tax below P&L tax lifts cash returns above reported.

C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and the behaviour of working capital

❌ D46

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: cash conversion is volatile, is currently at a trough, and over the forecast period should settle well below the FY2025 peak and somewhat below net income, with working capital the swing item and restructuring cash the hidden leak. The history: operating cash conversion was 92% (FY2023), 117% (FY2024) and 150% (FY2025, on a £14.8m inventory release), then 42% in H1 2026 on a £12.7m working-capital build; FactSet free cash flow was £18.7m, £21.5m and £34.2m for FY2023 to FY2025 and -£0.4m in H1 2026; net working capital was 12.6% of trailing revenue at FY2025 (a trough) and 19.7% at H1 2026 against a historical 17% to 20%; £11.2m of the FY2025 net debt reduction was a pension surplus refund. Guidance is 70% to 80% conversion for FY2026 and 90%+ in the medium term; consensus free cash flow is £9.0m, £18.9m, £26.7m and £30.4m for FY2026 to FY2029, which is 0.6x, 0.7x, 0.9x and about 1.0x net income on the peer table. The mechanics the Consumer should model: (1) H2 2026 must release £12m to £18m of working capital to hit guidance, mostly from the deliberate inventory build unwinding as Kuantan ships and Project CAIN-style safety stock is consumed; (2) thereafter working capital at about 17% of revenue absorbs £3m to £4m a year at 4% growth; (3) restructuring cash (£7.9m in FY2025, about £3.8m in H1 2026) sits outside adjusted profit but inside free cash flow and should fall to near zero by FY2027; (4) cash tax should run below the 36% to 39% adjusted rate because £282m of losses shelter US and UK profits, which is a positive leak the P&L hides; (5) capex rises to £12.5m. Peer context: Volex's conversion fell to 63.9% as growth absorbed inventory and discoverIE's held at 92%; TT's behaviour will resemble Volex's for two years. The risks family's trigger (conversion below 80% in FY2026 from inventory rebuilding) is set at the top of management's own guided range, so by that family's test FY2026 will fail; I would set the real test at the guided 70% and at a return above 90% in FY2027.

C-53

Gross and operating margins and their trend

C56

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement: margins are rising from a trough, the rise so far is self-help and mix rather than volume, and the next leg depends on volume and the Components exit. Gross margin on FactSet's basis was 22.8% (FY2023), 20.7% (FY2024), 22.6% (FY2025) and 25.3% in H1 2026 (25.6% on the earnings-review table), the H1 2026 figure being 620bp above H1 2025's 19.0% because scrap, rework and Plano's losses left the cost of sales while pricing actions and mix arrived. Adjusted operating margin was 8.6% (FY2023), 7.1% (FY2024), 7.7% (FY2025) and 8.1% in H1 2026 (5.8% in H1 2025), with the group's own history showing a 54% second-half weighting, so FY2026 at consensus £40m is 8.3% and implies an H2 margin near 8.5%. Divisional structure: Power about 16% (FY2025 restated) and 14.2% in H1 2026; EMS about 5% (FY2025) and 7.9% in H1 2026; Components loss-making to 2.7%; Europe 15.3% against North America 0.7% on the old basis, a 1,460bp dispersion. Consensus has the EBIT margin at 8.3%, 8.9%, 9.5% and 9.9% for FY2026 to FY2029, EBITDA at 10.9% to 12.3%; management's undated target is 12%; Jefferies has 9.0% and 9.5%. Peers: Volex 10.2%, discoverIE 13.8%, Solid State 6.2%, Cicor 5.7% (H1 2026), XP Power 7.9%; TT's EBITDA margin of 10% to 12% is mid-pack. The direction is up and the two-year trajectory to about 9.5% is well supported; the FY2019 FactSet operating EBITDA margin of 12.0% shows the business has earned near the target before.

C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted versus reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage

❌ D35

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement: the earnings base is unclean and is improving, and the Consumer must model three distinct leaks between adjusted operating profit and cash earnings per share. (1) Adjusting items: £65.4m in FY2025 (138% of the £28.2m statutory operating loss; £41.4m impairments, £15.2m restructuring including £7.0m Plano and £6.1m Cleveland, £2.6m acquired-intangible amortisation, £1.6m duplicate management costs), £60.6m in FY2024, over £180m over three years, a structural outlier against Senior and Volex where such charges run 15% to 30% of operating profit; in H1 2026 adjusting items fell to £8.8m (statutory EPS 1.9p against adjusted 5.7p), comprising a £3.8m Mexicali impairment, £1.0m restructuring, about £3m of cost-programme spend and amortisation. Restructuring is cash (£7.9m in FY2025) and must be in free cash flow even though it is outside adjusted EPS. (2) Tax: the adjusted effective tax rate was 57.1% in FY2025, 36.3% in H1 2026 and is guided to the mid-to-high 30s% for FY2026, against a normalised mid-20s%, because US deferred tax assets are not recognised; this costs roughly 15% of adjusted EPS each year until recognition and is the single largest leakage; the £282.4m of gross unrecognised losses (£225.1m US) mean cash tax is lower than the P&L charge, so the leak runs the other way in cash. (3) Non-recurring profits inside adjusted figures: £3.5m of Plano last-time-buy profit in H2 2025 (not repeating in 2026), and the £11.2m pension refund in the FY2025 net-debt move. Capitalised development is small (£1.1m additions). Governance history (the £5.7m 2023 restatement, Deloitte's zero reliance on controls) argues for a discount on reported precision until two clean years have passed. The base is cleaner each half and the FY2026 statutory-to-adjusted gap should be the smallest since 2022; the test the sector digest sets ("the first clean margins") is the FY2026 results in March 2027.

C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much a change in revenue moves operating profit, and the range of outcomes

C50

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement: operating leverage is high and symmetrical, is the dominant driver of the earnings range, and is currently working in TT's favour for the first time since 2022. The structure: gross margin of 22% to 25% means variable costs (mostly materials) are about 75% of revenue, factory overhead and selling and administrative costs of roughly 15% to 17% of revenue are largely fixed in the half-year, and the adjusted margin is about 8%; a 10% change in revenue with costs unchanged therefore moves operating profit by roughly £12m to £15m, or 30% to 35% of FY2026 consensus EBIT. Evidence that this is the real mechanism: the 2024 collapse (North America from double-digit margins to a loss on under-absorption, the Components division's margin "reversed sharply as volume declines unhedged fixed overheads"); H1 2026's 230bp margin gain on falling revenue, which came from removing fixed loss-making capacity (Plano, Cleveland scrap) rather than from volume; Jefferies' sensitivity that a 50bp margin move changes EPS by 10.6% while a 1 point revenue move changes it by 1%; and Jefferies' scenario range of 90p on an 8% margin against 250p on a 10% margin. Consensus embeds about 23% incremental EBIT margin on revenue growth in FY2027 and FY2028 (£5m of EBIT on £21m to £22m of revenue), which is consistent with full fixed-cost absorption and no cost growth; this is reasonable for FY2027 given the £3m of extra savings and the Kuantan ramp, and slightly optimistic for FY2028 when wage and compliance inflation return. The range for the Consumer: on revenue 5% above consensus FY2027 the margin reaches about 10% and EPS about 19p; on revenue 5% below, the margin falls to about 8% and EPS to about 12p; this is a two-to-one range in EPS from a plus-or-minus 5% revenue cone, and the Components disposal narrows it by removing the highest-leverage division.

C-76

Financial leverage or gearing: amplification of EPS and risk, and comfort

C64

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: financial leverage is modest, falling and comfortable, and it now amplifies EPS upward through a shrinking interest line rather than threatening the equity. Net debt ex leases was £50.3m at FY2025 and £52.0m at H1 2026 (covenant leverage 1.1x against a 3.0x limit and a 1.0x to 2.0x target range, interest cover 5.6x against a 4.0x minimum); on FactSet's definition including leases net debt is £68m, 1.5x EBITDA. The debt is £75m of fixed-rate private-placement notes at an average 3.65% maturing 2028 and 2031, a £105m RCF to June 2028 with £8.3m drawn, and £14.4m of leases. Adjusted net finance expense fell 40% to £2.7m in H1 2026 from £4.5m; FactSet's net interest was £8.8m in FY2025 (£11.4m in FY2024). On FY2026 consensus EBIT of £40m, interest of about £5.5m is 14% of EBIT, so a £1m change in interest moves pre-tax profit by 3%; the amplification is small either way. The going-concern material uncertainty disclosed in 2024 is removed, and Jefferies projects net cash by FY2028 even without a disposal. The comfort is high; the only leverage-related risks are the covenant leverage rising if the H2 2026 working-capital release fails (a £15m miss would take covenant leverage to about 1.4x, still comfortable) and the equity's small-cap, thin-liquidity fragility in a risk-off regime, which is a market risk rather than a balance-sheet one. The balance sheet is a source of optionality (dividend, bolt-ons) that management is choosing not to use yet.

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable

❌ D48

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: the easy cuts have been made, the remaining cuttable cost is in footprint and central overhead, and management has signalled it will not cut engineering, so the Consumer should model the £6m programme as the last of the announced savings and treat further cuts as upside dependent on the Components sale and site consolidation. The cost base is roughly 75% to 78% cost of sales (materials the majority, direct labour and factory overhead the rest) and 15% to 17% selling, general and administrative. Materials are not cuttable except through procurement scale, which TT lacks, and through freight consolidation (done under Dynamo). Factory overhead has been cut by closing Plano (£5.7m annual loss removed), Mexicali, and the pre-2024 consolidations at Covina, Lutterworth and Medina, and by the Cleveland productivity gain (57% to 81%); the remaining lever is footprint, where eight UK manufacturing sites and several small North American and Asian sites against revenue per site in the bottom quartile suggest further consolidation is possible (hypothesis: a further one or two site closures could release £2m to £4m), but each closure has cost £4m to £7m in cash and inventory write-offs and disrupted customers. Administrative cost is where the current programme sits (£3m net FY2026, over £6m from FY2027, "strictly administrative", duplicated regional layers removed) and where central costs after the move to divisions could yield more. Engineering and R&D (3.8% of sales) are not cuttable without eroding the moat and are already below peers. The precedents: Project Dynamo's original £17m net target was mostly delivered through 2024 to 2025; discoverIE's footprint savings were £6.6m; Volex released 50bp of margin by consolidating three sites. The disposal of Components would remove the division with the least absorbable overhead.

C-78

Scope beyond cost cuts to improve outputs: achievability, few big areas or many small, congruence with strategy

C62

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement: the scope to improve financial outputs beyond cost cuts is large relative to the current base, comes from four or five big areas rather than many small ones, is supported by peer precedent, and is congruent with the stated strategy; this is the part of the case the Consumer should spend the most modelling effort on. The big areas, with rough sizing against FY2026 consensus EBIT of £40m: (1) North American EMS from roughly break-even (0.7% regional margin in FY2025) toward a 5% to 8% margin as Cleveland fills its spare capacity, worth £5m to £8m; (2) the Components disposal, which removes a 2% to 3% margin division and lifts the group margin by about 100bp (a mix effect of about £1m of lost profit against £75m to £80m of lost revenue), plus £2m of interest saved on the proceeds; (3) Power returning from 14.2% to its FY2025 16% as deferred contracts land and growing at a mid-teens incremental margin on a 1.14x book-to-bill, worth £3m to £5m; (4) Kuantan at full rate restoring £10m of revenue at a high incremental margin, worth £2m to £3m; (5) the tax rate, which is not an operating item but is the largest EPS lever: recognition of US deferred tax assets would take the adjusted rate from the high 30s to the mid 20s and add roughly 15% to 20% to adjusted EPS. Together the operating items reach £12m to £17m, which takes the remaining group (ex Components) to £50m to £55m of EBIT on about £430m of revenue, or 11.5% to 12.5%: management's 12% target is therefore arithmetically reachable from the identified areas without heroic growth, over three to four years. Peer precedents support achievability: Volex from 2.8% to 10.2% by exiting negative-margin work and consolidating sites; XP Power from 4.3% to 7.9% in a year by closing China and moving to Vietnam; Cicor doubling EBITDA margins at acquired sites, including TT's own former sites. The congruence is complete: every area is one of the four stated priorities. The risk is sequencing and timing, not existence.

C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, how it is changing, and what it means for predictability

❌ D42

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement: the perimeter is wide for the company's size, is narrowing deliberately, and remains a drag on predictability that the Components sale would materially reduce. Today: three divisions, four reported end markets, manufacturing in at least five countries (eight UK sites, Kansas City, Minneapolis, Cleveland, Juarez, Kuantan, Suzhou and a China joint venture), 79% of sales outside sterling in four main currencies, two reporting bases in transition, and customers from defence primes to distributors with order visibility from twelve weeks to twenty years. The memo's own simplicity test failed on six ranked drivers, nine named plants and five simultaneous headwinds. Narrowing: Project Albert (three sites), Plano, Mexicali, the Suzhou refocus, the move from three regions to three product divisions, and the Components disposal in process; after the sale the group would be two divisions (Power and EMS) with a clear profit centre (Power, about half of revenue and most of the profit) and one execution problem (EMS margins). Predictability consequence: each exit removes a source of variance (Components had the shortest visibility and highest leverage), but EMS remains a 5% to 8% margin business with customer-driven phasing, and Power's revenue follows procurement cadence. Peers are narrower (Solid State three UK-centred divisions; discoverIE one model across 30 businesses) or wider but larger (Volex five verticals). For the model: predictability improves from poor to moderate over the forecast period; the hinge is the disposal.

Company - Management

C5211 questions
C-5546C-5654C-5762C-57b56C-5840C-5946C-6062C-6150C-6238C-6350C-6440

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The management picture is a strong recent operating record on top of a weak institutional one. The new team (CEO from April 2025, CFO from June 2026, Chair from May 2026, SID from August 2026) has done what it said it would do, in order, on time, with plain communication and realistic targets: Plano closed, Cleveland fixed, Suzhou moved, the structure simplified, costs cut, leverage halved, guidance beaten and then raised. That is the evidence that the margin and cash forecasts can be trusted to a degree the company's history would not justify. What the team has not done, and has not claimed it will do, is build a growth engine or set an outlier ambition: capex and R&D are at the bottom of the peer set, M&A is parked, the 12% target is undated, the CEO's background and shareholding are those of a restructurer rather than an owner, the culture is in repair, and governance has been rebuilt after a failure rather than ahead of one. Against the peer set the management quality sits below Volex and discoverIE and alongside or above Solid State, Cicor and XP Power. For the model: give full weight to management's execution items (savings, site economics, cash conversion recovery, disposal process) and discount management's growth and ambition items (anything above 4% organic growth, anything beyond 10% margin inside three years) until the team has delivered a full year of organic growth. The two people-related risks to carry are key-person dependence on the CEO and the integration of a team that has not yet worked together through a difficult period.

C-55

Size of the company's ambition or vision, particularly against peers

❌ D46

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement: the ambition is deliberately modest, framed as restoration rather than conquest, and is the smallest in the peer set; this is the right posture for the moment and the wrong posture for a compounding case. The stated targets are 4% to 6% organic growth (at or below the market), a 12% adjusted operating margin now undated, mid-to-high-teens ROIC, 90%+ cash conversion, leverage below 1.5x, and a dividend "under review"; the CEO's own framing is to "consolidate near-term operational gains before trying to build a platform for growth", "profitable growth, not just top line", and "our focus is firmly on what we can control". Peers: Volex targets $2bn of revenue (from $1.24bn) and 12% margins with $300m of acquisitions and a buyback; discoverIE has raised its margin target for the tenth time to 17% and aims to double EPS every five years; Solid State targets £250m of revenue and 20p of EPS by 2030 from 11p. The previous TT management's ambition (12% by 2026, 4% to 6% growth, acquisitions) was larger and failed, which is why the new team's realism is credible and why the sell-side calls them "honest trackers". The consequence for the Consumer is that the company's own numbers will not pull estimates up; the sell-side and the model will have to do that from the building blocks.

C-56

Quality of the strategy: widening the moat, growth as a priority, dynamic across environments

C54

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: the strategy is sound, specific and defensible but not great or bold; it widens the moat marginally, puts margin and cash ahead of growth, and is robust to a downturn precisely because it asks little of the environment. The four priorities (product-led divisions, cost reduction, sales transformation, portfolio optimisation) are each measurable and each has already produced evidence; the move to product divisions aligns technology and customer engagement and has produced multi-site wins; the Components exit concentrates capital on the qualified core; the technology roadmap (SiC, silver sintering, AX-FORCE, Altitude DC) is the one moat-widening investment. What is missing is a growth engine: no acquisition programme, no new vertical, no scale-building footprint move, and R&D and capex below peers, which leaves the company dependent on its order book and the market's growth and with no answer to Volex's or discoverIE's compounding. The strategy is dynamic across environments in one sense (it works in a slow industrial economy because it is self-help) and not in another (it cannot exploit a strong upturn beyond the fixed-cost leverage it already has). Against the standard of "widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, dynamic": one of three.

C-57

Quality of the chief executive, judged by actions

C62

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement: Eric Lakin has, in eighteen months, delivered the most consequential set of actions in the company's recent history and has done so without over-promising, which makes him a strong operator by the evidence so far; the open questions are growth and a long record. Actions: became acting CEO the day Peter France left (April 2025), cut and widened FY2025 guidance immediately rather than defend it, closed Plano within two months of the decision, deployed external specialists to Cleveland and took productivity from 57% to 81% and rework from 12% to 5%, completed the Suzhou-to-Kuantan transfer, abolished the regional structure in favour of product divisions, launched and substantially completed a cost programme, instituted bid and pricing discipline, ran a Components review to a saleable conclusion, halved leverage, removed the going-concern uncertainty, rebuilt the finance function with a permanent CFO, and delivered FY2025 ahead of consensus and H1 2026 12% ahead, then raised guidance. Style: plain-spoken about internal failures ("the issues are internally in terms of yield, the cost of poor quality, and inventory management"), clear on mechanisms (tariff co-terms), realistic on targets. Weaknesses by the evidence: a divisional-finance and advisory background (Smiths Interconnect CFO, Ceres Power CFO, Credit Suisse, Alchemy, Oakley) rather than a factory-floor or standalone chief-executive record, so his capacity to drive organic growth is unproven; he holds only 21% of salary in shares against a 200% guideline and has made no open-market purchase; and the sector's precedent for finance-led restructurers is mixed (Nat Rothschild at Volex is the positive case). The archetype verdict in the leadership work ("strong on force and agency, strong on communication, partial on capital allocation, weak on outlier standards") is fair.

C-57b

Priority on hiring well, and the leadership team built

C56

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement: hiring has been a visible priority and the team is stronger than any TT has had for a decade at board and finance level, but it is thin below the executive and has not yet worked together through a full cycle. Hires: Ian Ashton as CFO (ex SIG plc and Low & Bonar, with buyout and 150%-of-salary LTIP awards to secure him) after a fourteen-month interim gap; Phil Swash as Chair (BAE Systems, Airbus, GKN; bought 100,000 shares at 119p on appointment); Mary Waldner as SID and Audit Chair (listed CFO experience at Ultra Electronics and Senior); Karina Rigby as NED (Eaton, Siemens global P&L); a rebuilt full-strength site team at Cleveland; upgraded business-development talent under the sales transformation; external investor relations. The leadership work rates key-person dependency high, internal successor visibility "severely constrained" across the three divisions, and both CEO and CFO under two years in post; the divisional heads under the new structure are not named in the file. Comparison: Volex has a COO of nine years, five regional COOs and virtually no senior turnover; discoverIE has a 17-year CEO and 16-year CFO. TT's bench is therefore a work in progress, with the board now ahead of the executive in depth.

C-58

How well resourced and invested the whole business is

❌ D40

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement: the business is under-resourced and has been deliberately starved to fund deleveraging, which is a rational short-term choice that leaves it with less resilience than its peers and limits its ability to lead the next technology cycle. Net capex was £7.5m to £8.1m in FY2025 (1.8% of sales, down from £22.4m in 2023) against peer averages near 3.5% (Volex 3% to 4%, Cicor 3%); product development additions fell to £1.1m; R&D runs at 3.8% of sales against XP Power's 12%; inventory was cut to a trough of 12.6% of revenue before being rebuilt; over 500 jobs were removed, mostly in North America; the site estate includes assets that Deloitte audits substantively because systems are decentralised and documentation is weak. Positives: the £105m RCF with £8.3m drawn gives liquidity, the pension is bought in, the Cleveland site has spare capacity and new SMT lines are installed at Kuantan, and capex is guided to rise to about £12.5m. The "ant colony" test (built yesterday for resilience) is failed: the 2024 crisis showed that site-level failures propagate to the group with no buffer, and the risks family's correlated-failure analysis (factory execution plus demand softness) remains the central downside. For the model: capex at 2.5% to 3% of sales is the right assumption, and a return of R&D intensity toward 4.5% to 5% should be considered as a cost the next phase of growth will require.

C-59

How holistic and congruent the culture is across the total system

❌ D46

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement: the system is becoming congruent after a period in which it was not, but it is a culture under repair rather than an aligned one, and the alignment that exists is mechanical (incentives, structure) rather than cultural (Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair style). Congruent elements: STIP weighted 50% to adjusted operating profit and 25% to free cash flow with 30% deferred into shares, LTIP on 12% EPS CAGR and 95% cash conversion, the product-led structure aligning technology with customers, site-level accountability with pulse surveys feeding the board, bid discipline aligned with "profitable growth", and a Chair, CEO and CFO all chosen for operational and financial discipline. Incongruent elements: the leadership work classes the archetype as "bureaucratic transitioning toward functional centralisation" with frontline morale strained by closures and 500 redundancies; the Best Companies three-star rating with 91% participation dates from 2023, before the crisis, and the framework was then abandoned for pulse surveys; executives earned £375k of variable pay in 2025 while the dividend was suspended; decentralised controls allowed a site to misstate; the CEO's shareholding is 21% of salary. Senior leadership engagement improved between April 2025 and January 2026 and key engineers were retained against headhunting. Peers: discoverIE's decentralised model with founders retained and voluntary turnover of 12%, Volex's kaizen system across 23 sites and 3% voluntary turnover, are more holistic. For predictability, a culture in repair argues for a wider cone on execution items (Kuantan, Cleveland persistence) than the incentives alone would suggest.

C-60

Clarity and helpfulness of communication, including the driver transmission mechanism

C62

Question asked How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement: communication is clear, direct and above the small-cap standard, and it got better under the new team; its one weakness is the absence of quantified guidance for the lines the market most needs. Strengths: a company-compiled consensus range is published and guided against; the H1 2026 report gives an underlying organic growth figure that strips the self-inflicted items; divisional margins and book-to-bill are disclosed; FX sensitivities are given per cent and per RMB 0.1; the tax rate is guided with its cause; the cost programme is quantified (£3m net, over £6m run-rate) with its restructuring cost; cash conversion is guided with the working-capital reason; the transmission mechanism (Cleveland as the largest supplier to the Kansas Power site, Plano's last-time-buy effect, Kuantan's safety-stock drag) is explained; and the CEO names internal causes rather than macro excuses. Weaknesses: no revenue or margin guidance beyond "ahead of expectations", the 12% target undated, no capex guidance, no price/volume split, the FY2026 divisional baseline restated without a long history, and the earlier management's over-promising (12% by 2026) left a legacy of scepticism that still costs the shares a Hold at Jefferies. Compared with Volex (precise annual guidance, usually beaten) and discoverIE (conservative numbers "with the intention fully of delivering on them"), TT is now in the same honest-tracker class but with less precision.

C-61

Record of acting, not just talking: recent huge delivery and mid-term track record

C50

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement: the recent record (eighteen months) is one of huge delivery and the mid-term record (three to five years) is one of repeated misses and justifications, so the Consumer should weight the recent record for execution items and the mid-term record for anything that depends on demand or on ambition. Recent delivery: FY2025 adjusted operating profit of £37.2m against a cut guidance midpoint of about £36m and consensus of £34.5m; H1 2026 at £18.5m against £16.5m consensus; Cleveland from a £5.7m restatement and heavy losses to consistent profit; Plano closed on schedule; Suzhou transferred; leverage from 1.8x to 1.1x; guidance raised. Mid-term record: the 10% margin target set in 2019 was missed (8.6% in 2023, 7.1% in 2024); the 12% by 2026 target set at the April 2024 Capital Markets Day was withdrawn twelve months later; profit warnings in late 2023, April 2024 and November 2025; four severe downgrades in eight months in 2024; FY2024 EBITA of £37.1m against guidance of £37m to £42m and consensus 27% higher a year earlier; a £5.7m restatement; £93.6m of North American impairments; Stadium written down. Delivery in tougher environments was worse, not better: the destocking downturn exposed the factories. The honest conclusion is that the current team's record is short but unblemished and the company's record is long and poor; the market is pricing the former and the risks family the latter.

C-62

Outlier standards: higher, faster, better, with a record of out-executing competitors

❌ D38

Question asked To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

Judgement: TT does not hold outlier standards and its record is of being out-executed by competitors; the current improvement is from below-par to par, not to outlier. Evidence: the yield, scrap and inventory failures at Cleveland and Kansas City in 2024; the restatement from inappropriate capitalisation; stock-outs noted by industry experts; Cleveland productivity of 81% described as the target (not a stretch); a rework rate target of 5%; Cicor doubling margins at the sites it bought from TT; Volex's near-perfect guidance record and 2.8%-to-10.2% margin arc; discoverIE's ten consecutive margin-target upgrades; XP Power's one-year margin recovery. The leadership work's archetype verdict on outlier standards is "weak". The positive is that standards are now measured and published (productivity, rework, on-time delivery, pulse surveys) and that bid discipline has replaced volume-chasing. For the model, assume execution at industry-normal standards, which is enough for the 9% to 10% margin path and not enough for 12% without mix help.

C-63

Shareholders first, and prudence of governance: what the evidence shows and why

C50

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement: the evidence shows a company that has moved from inadequate governance to adequate governance with real scaffolding being erected, but one where minority shareholders sit behind a 29% blockholder and where management's own financial exposure to the outcome is thin. Shareholders-first evidence: the board recommended a 150p cash offer at a 58% premium rather than defend independence; the previous board rejected Volex at 135.5p to 139.6p and a higher third-party offer as undervaluing the assets, which with hindsight at 186p was right; zero bonus and zero LTIP vesting in 2024, malus applied to 2023 awards; CEO pay of £909k at 0.7x the sector median; 2026 executive salary rises capped at 2.5% below the workforce's 3.75%; dividends suspended to protect the balance sheet; the RCF right-sized; the Components sale "strictly subject to value". Governance scaffolding: 71% board independence, 43% female representation, fully independent committees, an audit chair with listed-CFO experience, a 50% non-audit fee cap against the 70% statutory limit, a Provision 29 readiness project, ISO 27001, an ethics portal, Deloitte since 2020 after a tender, one share one vote with no defences. Evidence against: the £5.7m restatement from "inappropriate direction from senior finance staff", Deloitte's zero reliance on controls, £375k of 2025 executive variable pay while dividends stayed at nil, executive shareholdings under 0.5% of the float and the CEO at 21% of salary against a 200% guideline, non-executives holding no shares collectively before the Chair's purchase, £1.6m of duplicated management transition cost, the former Chair selling his entire holding at 109p in January 2026, DBAY's 29.04% near-blocking stake and its prior relationship with the new Chair (reviewed and cleared), and the generous £533k departure terms for the former CEO. On balance: prudent, improving, not yet "scaffolding put in place where it was not needed"; the scaffolding is being built where it was needed and after the collapse.

C-64

Enlightened capital allocator and dynamic repurchaser, with a record of high-return allocation

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: TT is not an enlightened capital allocator by record and is not a repurchaser at all; the current team is a disciplined de-allocator, which is the appropriate first step but not the thing the question asks for. Record: Stadium (2018) and Precision acquisitions led to a £32.5m write-down and the disposal of Hartlepool and Dongguan; Torotel and Ferranti added complexity; £93.6m of North American goodwill was impaired over 2024 to 2025; Project Albert sold three sites for £20.8m (£12.2m net) after a £32.5m held-for-sale write-down, and the buyer recovered 84% of the price within nine months; cumulative restructuring charges exceeded £43.9m between 2019 and 2023 before the £15.2m of 2025; dividends were paid through 2023 at a 62% payout while leverage rose to 1.8x and then suspended. Current practice: priority to net debt reduction, organic investment second, dividend under review for March 2027, bolt-ons only "medium-term" and "selective", no buyback contemplated, the Components sale value-led. Peers: discoverIE's thirty deals under 9x EBITA with no equity issuance and 28% ROCE on pre-FY2018 acquisitions; Volex's 112 targets reviewed for six completed at 5.2x EBITDA and a public walk-away from TT; Oxford Instruments' £100m buyback. The allocation decisions that matter in the forecast period are three and all are defensive: the price achieved for Components, the timing and size of the dividend, and whether proceeds go to debt or to a bolt-on. The Consumer should model proceeds to debt and a dividend of 2p to 3p from FY2027, and should not model buybacks.

Risks

❌ D404 questions
C-8052C-8145C-8233C-8335

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The risk picture has inverted since the August memo: the fundamental risks have fallen (the factories are fixed, leverage is 1.1x, guidance has been raised, the order book covers next year) and the price risk has risen (the shares are up 95% in twelve months, at the 93rd percentile of their own forward P/E, in a risk-off regime, with the cohort's restocking test and the company's own cash-conversion test both inside six months). The fundamental downside that remains is a correlated site-execution failure (Kuantan, Cleveland, a defence programme slip) that would take the margin back toward 7% and EPS to about 9p; its probability is moderate and it is not a solvency event. The sentiment downside is a 20% to 35% de-rating on no change in earnings, with triggers in December 2026 (discoverIE's interims), March 2027 (TT's cash conversion and dividend) and in DBAY's hands at any time. The growth engine is no longer decelerating. For the model: carry a base case on consensus, a correlated-execution downside (margin 7%, revenue 5% below consensus, EPS about 9p in FY2027) and a sentiment case in which earnings are met and the multiple returns to 8.5x; the trough multiple for stress testing is 7x on cut earnings, about 90p to 95p.

C-80

Deceleration of the core growth engine over the last twelve months

C52

Question asked To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement: the deceleration happened in 2023 to 2025 and has now stopped; over the last twelve months the revenue growth rate has run well below the company's normal rate but the input change force has turned from slowing to accelerating, so the question for the model is the pace of re-acceleration, not further deceleration. Reported revenue growth was +29.6% (FY2022), -0.5% (FY2023), -15.1% (FY2024) and -7.6% (FY2025); organic growth was +3% (2023), -2.0% (2024), -2.7% (2025) and -2.7% in H1 2026 (but +4% underlying once Plano and Suzhou are excluded); the company's "normal rate", its own through-cycle target, is 4% to 6%, so it has run below normal for three years. The input forces: short-cycle industrial and distribution demand, the force that drove the decline (distribution -27% in 2024, Automation and Electrification -13% in 2025, Asia -9.2%), is now rising (Components +5.8% organic in H1 2026, cohort book-to-bill above 1.0, distributors reporting stock too low); the Aerospace and Defence force never decelerated (+12% in FY2025, European book-to-bill 135%) though Power's H1 2026 revenue was flat on customer finalisation delays, which is phasing rather than demand; healthcare has stopped falling. The honest caveat is that re-acceleration is so far visible in orders and underlying growth rather than in reported revenue, and that the sector's restocking pulse is expected to plateau by late 2026, so the engine could re-accelerate to 4% in FY2027 and slow again in FY2028 if use demand does not follow refill demand.

C-81

Fundamental risks that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability: plausibility and size

❌ D45

Question asked What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

Judgement: seven such risks are identifiable and none of them threatens solvency, but three could remove a quarter or more of forecast EBIT in a single period, and two of those are correlated with each other. (1) Kuantan ramp failure: the transferred EMS customer's volumes do not reach full rate or are disrupted; medium plausibility, £5m to £10m of revenue and £2m to £3m of profit in FY2027, plus customer-relationship damage. (2) A defence programme slip or award delay: Power's revenue is lumpy on procurement cadence (US stopgap budgets, the slipped UK Defence Investment Plan); medium plausibility, £5m to £15m of revenue shifting between halves with mid-teens incremental margin lost or deferred; already partly visible in H1 2026 Power. (3) A relapse at Cleveland or another North American site: productivity flattening and rework rising; low-to-medium plausibility, but the precedent is a £2.7m regional loss and £52.2m of write-downs within a year of the 2024 failure; correlated with (1) because both are site-execution risks under the same thin management bench. (4) Another prior-period restatement from decentralised controls on which Deloitte places zero reliance; low-to-medium plausibility, magnitude £3m to £6m and a confidence shock out of proportion to the sum. (5) A component shortage halting a programme (memory, mature-node ICs, PCBs booked to 2028); medium plausibility, small magnitude per event but a delivery-penalty and reputational tail. (6) A sterling spike (GBP/USD to 1.40 with MYR and CNY strength): the risks family's reverse-engineered case strips £4.5m from operating profit; low-to-medium plausibility, with the current forecast consensus the other way. (7) Regulatory default under EO 14415 or a poor NIST score excluding TT from a US award; low plausibility, high magnitude for the Kansas City business. A customer design-out is the risk the moat makes least likely (no customer above 10%, 18-to-24-month requalification, zero losses through 2024). The joint plausibility of (1), (2) and (3) is the risks family's "high" correlated-failure case and the one I would carry as the explicit downside scenario: operating margin back toward 7% in FY2027 (Jefferies' bear 7.0% by FY2027), EPS near 9p.

C-82

Perception- or sentiment-driven risks that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere

❌ D33

Question asked What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: sentiment risk is the largest risk to the share price over the next twelve months and is larger than the fundamental risk, because the shares have re-rated 95% in a year to the 98th relative-strength percentile and the 93rd percentile of their own five-year forward P/E range in a regime the portfolio's own environment reading describes as refusing re-ratings and punishing fragility. Specific triggers: (1) the restocking pulse plateauing or being revealed as refill rather than use, which the sector expects by late 2026 and which XP Power's 20 October update already illustrated ("early customer bookings inflate Q3 orders, reported revenue dips", shares fell); the cohort's dated test is discoverIE's half-year in early December 2026, and a disappointment there would be read across to TT regardless of its own trading. (2) Defence award timing: Solid State fell 15% in three months when UK awards paused, and the sector rule is that these shares follow awards, not budgets. (3) The AI-chain credit crack spilling into electronics manufacturers generally; TT has no AI exposure but trades in the cohort. (4) DBAY: at 29.04% a move above 30% forces a bid, a sale of part of the stake would remove the implicit floor and flood a stock trading $1m a day, and a renewed bid rejected by DBAY would cap the shares as the Cicor episode did; the sector digest's finding that a bid can set a ceiling applies. (5) The March 2027 results: a cash conversion print below 70%, a dividend not reinstated, or H2 profit growth that looks muted because the H2 2025 comparator contains £3.5m of Plano last-time-buy profit, could each trigger a de-rating even if the underlying trajectory is intact. (6) Small-cap and UK fiscal risk-off flows. The skew over twelve months is negative on sentiment and positive on fundamentals, and the cone is wide.

C-83

How far the shares could fall to a fair trough multiple

❌ D35

Question asked How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement: a fall of 20% to 35% is the sentiment-only trough and a fall of 40% to 50% is the earnings-plus-sentiment trough, and neither is far-fetched for a stock with this history. Reference points: the 24-month-forward P/E is 10.5x against a five-year median of 7.2x (25th to 75th percentile 6.5x to 8.2x, 10th percentile 4.6x) and ten-year bands of 6.1x ("crash"), 8.5x ("conservative"), 9.8x (median) and 12.5x ("defendable"); 24-month-forward EPS is about 17p to 17.5p. On unchanged earnings: the ten-year conservative band of 8.5x gives about 148p (-20%); the five-year median of 7.2x gives about 126p (-32%); the ten-year crash band of 6.1x gives about 107p (-42%). With earnings cut by 25% to about 13p (the correlated execution downside, margin back toward 7%) at 7.2x: about 94p (-50%), which matches Jefferies' own 90p downside scenario on an 8% margin and sits above its older 55p bear case. Informal floors are below these levels: DBAY's last proposal was 130p and Volex's 135.5p to 139.6p, and neither is a hard floor given the NSI Act review and DBAY's veto. Against the memo's park at 137.6p in August, the shares are now 35% higher with consensus targets of 185p to 190p, so the fair trough has moved up with estimates but the distance to it has widened. The modelling implication is that the entry point, not the business, is where the risk sits: the multiple is at the top of its own history even though it is the cheapest seat in its cohort (discoverIE 18.5x, Volex 14.5x).
