Guidance

C566 questions
C-6855C-7056C-7158C-7258C-8558C-7350

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Guidance under the new team is honest, mechanism-rich and number-light. It has been raised once, after evidence, in words rather than figures; it is level with consensus for FY2026 (with consensus at the top of the words), absent for FY2027, and far above consensus in the undated medium term. The building blocks (savings, cash conversion, tax, FX, Kuantan, Power phasing) are specific enough to build FY2026 and FY2027 from the bottom up and point to profit modestly above consensus in both years, while the one non-guided assumption that most changes EPS, the tax rate, is where consensus has quietly become more optimistic than management. The history of guidance before 2025 was poor and is the reason the shares still carry Hold ratings at 186p; the behaviour since is the reason the shares are at 186p. For the model: take FY2026 adjusted operating profit at £41m to £42m against consensus £40m, FY2027 at £46m to £47m against £45m, hold the adjusted tax rate at 35% in FY2027 unless the Consumer chooses to model recognition explicitly, and treat the 12% margin as a four-year ambition reachable only with the Components disposal.

C-68

Guidance above or below consensus for this year and next, and why

C55

Question asked To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: for FY2026 guidance and consensus are level, with consensus having moved to the top of what the words imply; for FY2027 there is no guidance and consensus stands alone; for the medium term management's ambition sits far above consensus. FY2026: on 2 September 2026 the board guided adjusted operating profit "ahead of current market expectations" when the company-compiled consensus range was £32.6m to £38.5m; Jefferies read this as £37m to £39m and raised its own figure to £39.2m; FactSet's median EBIT is now £40.0m (RBC 8 October, Investec, Stifel, Jefferies), which is at or slightly above the literal guidance. Why consensus sits at the top: H1 was £18.5m, the group's own seasonality puts 54% of profit in H2, the second £1.5m of savings and the Kuantan volumes land in H2, and the sell-side has decided to believe the trajectory. FY2027: no guidance; consensus EBIT £45.0m (8.9%) rests on brokers extrapolating the £6m savings run-rate, the order book and the Power recovery; Jefferies is at £44.6m (9.0%). Medium term: management's 12% margin against consensus FY2028 and FY2029 margins of 9.5% and 9.9%, so consensus does not believe the target inside three years, and management has not dated it. Other lines: cash conversion guided 70% to 80% and consensus free cash flow of £9m is consistent; tax guided mid-to-high 30s and consensus FY2026 EPS of 11.9p is consistent, while consensus FY2027 EPS of 15.9p implicitly assumes the rate falls toward 30%, which is not guided. Net: guidance is neither above nor below consensus for the current year, is absent for next year, and is above consensus beyond that for the right reason (the target is undated).

C-70

The record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance

C56

Question asked Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement: the company had no record of upgrading guidance between 2022 and mid-2026 and has now done it once, in the manner of an honest tracker: set low, hold, deliver, then raise with evidence. The history: an October 2022 reset ("increasing uncertainty"), a November 2024 move to the lower end of the range, an April 2025 cut of the FY2025 EBITA range from £40m to £46m to £32m to £40m (16% at the midpoint) with the CEO leaving the same day, and the withdrawal of the 12% by 2026 target. Under Eric Lakin: FY2025 guidance reiterated at the June 2025 five-month update and at the September 2025 interims, then delivered at £37.2m against a revised £33.7m and consensus of £34.5m; FY2026 set in March 2026 "in line with consensus" and reiterated at the May AGM despite sales down 4.8% organically over four months; then raised in September 2026 after a 12% beat. The nature of the upgrade is qualitative ("ahead of market expectations") rather than numeric, which preserves room and is characteristic of a management that does not want to be caught again; the leadership and change-forces work both call the archetype "honest trackers rather than over-promising". The behavioural read for the Consumer: this management will not pre-announce upside and will only raise when a half is in the bag, so consensus will tend to lag delivery in an upturn, which is the mechanism by which further beats occur; it is not yet a sandbagging-with-precision archetype because the guidance is too loose to sandbag precisely.

C-71

How conservative is guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five, adjusted for input change forces

C58

Question asked How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement: FY2026 guidance (and the consensus £40m that embodies it) is moderately conservative against the current input forces, and the medium-term 12% target is not conservative at all; the Consumer should lean FY2026 to FY2027 profit modestly above consensus and the out-years at or below. The arithmetic for FY2026: H1 was £18.5m; the group's typical 54% H2 weighting alone implies about £40m; H2 adds roughly £1.5m of incremental savings, the return of about £10m of Kuantan revenue at a high incremental margin, Power's deferred contracts and Components' continued recovery, so £41m to £43m is reachable and £40m is the floor case. The comparator trap: H2 2025 adjusted operating profit was about £23.7m including £3.5m of Plano last-time-buy profit (about £20.2m without it), so consensus H2 2026 of £21.5m is +6% on the clean comparator and -9% on the reported one; reported H2 profit growth will look muted even when underlying growth is mid-teens. Against the last five years: adjusted operating profit was £37.1m to £52.8m (FY2023 on the earnings-review table; £47.1m on another reading) on revenue of £521m to £617m, so FY2026 at £40m on £482m is below the FY2023 level on a smaller base and a slightly lower margin (8.3% against 8.6%); the input forces then (post-pandemic restocking) were similar in kind to now (early restocking plus structural defence), which argues that an 8.3% margin is not a demanding ask. FY2027 consensus of £45m at 8.9% is fair rather than conservative: it requires the full £6m of savings, 4.4% growth and no FX or Power dilution. The 12% target implies £55m to £60m on today's perimeter and is a four-year ambition, not guidance.

C-72

How specific is the guidance: building blocks or vague

C58

Question asked How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

Judgement: guidance is specific about mechanisms and sparse about numbers: every driver is named and most are sized, but the output lines the market models most (revenue, margin, capex, working capital) are not guided. Stated building blocks: £3m net savings in FY2026 rising to over £6m annualised from FY2027 with about £3m of restructuring cost, self-funded and "strictly administrative"; cash conversion 70% to 80% for FY2026 because of a planned working-capital build, 90%+ medium term; an adjusted tax rate in the mid-to-high 30s% for FY2026 with the cause (US deferred tax non-recognition) and the normalised rate (mid 20s) stated; FX sensitivity of £0.1m per cent on GBP/USD and £0.1m per RMB 0.1; a £10m revenue effect from the Suzhou transfer, now complete, with Kuantan "positioned to support higher production volumes in H2"; Power's deferred contracts "finalised" and expected to drive H2 phasing; an H2 return to organic growth; leverage below 1.5x; a dividend update in March 2027; the Components process "subject to value". Not given: FY2026 or FY2027 revenue or margin, divisional margin guidance, capex, the size of the H2 working-capital unwind, the Components perimeter or proceeds, a date for 12%. Against peers: Volex guides revenue and profit floors annually and beats them; discoverIE guides earnings "ahead of expectations" with a dated margin target; Solid State guides revenue "not less than" a figure. TT is more specific than Solid State on mechanisms and less specific than Volex on outputs. For the Consumer, the building blocks are sufficient to construct FY2026 and FY2027 from the bottom up; the gaps are exactly the lines the second set's research should fill.

C-85

Informal and qualitative commentary against formal guidance and consensus, and why

C58

Question asked To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: informal commentary runs ahead of formal guidance and modestly ahead of consensus for the next twelve months, and the gap is explained by management's decision to guide only on what has already been delivered. The qualitative statements: "transitioned from stabilising the business in 2025 to executing a clearly defined value creation plan"; a return to organic revenue growth in H2 driven by improving order intake across divisions; Cleveland "not only profitable but also has existing capacity to handle increased demand"; Kuantan positioned for higher H2 volumes; Power's finalised contracts to deliver phasing benefits in H2; healthcare "stabilising" with encouraging scientific-instrument logos; wafer-fabrication order growth in Asia; Components "supported by stronger distributor markets"; early commercial benefits from the product-led structure including multi-site wins; the lifetime Rolls-Royce agreement and MBDA letter of intent secured post-period; "an encouraging number of indications of interest" for Components; the cost programme "substantially complete". Set against the numbers: consensus FY2026 revenue of £482m needs H2 revenue of about £254m (+4% on H2 2025), which is exactly what the commentary describes, so consensus revenue is not stretched; consensus H2 profit of £21.5m is below what the commentary implies once savings, Kuantan and Power phasing are added, so the commentary points to £41m to £43m; for FY2027 the 4% to 6% growth target and the full £6m of savings support consensus of £45m with some upside. The one place commentary is more cautious than consensus is tax: management guides a high rate with no date for recognition, while consensus FY2027 EPS implies a lower rate. Why the gap exists: the honest-tracker posture and a management that has been burned twice by precision.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about guidance or investor relations

C50

Question asked What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement: six features are uncommon and three of them matter to the forecast. (1) The company publishes a compiled consensus range and guides against it, which makes "ahead of expectations" a measurable statement and makes beats and misses visible at each update. (2) Coverage is thin and was thinner: Deutsche Bank discontinued in September 2025, leaving Jefferies as the dominant voice, and only two brokers formed the interim consensus; four now contribute (Jefferies, RBC, Investec, Stifel), which means consensus is still easily moved by one model and that the dispersion (FY2027 EPS from 15.3p to 17.0p; revenue from £490m to £521m) is wide. (3) Profit is second-half weighted (about 54%) and the H2 2025 comparator contains £3.5m of non-repeating last-time-buy profit, so FY2026 reported H2 growth will understate the run-rate. (4) The reporting basis changed in 2026 from regions to product divisions with only FY2025 restated, so divisional history is one year deep and the Consumer will have to rebuild EMS and Components trends from regional data. (5) Investor relations moved to an external agency (Equitory), and a 29% shareholder sits outside the normal IR relationship with the power to block transactions. (6) The incentive plan gives an internal target the Consumer can use: full LTIP vesting needs 12% constant-currency EPS compound growth and 95% cash conversion over three years from 2026, which from 11.9p implies about 16.7p by FY2029 (below consensus 19.9p) and tells the Consumer that management's own stretch on EPS is below the street's. Results dates: FY2026 on 10 March 2027, an AGM trading update in May, interims in September; no quarterly reporting.

Sell-side estimates

C553 questions
C-6756C-6946C-7462

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The sell-side has been through the full cycle: over-optimism (23p of FY2026 EPS in early 2025), capitulation (8.7p by March 2026, unanimous Hold, a discontinued coverage), and the start of a rebuild (four brokers, 60% Buy, FY2026 EPS +37% in six months, targets +40% in three months). The forecasts that result are credible in their operating shape (a margin recovery to about 9.5% on 4% growth, consistent with the self-help building blocks and the order book), modestly conservative on FY2026 and FY2027 profit, and quietly optimistic on the tax rate from FY2027. The delivery record says surprises follow the cycle: beats in restocking upturns, misses in destocking downturns, both amplified by operating leverage and site execution. The estimate momentum is positive but the easy upgrades (FY2026) are done; the next upgrades require FY2027 evidence that will not arrive before March 2027. For the model: treat consensus EBIT as a floor for FY2026 and FY2027, build EPS from an explicit tax assumption rather than from consensus, note that the fourth forecast year rests on two brokers, and expect consensus to keep lagging delivery for as long as management guides qualitatively.

C-67

Sell-side forecasts for the next one to three years against the last one to five, line by line, and the credibility of the gap

C56

Question asked How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

Judgement: the forecasts describe a margin and tax recovery on flat-to-modest revenue, which is credible in shape, modestly conservative on profit in FY2026 and FY2027, and dependent on an unstated tax assumption from FY2027; by FY2029 they only restore the company to its FY2023 earnings. Revenue: consensus £482m, £503m, £525m, £535m (FY2026 to FY2029), growth of 0.1%, 4.4%, 4.4% and 1.9% (the last on two brokers), against history of £617m, £614m, £521m, £481m (FY2022 to FY2025) and growth of +29.6%, -0.5%, -15.1%, -7.6%; the forecast growth is below the 4% to 6% target in year one and at its low end thereafter, and the order book (£550m, +20%) covers FY2027 almost fully, so revenue is credible and, if restocking persists, slightly low in FY2027. EBITDA margin (brokers' basis): 10.9%, 11.5%, 12.1%, 12.3% against 10.3% in FY2025 on the same basis and a FactSet operating peak of 12.0% in FY2019; EBIT: £40.0m, £45.0m, £50.0m, £52.9m at 8.3%, 8.9%, 9.5%, 9.9% against 7.7% in FY2025, 7.1% in FY2024 and 8.6% in FY2023; the incremental EBIT margin embedded is about 23%, consistent with fixed-cost absorption plus £6m of savings, credible for FY2027 and a little full for FY2028 without the Components exit. Pre-tax profit: £33.7m, £39.0m, £44.2m, £48.9m, implying net interest falling from about £6m to £4m, credible on the debt path. EPS (brokers' basis): 11.9p, 15.9p, 17.8p, 19.9p against 19.0p, 10.9p and 6.8p for FY2023 to FY2025; the implied tax rate falls from about 37% in FY2026 to roughly 27% to 30% in FY2027 and beyond (15.9p on £39.0m pre-tax over about 180m shares), which is the least-supported assumption in the set because management guides no date for US deferred-tax recognition; on a 35% rate FY2027 EPS would be about 14p. Free cash flow: £9.0m, £18.9m, £26.7m, £30.4m against FactSet's £18.7m, £21.5m, £34.2m for FY2023 to FY2025, credible given the working-capital rebuild and capex rising to £12.5m to £14m (2.6% of sales against 1.7% to 3.6% historically). Dividends: nil, 2.5p, 3.1p, 4.3p, credible from FY2027. Net debt: total debt £99.6m to £92.0m on FactSet, with Jefferies at net cash by FY2028. Share count flat at about 180m to 186m, credible (no buyback, LTIP dilution small). Credibility of the gap overall: the operating lines are consistent with the change forces (self-help, mix, order book) and the foundations (high operating leverage from a trough); the gap that is not fully credible is EPS, where consensus has embedded a tax improvement the company has not guided; coverage is four brokers, so "consensus" is thin.

C-69

Record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and estimates over one, three and five years, including comparable conditions

❌ D46

Question asked What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

Judgement: the one-year record is beats, the three-year record is dominated by a severe miss, and the five-year record is a cycle of beat-in-upturn and miss-in-downturn, which means the record in conditions comparable to the next eighteen months (an early restocking upturn) is favourable and the record in conditions comparable to a renewed destocking is poor. One year: FY2025 adjusted operating profit £37.2m against consensus £34.5m (beat, +8%) and revised guidance £33.7m (beat) with revenue slightly below (£481.4m against £483m) and EPS a miss on tax (6.9p against 8.0p); H1 2026 £18.5m against £16.5m consensus (+12%) and Jefferies £17.5m (+6%), EPS 5.7p against 4.0p (+43%), revenue in line. Three years: FY2024 EBITA £37.1m against guidance of £37m to £42m (miss at the bottom) and against the prior-year consensus 27% higher (severe miss), after four downgrades in eight months and two profit warnings; FY2023 met its targets and beat on organic growth; FY2025 as above. Five years: FY2021 and FY2022 delivered growth but with an October 2022 reset on "increasing uncertainty" and a profit warning in late 2023; the 10% margin target set in 2019 was never met. In comparable conditions: in the 2021 to 2022 post-pandemic restocking the company grew revenue 10% and 30% and beat; in the 2023 to 2025 destocking it missed badly because fixed-cost under-absorption met factory failure. The structural lesson is that TT's results amplify the cycle through operating leverage and site execution, so the direction of the cycle predicts the direction of the surprise better than management's own words do.

C-74

Have estimates been cut deeply and fully, and has guidance been rebased, kitchen-sinked or sandbagged

C62

Question asked To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

Judgement: yes to all three in the twelve months to March 2026, which is the textbook set-up for an upgrade cycle, and the upgrade cycle has now begun, so the question has shifted from "is the trough in" to "how much of the rebound is already in the estimates". The cuts: consensus FY2026 EPS fell 57.3% between March 2025 and March 2026 (Zeus), with the FactSet series ranging from 23.4p to 8.7p and FY2027 from 27.9p to 13.7p; Jefferies cut FY2026 revenue by 15% and EPS by 33% in April 2026; Deutsche Bank discontinued its Buy; every active broker went to Hold; twelve-month revisions are still negative on every line (EPS -13%, EBITDA -11%, sales -7%). The rebasing: FY2025 guidance cut 16% at the midpoint in April 2025 with the range widened; the 12% target withdrawn; FY2026 set "in line with consensus" at a transition-year level. The kitchen-sinking: £65.4m of adjusting items in FY2025 including £41.4m of impairments and £15.2m of restructuring, £93.6m of North American goodwill written off over two years, US deferred tax assets derecognised, a dividend of nil. The sandbagging: mild, the FY2026 "in line" guidance was held through a weak four-month update and then raised after H1. Since then: FY2026 EPS +37% over six months, FY2027 +14% over three months and +6% over one, target prices +40% over three months, buy share 60% (two of four), RBC and Investec and Stifel initiating or re-entering. The depth and breadth of the cut were complete; the recovery in estimates is about half done by the measure of the FY2027 series (13.7p trough, 15.6p now, 27.9p prior peak, though the prior peak was unrealistic). The remaining upgrade room is in FY2027 and FY2028 EBIT (if the £6m savings and Kuantan deliver) and in the tax rate, not in FY2026.

Stock market narrative

C551 question
C-2258

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The narrative has moved in twelve months from "another TT profit warning is coming" to "the turnaround is delivered, now prove the cash and the growth", and the share price has moved with it to the consensus target. What is left for the market to look forward to is a short list of dated, well-founded steps (clean FY2026 results with cash conversion in range, a dividend, the Components sale) and a longer list of undated ones (tax recognition, a dated margin target, net cash, corporate interest). The next step on the sector's two-step clean-up pattern is the March 2027 print, and it carries a comparator trap (the £3.5m Plano last-time-buy in H2 2025) that could make a good result look ordinary. The cheapest seat in the cohort at 10x is cheap against peers and expensive against its own history, which is why the narrative risk is now a de-rating on a sentiment event rather than a downgrade on a fundamental one. For the model: the staircase supports a base case in which estimates drift up modestly through 2027, with the disposal and the tax step as the two dated, discrete items to carry as scenarios rather than in the base.

C-22

The staircase of novel positive developments: next 6 months, months 6 to 18, months 18 to 36; novelty and foundation

C58

Question asked What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

Judgement: the staircase is real and dated but front-loaded with developments the market already expects, so its novelty is moderate and its foundation is good for the near steps and speculative for the far ones. Next six months (to April 2027): (1) the Components disposal, the most novel and the least certain step (indications of interest received, process value-led, no timing given; a sale at £35m to £40m would cut net debt to under £15m, lift the margin about 100bp and simplify the group to two divisions; medium foundation); (2) FY2026 results on 10 March 2027, where the novel items are the first statutory-to-adjusted reconciliation without large impairments, cash conversion delivered inside 70% to 80%, the dividend decision, FY2027 commentary and possibly a dated margin target under the new Chair and CFO (well founded on the H1 evidence, with the H2 comparator trap); (3) cohort reads that could help or hurt (discoverIE's interims in early December 2026, Volex's in November); (4) defence award flow as the UK Defence Investment Plan is published and Rolls-Royce and MBDA frameworks convert (well founded in direction, uncertain in date); (5) a DBAY move above 30% or a sale of its stake (low foundation, high impact either way). Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028): the May 2027 AGM update showing reported organic growth positive for a second half; H1 2027 interims in September 2027 with a 9%-plus margin and cash conversion back above 90%; the first dividend (consensus 2.5p); net debt toward £20m without a disposal or net cash with one; recognition of US deferred tax assets as US profitability recurs, which would be a one-off step in adjusted EPS of 15% to 20% (medium foundation, undated); Tempest and FCAS contract awards and the first SiC module production orders (low-to-medium foundation); a possible capital-markets event under the new team (not announced, plausible); the LTIP's first measurement. Months 18 to 36 (to October 2029): net cash by FY2028 on Jefferies' path; the margin approaching 10% to 11% on the remaining perimeter; bolt-on acquisitions from a repaired balance sheet; the re-emergence of corporate interest, with DBAY as the likely arbiter and the sector's precedent that cleaned-up mid-caps are sold; Kuantan's second-phase expansion and the Asia-for-Asia Suzhou business. Novelty assessment: the disposal, the dividend and the tax step are novel; the margin progression is the expected path and is already in consensus and in the price (186p against targets of 185p to 190p); the sector digest's two-step re-rating rule says the second step (clean margins) is the one still to be earned, and that is what the March 2027 results are for.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: TT Electronics plc (TTG-GB, London); a designer and manufacturer of engineered electronics for performance-critical applications in aerospace and defence, healthcare, and industrial automation and electrification, reporting from 2026 in three product-led divisions: Power (custom power conversion, magnetics and electromechanical assemblies, about two thirds aerospace and defence), EMS (high-mix low-volume electronic manufacturing services in the UK, North America, Mexico, Malaysia and China) and Components (passive and sensor components sold largely through distribution, under strategic review for possible divestment).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Volex plc (VLX LN; the UK listed power and data connectivity and harness manufacturer that approached TT Electronics in November 2024 and whose underlying operating margin rose from 2.8% in FY2017 to 10.2% in FY2026 per its annual reports); discoverIE Group plc (DSCV LN; the UK listed design-and-manufacture components group with a 13.8% FY2026 operating margin and a published 17% medium-term target, a low-operating-leverage comparator); XP Power Ltd (XPP LN; the UK listed power converter maker whose H1 2026 adjusted operating margin recovered to 7.9% from 4.3% after closing its China plant and moving production to Vietnam, per its interim results of 4 August 2026); Cicor Technologies Ltd (CICN SW; the Swiss listed EMS group that bought three TT Electronics sites in Q1 2024 and made a recommended offer for TT Electronics in October 2025 that lapsed in January 2026); Solid State plc (SOLI LN; a UK listed defence-weighted electronics group whose operating margin moved from 10.8% to 4.8% to 6.2% across FY2024 to FY2026 on contract timing, per its annual reports); Kitron ASA and Scanfil Oyj (Nordic listed EMS groups with European defence and industrial exposure, as scale comparators). Inputs: What is the split of fixed and variable costs in a high-mix low-volume EMS and custom power manufacturer, and specifically at TT Electronics, given a reported gross margin of 22.6% in FY2025 and 25.3% in H1 2026 (FactSet basis) and an adjusted operating margin of 7.7% and 8.1%? How did TT Electronics' adjusted operating margin move with volume in the 2021 to 2022 upturn (revenue £476m to £617m) and the 2023 to 2025 downturn (£614m to £481m), by region and by site where disclosed, and how much of the 2024 North American loss was under-absorption versus scrap, rework and inventory write-downs? What did TT Electronics' cost programmes deliver against what was promised and on what timetable: the 2020 to 2023 footprint consolidations (Covina, Lutterworth, Medina), Project Dynamo (announced 2024 with a net benefit target later raised to £17m per Deutsche Bank Research, December 2024), the Plano closure (completed late 2025), the Mexicali closure (H1 2026), and the February 2026 cost-reduction programme guided at £3m net in 2026 and over £6m annualised from 2027 (interim results, 2 September 2026)? Which cost lines proved cuttable and which did not, and what did each site closure cost in cash, inventory write-offs and customer disruption? What is the incremental operating margin on the Kuantan, Malaysia site as transferred volumes ramp, and on the Cleveland, Ohio site, which management said in September 2026 "has existing capacity to handle increased demand"? How much further footprint consolidation is realistic across TT Electronics' eight UK manufacturing sites and its North American sites, and what have peers (Volex consolidating into five Centres of Excellence; XP Power's Vietnam move; discoverIE's £6.6m footprint savings) delivered from similar moves, with timing and one-off costs? How large are central and divisional overheads after the April 2026 move from three regions to three product divisions, and what is the realistic further saving without cutting engineering, which management has said it will preserve? What is the operating leverage of the Components division specifically, and how would the group's fixed-cost base and margin volatility change if that division were sold? How have Volex, XP Power, Cicor, Kitron and Scanfil margins behaved through the same 2023 to 2025 destocking cycle and the 2026 recovery, and what incremental margins did they report on recovering volume? Focus: How much of TT Electronics' margin recovery from 7.7% toward management's undated 12% target can come from fixed-cost absorption on volume and from identified cost actions, over what timetable, and with what evidence from the company's own history and from peers that the North American and Malaysian sites can hold their new cost structure through a full cycle.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Volex plc (VLX LN; a contrasting pricing model, with copper indexation clauses and a reported practice of aggressive bidding followed by renewal pricing, per expert commentary in 2025); discoverIE Group plc (DSCV LN; the closest design-in pricing comparator, whose components are a small share of customer system cost and which reported organic gross margin improvement from value-based pricing in FY2026); XP Power Ltd (XPP LN; reported a 45.9% adjusted gross margin in H1 2026 with a stated pricing contribution on extended lead times); Solid State plc (SOLI LN; reported passing on tariffs with about 10 basis points of gross margin erosion in FY2026); Cicor Technologies Ltd (CICN SW; European EMS pricing behaviour and the margins achieved at the former TT Electronics sites after the 2024 acquisition); Senior plc (SNR LN; a UK aerospace supplier whose 2025 results referred to renegotiated pricing and commercial settlements); Amphenol and TE Connectivity (listed first-tier connector makers, for the price premium of first-tier over second-tier suppliers). Operating conditions: a distributor and OEM destocking downturn with falling volume (2023 to 2025); an early restocking upturn with lead times extending (2021 to 2022 and 2026); rapid component input inflation and shortages (the 2021 to 2022 semiconductor shortage and the 2025 to 2026 memory and PCB price rises); a new tariff regime with customer co-terms (United States tariffs from 2025, including a stated 24% duty on Malaysian imports referenced by management in September 2025); sterling appreciation against the dollar and Asian currencies with 79% of sales outside sterling; a defence procurement pause or award delay; a site closure or transfer generating last-time-buy orders (Plano, 2025) or safety-stock builds (Suzhou to Kuantan, 2026); multi-year fixed-price OEM contracts versus indexed contracts. Inputs: What price increases has TT Electronics achieved and held, and where has it given price back, across Power, EMS and Components, since 2021, and what is the evidence behind management's statements that it recovered about £40m of input inflation through contractual adjustments and surcharges (annual report 2022) and that it renegotiated underperforming legacy European contracts (annual report 2025)? What share of TT Electronics' revenue is on indexed contracts with pass-through clauses, what share is on multi-year fixed-price contracts, and what are the lags (industry commentary cites 80% to 90% pass-through with a three-to-six-month lag in some contracts and six to twelve months in short-cycle fixed-price work)? How do tariff co-terms work in TT Electronics' customer contracts and did they protect margin in 2025 and 2026, and did they cost volume (Asia organic revenue fell 9.2% in FY2025 on order deferrals, per the full-year results of 25 March 2026)? What pricing power exists in the aftermarket and in last-time-buy situations (the Plano closure generated about £3.5m of last-time-buy operating profit in H2 2025, per the full-year results), and in sole-source designed-in defence programmes at renewal? Do customers, distributors and competitors describe TT Electronics as a price-taker or a price-setter in each division, and what is the switching cost in time and money for a customer to requalify an alternative supplier? How concentrated are TT Electronics' customers (no customer above 10% of revenue; the top 40 about 58%, per company disclosures) and what payment-term and inventory-hub demands are OEMs making? What have Volex, discoverIE, XP Power and Solid State said in 2025 and 2026 about their ability to raise prices in the restocking upturn, and did their reported price contributions hold as input costs moved? What price erosion is normal at renewal for second-tier EMS and power suppliers against first-tier suppliers, and does TT Electronics' design-in position in Power exempt it? Focus: Whether TT Electronics has real pricing power beyond cost pass-through in its Power division and in its qualified EMS work, how it behaves when input costs rise and when volume falls, and how much of the group's forecast margin progression can be attributed to price rather than to cost and volume.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The company is at the start of a turnaround in which several dynamics feed on themselves in both directions: restored site profitability and a growing order book raise utilisation and cash, which lower interest and could unlock recognition of large unrecognised tax losses, while recovering volume absorbs working capital and lengthening component lead times pull orders forward; the model needs to know which loops are operating and for how long. Peers: Volex plc (VLX LN; the precedent for a self-help margin recovery compounding into growth, and for cash conversion falling to 63.9% in FY2026 as growth absorbed inventory, per its annual report); discoverIE Group plc (DSCV LN; the precedent for order growth leading revenue with a stated six-month lag and a correlation coefficient of 0.72 since 2017, per Deutsche Bank Research, July 2026); XP Power Ltd (XPP LN; whose Q3 2026 trading update of 20 October 2026 said early customer bookings inflated orders as lead times extended); Solid State plc (SOLI LN; the precedent for defence order lumpiness and for deliberate inventory builds ahead of programme deliveries); Cicor Technologies Ltd (CICN SW; what happened to margins, customer retention and working capital at the three former TT Electronics sites after acquisition in 2024). Inputs: Does a profitable, under-utilised site (Cleveland, Ohio, which returned to consistent profitability in H1 2026 per the interim results) win more work because it is profitable and reliable, and did the three new customers and six product wins it secured in H2 2025 (per the full-year results) lead to further wins in 2026? Does the restocking cycle in electronic components pull orders forward as lead times extend, and does that reverse when lead times normalise, in TT Electronics' distribution channel and in its EMS order book? How does TT Electronics' working capital behave as volume recovers (H1 2026 cash conversion was 42% on a £12.7m working-capital build, per the interim results) and how long did peers take to return to their target conversion after a recovery began? Is there a reinforcing loop between deleveraging, lower finance costs (adjusted net finance expense fell to £2.7m in H1 2026 from £4.5m, per the interim results), dividend reinstatement and the ability to invest in capacity and sales resources? Under what conditions do companies with large unrecognised deferred tax assets (TT Electronics disclosed £282.4m of gross unrecognised tax losses at end-2025 in its annual report) recognise them, what accounting evidence of future profitability is required, and how did peers and UK industrial companies that recognised such assets treat the step in their adjusted earnings? Does defence programme revenue in Power compound through platform lifecycles (follow-on orders, aftermarket, next-generation variants) once a supplier is designed in, as the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement and MBDA letter of intent announced in September 2026 suggest? Which balancing loops are visible: customers extending payment terms, distributors withholding design-in discounts, wage and compliance inflation at regional sites, the removal of Plano last-time-buy profit from the 2026 comparator, and the loss of scale in procurement as volume concentrates in fewer sites? Focus: Which of the loops now at work in TT Electronics (utilisation to wins to margin; lead times to orders; growth to working capital; deleveraging to earnings; profitability to tax recognition) are reinforcing and which are balancing over the next 18 months to four years, with dated precedents for how far and how long each ran at the company and at peers.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The gap between adjusted operating profit and adjusted earnings per share at TT Electronics is unusually wide and moving (an adjusted effective tax rate of 57.1% in FY2025 and 36.3% in H1 2026 against a stated normalised rate in the mid 20s, restructuring cash outside adjusted profit, a falling interest line, a possible divestment of Components and a dividend decision due in March 2027), so the inputs to the earnings-per-share bridge need separate research from the operating forecast. Peers: XP Power Ltd (XPP LN; a UK power electronics company that has carried unrecognised tax losses and legal settlement cash flows through its adjusted earnings); Volex plc (VLX LN; whose Turkish tax law change in December 2025 produced a $4.2m adjusting charge, per its annual report, and whose adjusted and statutory earnings reconcile with disclosed acquired-intangible amortisation); discoverIE Group plc (DSCV LN; a comparator for interest-rate sensitivity, with its finance director stating in 2026 that a 1% rate cut adds about 3% to earnings per share, and for dividend cover policy); Solid State plc (SOLI LN; a comparator for the treatment of capitalised development costs and acquired-intangible amortisation in adjusted earnings); Senior plc (SNR LN; a UK aerospace supplier with a similar adjusted-to-reported gap and a disclosed currency sensitivity per 10 cents on GBP/USD). Inputs: The adjusted effective tax rate: what are the components of TT Electronics' FY2025 adjusted tax charge of £13.9m (full-year results, 25 March 2026) and its guided mid-to-high 30s% rate for 2026 (H1 2026 slides, 2 September 2026); what are the jurisdictions and expiry profiles of the £282.4m of gross unrecognised losses (£225.1m in the United States, per the 2025 annual report); what cash tax has the group actually paid in each of the last five years against its P&L charge; and what conditions, evidence and timing would allow recognition of United States deferred tax assets? Net finance cost: the terms, maturities and rates of the £75m of fixed-rate private-placement notes (maturing 2028 and 2031, average 3.65%, per the 2025 annual report) and the £105m revolving credit facility extended to June 2028, lease interest, pension interest, and the interest income on cash. Adjusting items and the statutory-to-adjusted bridge: the history of restructuring charges and their cash timing (£15.2m charged in 2025 with £7.9m cash impact, per the annual report; about £3m of cost-programme spend in H1 2026), acquired-intangible amortisation (£2.6m in 2025), impairments (£41.4m in 2025, £3.8m at Mexicali in H1 2026), management transition costs, and what the company and its auditor Deloitte say about the controls remediation after the £5.7m prior-period restatement disclosed in 2025. Share count: diluted shares (FactSet shows 186.4m diluted against 178.3m basic at end-2025), LTIP and buyout awards granted in 2026 (723,938 conditional shares to the chief executive on 2 April 2026 and 576,419 LTIP plus 472,304 buyout shares to the chief financial officer on 29 June 2026, per regulatory filings), employee share purchases and any stated buyback policy. Dividends: the policy under review with an update promised for March 2027 (interim results, 2 September 2026), historical cover and payout, and what peers reinstated after a suspension. Working capital and free cash flow: the H2 2026 working-capital unwind implied by the 70% to 80% cash conversion guidance, inventory days, receivable days and payable days by division, and capital expenditure plans including Kuantan and Bedlington. The Components divestment: the division's standalone revenue, profit, net assets, working capital and tax basis; the indicative multiples for comparable component and sensor businesses (Jefferies Research of 1 October 2026 applied 5.0x EV/EBITA in its sum of the parts); separation costs and stranded central costs; and the accounting treatment of a disposal gain or loss. Currency: the disclosed sensitivities (£0.1m of operating profit per 1 cent on GBP/USD and per RMB 0.1, per the H1 2026 slides), the hedging policy (75% of expected exposures over 12 months, 50% over 12 to 24 months, per the 2025 annual report) and how translation moved reported revenue and profit in each of the last five years. Focus: How much of the growth in TT Electronics' adjusted earnings per share over the next 18 months to four years will come from items below operating profit (tax rate normalisation or deferred tax recognition, falling finance costs, the end of restructuring charges, a disposal, share count) as opposed to operating profit, and what the sell-side has assumed for each, so that the earnings-per-share forecast can be built from explicit inputs rather than inherited from consensus.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 12,583 words · written 23 October 2026, 11:25 to 11:43 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
TT Electronics (TTG-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Written 2026-10-22 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets (predictability of the forecasts; guidance, consensus and narrative).

  • Basis. All profit figures are on the company's adjusted basis (adjusted operating profit before acquired-intangible amortisation, restructuring, impairments and transition costs), which is also the basis the four brokers submit to FactSet. FactSet's own history lines (operating EBITDA £47.5m, adjusted EBIT £32.8m, net interest £8.8m for FY2025) sit on a different basis and are used only for trends. EPS is diluted adjusted, the consensus basis. Money in GBP millions; per-share figures in pence.
