Financial forecasts

C609 questions
P-0164P-0262P-0358P-0450P-0558P-0655P-0860P-0962P-1060

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts for TT Electronics over the next 18 months to four years describe a repair whose mechanics are unusually legible: operating leverage running upward into a fixed base that has already been cut, a £6m cost programme that is finished and will be eaten by inflation within two years, a profit engine (Power) compounding on defence platforms while the two other divisions recover from self-inflicted lows, an interest line that falls steadily and a tax rate that falls sharply, and a cash flow that becomes profit-led for the first time in the company's recent history but lags the P&L because growth absorbs working capital. The five drivers that produce the difference from the past (volume absorption at Cleveland and Kuantan, the programme, Power phasing and mix, the tax rate, the interest line) are internal, dated and mostly already visible in the H1 2026 numbers, which is why the forecast for the next two halves deserves high weight and should be leaned above consensus on operating profit (£41.5m and £47.0m against £40.0m and £45.0m) and below it on FY2027 EPS (15.5p against 15.9p, because consensus has lowered the tax rate faster than the company has guided). From FY2028 the reinforcing loops slow, the balancing loops persist, and the base case flattens into a 10% to 10.5% margin growing 3.5% a year with a dividend and net cash: a cleaned-up mid-cap whose end state, on the sector's own precedent and with a 29% blockholder, is more likely a sale than a compounding path. The Components disposal is the one dated event that changes the shape (margin plus 100bp, net cash a year earlier, a narrower cone, EPS-neutral) and should be carried as a scenario; recognition of the US tax assets is the one undated event that changes EPS discontinuously (plus 15% to 20%) and should be carried as upside. Pricing contributes almost nothing to margin and about a point a year to revenue. The cone over 18 months is moderate and skewed positive on profit; over four years it is wide and symmetrical on earnings and skewed positive on quality. For an investment case, the forecasts are supportive: they are more likely to be beaten than missed on profit for the next two results, they are honestly built from identified items rather than from demand, and their main risk is the one thing they cannot control, the volume arriving on the customers' schedule without execution cost.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C64

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is the single largest operating driver of the difference between the next four years' financials and the last six, and for the first time since FY2022 it runs upward; it will add roughly 1.5 to 2.0 points to the adjusted operating margin between FY2025 and FY2028 on about 9% cumulative revenue growth, which is more than the cost programme (about 1.2 points gross, under 1 point net of inflation) and far more than price. The mechanism is symmetrical and has been demonstrated in both directions, so it is also the main reason the cone of outcomes is wide: a 5% revenue shortfall against my base in FY2027 takes the margin from 9.3% to about 8.3% and EPS from 15.5p to about 12.5p.

The structure, sized. Cost of sales is 75% to 78% of revenue, of which materials (about 55% to 60% of revenue) are fully variable and direct labour and factory overhead (about 18% to 20% of revenue) are semi-fixed within a half-year; selling, general and administrative cost is 14% to 16% of revenue and almost entirely fixed; adjusted central costs are £7.5m to £7.7m a year (£3.7m in H1 2026). On that structure, the drop-through on a marginal pound of revenue into an existing site with spare capacity is 30% to 40% (the gross margin of 22% to 25% plus the share of factory overhead that does not move), and the drop-through on a pound of revenue that requires new capacity or new programmes at introductory pricing is nearer 15% to 20%. The evidence that the real mechanism sits in that range: Deutsche Bank's calculation that the September 2024 North American warning implied an 88% drop-through (that figure included scrap, rework and inventory write-downs, so it is the ceiling, not the norm); Jefferies' estimate of a 50% to 60% drop-through at XP Power on recovering volume; consensus for TT itself embedding 23% incremental EBIT margins for FY2027 and FY2028 (£5m of EBIT per £21m to £22m of revenue); and TT's own FY2022 upturn, in which revenue rose 29.6% but adjusted operating profit rose only 19%, because £28m of the £40m of recovered inflation was zero-margin pass-through and price lagged cost. I use 25% to 30% as the blended drop-through on volume over the forecast period: higher than consensus for FY2027, when the volume lands in sites (Cleveland, Kuantan) that already carry the fixed cost, and at or below consensus from FY2028, when wage, compliance and energy inflation have to be covered.

What it does to each line item, against the last four to six years.

  • Revenue: operating leverage does not drive revenue, but the removal of fixed loss-making capacity (Plano, Mexicali, the Suzhou transfer) has depressed reported revenue by about £25m to £30m across FY2025 and H1 2026 while raising margin; that is the opposite of the FY2022 to FY2024 pattern, in which revenue was held up by pass-through and low-margin sites while margin fell. From H2 2026 the two effects align: revenue grows and the fixed base is already lean.
  • Gross margin: 24.0% (FY2019), 21.6% (FY2022, pass-through dilution), 20.7% (FY2024, under-absorption plus scrap), 22.6% (FY2025), 25.3% in H1 2026 on FactSet's basis. I expect 25% to 26% from H2 2026 and 26% to 27% by FY2028: the H1 2026 jump of 620bp on H1 2025 was the removal of Plano's losses and Cleveland's scrap from cost of sales, not volume, so volume absorption is still to come. The gross margin will give back 30bp to 60bp in any half in which input inflation runs ahead of indexed recovery (C-05).
  • Operating margin: 7.3% (FY2019), 6.3% to 6.4% (FY2020 to FY2022 on FactSet's basis; 7.6% company basis in FY2022), 8.6% (FY2023 company), 7.1% (FY2024), 7.7% (FY2025), 8.1% (H1 2026). My path is 8.6%, 9.3%, 9.8%, 10.0%, 10.2% for FY2026 to FY2030. The bridge from 7.7% to 9.8% by FY2028 (210bp) decomposes as: volume absorption on about £43m of revenue growth at 25% to 30% drop-through, about £11m to £13m, or 180bp to 220bp gross; the net cost programme, £6m or about 120bp; the loss of Plano's £3.5m last-time-buy profit, minus 70bp; wage, compliance and energy inflation on the fixed base, minus £3m to £4m a year cumulatively, about minus 150bp over three years; mix toward Power, plus 30bp to 40bp; central costs flat in pounds, plus 20bp of leverage. The sum is 210bp to 260bp, which is why I sit above consensus on FY2027 and FY2028 EBIT (£47.0m and £51.5m against £45.0m and £50.0m) but not far above.
  • EBITDA: rises faster than EBIT in pounds (D&A rises from £12.6m to £13.5m as capex doubles from £8m to £13m), so the EBITDA margin moves from 10.3% to 12.4% by FY2028, a 210bp move that matches EBIT's.
  • EPS: operating leverage is amplified at the EPS line by the fall in the tax rate and the interest line (P-02, P-08); of the 11p of EPS growth between FY2025 and FY2028 in my base, about 6p is operating profit, about 3.5p is tax and about 1p is interest.
  • Cash: the same leverage runs through working capital in the opposite direction (P-05): each £1 of revenue growth absorbs about 16p of working capital, so FY2027's £24m of revenue growth costs about £4m to £6m of cash, and conversion in the growth years will be 85% to 95%, not the 117% to 150% of the shrinking years.

Predictability and the cone. Operating leverage makes the EBIT forecast more sensitive to volume than any other input: plus or minus 5% on FY2027 revenue (£25m) moves adjusted operating profit by about £6m to £8m, or 13% to 17%, and EPS by about 3p either way. The asymmetry the Consumer should carry is that the downside drop-through is higher than the upside drop-through, because a volume shortfall in a half-year hits sites whose cost cannot be cut inside the half (the 2024 precedent), whereas a volume surge above capacity attracts overtime, expediting and introductory pricing. The Components disposal narrows the cone by removing the division with the shortest visibility (ten to twelve weeks) and the highest gearing (Jefferies: "high operational gearing because of its transactional sales model"); it would take about £75m to £80m of revenue out with only £2m to £3m of profit.

Structural or cyclical. The leverage itself is structural (a high-mix, low-volume manufacturer with eight UK sites and revenue per site in the bottom quartile cannot be made asset-light), but its direction is cyclical and its magnitude has been reduced structurally by the removal of three sites and about 500 heads since 2024. Peers: discoverIE has low operating leverage by design (design-and-manufacture, 30 businesses, gross margin near 40%) and its margin held at 13.8% through the destocking; Volex, Kitron and Scanfil are volume-sensitive contract manufacturers whose margins moved 100bp to 300bp with utilisation; Solid State's margin swung from 10.8% to 4.8% to 6.2% on award timing alone. TT sits with the contract manufacturers, not with discoverIE, and will not earn discoverIE's multiple until the perimeter is narrower.

What would change the judgement: a second half of 2026 in which revenue grows but the margin does not reach 9% (the leverage is weaker than I think, or price is being given to win volume); a cash-conversion print below 70% for FY2026 (the leverage is running through working capital harder than I think); or evidence at Kuantan or Cleveland of the 2024 pattern (volume arriving with scrap and rework). The indicator to watch at the March 2027 results is the H2 2026 EMS margin: above 8.5% confirms the mechanism; below 7.5% says the leverage is being absorbed by execution cost.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C62

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: financial leverage is low, is falling to zero within about two years, and over the forecast period it contributes a small, steady and highly predictable lift to EPS (about 1p, or roughly 5% of FY2028 EPS, from a net finance cost falling from about £8m in FY2025 to about £3m by FY2030) rather than any amplification of risk; the balance sheet moves from £50m of net debt ex leases to roughly net cash by the end of FY2028 without a disposal and by the end of FY2027 with one, and the question the Consumer should carry is not whether the balance sheet is safe but what management does with the surplus from FY2028, because a company with net cash, no acquisition record and a 29% blockholder is a company whose end state is decided by others.

The debt and its cost, and why the interest line is nearly fixed. £75m of private-placement notes at an average 3.65% (two tranches of £37.5m maturing December 2028 and December 2031), a £105m revolving credit facility to June 2028 with £8.3m drawn at floating rates, lease liabilities of £14.4m carrying about £0.6m of interest, a small pension interest credit that is disappearing as the UK scheme buys out, and negligible interest income. The adjusted net finance expense was £2.7m in H1 2026 against £4.5m in H1 2025, so the FY2026 run rate is about £5.5m, down from about £8m in FY2025 (FactSet's £8.8m includes items outside the adjusted line). Because 84% of borrowings are fixed, the company says a 1% move in rates changes profit after tax by only £0.1m; discoverIE, by contrast, gains about 3% of EPS from a 1% rate cut. The consequence for the model is that the finance line is the most predictable input in the forecast: £5.5m in FY2026, £5.2m in FY2027 (RCF undrawn, cash builds), £4.8m in FY2028, £3.8m in FY2029 after the December 2028 tranche is repaid from cash, £3.2m in FY2030. Consensus implies a higher line (pre-tax profit of £33.7m on EBIT of £40.0m in FY2026 implies £6.3m; £6.0m in FY2027), which is one of the two places where my EPS sits above consensus for FY2026.

The balance sheet path. Net debt ex leases of £50.3m at FY2025 and £52.0m at H1 2026 (covenant leverage 1.1x against a 3.0x limit; FactSet's definition including leases gives £68m, 1.5x). On my free cash flow (£8.5m FY2026 after about £6m of restructuring cash and a £10m working-capital build; £20m FY2027; £28m FY2028; £31m FY2029) and dividends paid of £0.7m, £2.9m, £5.4m and £7.3m, net debt ex leases falls to about £42m at FY2026 (Jefferies £43.2m), £24m at FY2027, about zero at FY2028, net cash of about £23m at FY2029 and £48m at FY2030. Covenant leverage falls below 1.0x in FY2026 and below 0.5x in FY2027. With a Components disposal at £25m to £35m net in 2027, net cash arrives a year earlier. The December 2028 maturities (the first note tranche and the RCF) are funded from cash on this path and should be refinanced on better terms, which is a further £0.5m to £1.0m of interest saving from FY2029 that I have included.

EPS amplification, sized honestly. From FY2025 to FY2028 the finance line falls by about £3m; after tax at about 29% that is £2.1m of net income, or about 1.1p of EPS, roughly 10% of the 11p of EPS growth in the period. It is small because the balance sheet was never highly geared in absolute terms (the risk in 2024 was covenant headroom on a collapsing EBITDA, not the quantum of debt), and because the fixed-rate notes cannot be prepaid cheaply. The deleveraging loop (P-05) therefore matters less through interest than through what it permits: a dividend (consensus 2.5p for FY2027, about £4.5m), growth capex (guided £10m to £13m in FY2026, up from £8m) and, from FY2028, either bolt-on acquisitions or a cash pile that invites a bidder.

Risk, in both directions. The downside case for the balance sheet is not solvency; it is a repeat of H1 2026, in which a working-capital build on flat revenue took conversion to 42% and free cash flow to nil. A £15m shortfall against my FY2026 cash flow would leave covenant leverage at about 1.4x, still comfortable; a correlated execution failure (Kuantan plus Cleveland, margin back toward 7%) would take leverage to about 1.8x to 2.0x in FY2027, uncomfortable but inside covenants, and would defer the dividend. The upside case is that a net-cash balance sheet with a 10% margin and a 4% growth rate is worth more to a strategic or private buyer than to the public market at 10x earnings, which is why the sector digest's rule that cleaned-up mid-caps here end in a sale applies with more force each year the cash builds. DBAY at 29.04% decides that outcome.

Against the last four to six years: net debt ex leases went £126m (FY2023), £80m (FY2024), £50m (FY2025); leverage 1.8x to 2.4x at the peaks, with a going-concern material uncertainty disclosed in 2024, covenants temporarily relaxed and dividends suspended. The next four years invert that: a shrinking interest line, no covenant question, a dividend from 2027, and surplus capital by 2028. The balance sheet moves from being the constraint on the equity to being an unanswered question about capital allocation.

P-03

Scope for cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when

C58

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement: the cost programme that is announced and funded (£3m net in FY2026, over £6m annualised from FY2027) is almost entirely banked and will reach the operating-profit line on schedule, but the Consumer should carry it as the last of the announced savings and should net it against £3m to £4m a year of wage, compliance and energy inflation on a fixed base of about £110m to £120m, so that the net contribution of cost action to the margin between FY2025 and FY2028 is about 50bp to 80bp, not the 120bp the headline implies; the larger cost effect over the period is not a programme at all but the removal of loss-making capacity already completed (Plano £5.7m to £6m a year, Cleveland's scrap and rework, Mexicali), which is worth about 200bp of margin against the FY2024 base and is already in the H1 2026 run rate.

What is in hand, and its timing. (1) The February 2026 administrative programme: £3m net in FY2026 (about half in H1, the rest in H2), rising to over £6m annualised from FY2027, so FY2027 gains a further £3m and FY2028 nothing further; restructuring cost of about £3m was taken within or alongside H1 2026 adjusted profit and the programme is "substantially complete". The savings are "strictly administrative" (duplicated regional layers removed by the move to product divisions) and management has said engineering will be preserved, so there is no revenue cost. I take the £6m at face value because it is small, specific and already visible in central and divisional overhead. (2) Site removals: Plano's £5.7m to £6m annual loss gone from the base from Q4 2025; Cleveland's turnaround (productivity 57% to 81%, rework 12% to 5%) worth perhaps £4m to £6m a year against the FY2024 loss; Mexicali closed in H1 2026 at £1.0m of cash cost and £3.8m of impairment. These show up as the EMS margin moving from 4.9% (FY2025) to 7.9% (H1 2026) and Components from a loss to 2.7%. (3) Project Dynamo's earlier procurement and freight consolidation (Deutsche Bank: £6m of SG&A savings achieved by December 2024 against a £17m net target later overtaken by events), which is in the FY2025 base.

What is not in hand, and why I do not model it. Further footprint consolidation: eight UK manufacturing sites and revenue per site in the bottom quartile suggest one or two more closures could release £2m to £4m (hypothesis), but Jefferies' record of management's view in April 2024 is that UK rationalisation is "extremely difficult" because of customer and regulatory approvals, each prior closure has cost £4m to £7m in cash and inventory write-offs (Plano £7.0m, of which £4.8m inventory), and the aerospace requalification cycle of 18 to 24 months means a transfer produces double-running cost before any saving. I therefore carry zero further footprint savings in the base and treat them as FY2028-plus upside. Central costs after a Components disposal: Jefferies holds central costs flat at £7.7m in FY2027; a disposal would leave £1m to £2m of stranded cost that the company would need to remove, which I net inside the disposal scenario rather than the base. Engineering and R&D (3.8% of sales against XP Power's 12%) are not cuttable and may need to rise; I carry them flat as a percentage.

The balancing item: inflation on the fixed base. UK salaries rose 3.3% on average in 2025 and 2.5% at the executive level in 2026; US and Malaysian wage inflation is similar or higher; EO 14415 and NIST SP 800-171 add compliance cost at Kansas City and Minneapolis that the H1 2026 report itself flags could offset part of the programme; energy at the furnace-intensive sites is exposed. On a fixed cost base (direct labour, factory overhead and SG&A) of roughly £110m to £120m, 3% is £3.5m a year. The programme's £6m therefore covers about two years of inflation and no more. This is the main reason my FY2028 margin (9.8%) is only modestly above consensus (9.5%) despite higher volume: consensus's 23% incremental margins already assume no cost growth, and I do not think that holds beyond FY2027.

When it reaches the line items. Operating profit: £1.5m in H1 2026 (done), £1.5m in H2 2026, £3m more across FY2027, nothing more thereafter; against this, minus £3m to £4m a year of inflation from FY2027. Cash: restructuring cash of about £6m in FY2026 (£3.8m paid in H1, the balance in H2 for Mexicali and the programme), about £2m in FY2027, then near zero; this sits outside adjusted profit and inside free cash flow, and is why FY2026 free cash flow is only about £8m to £9m. Statutory profit: the FY2026 statutory-to-adjusted gap should be the smallest since 2022 (about £10m to £12m: £3.8m Mexicali impairment, £2.6m acquired-intangible amortisation, £3m to £4m of restructuring and programme cost), against £65.4m in FY2025 and £60.6m in FY2024; that is the "first clean margins" test the sector digest sets.

Peer precedents, briefly. Volex released 50bp by consolidating three sites and took nine years to go from 2.8% to 10.2%; XP Power's Vietnam move and China closure took the margin from 4.3% to 7.9% in a year but required a full plant relocation; discoverIE extracted £4.4m to £6.6m from office and warehouse consolidation; Cicor doubled the EBITDA margin at the three sites it bought from TT within a year, which says the remaining TT estate is run below its potential and is the strongest argument that more cost can come out, but under an owner willing to take the disruption. The common lesson is that administrative programmes deliver on time and footprint programmes deliver late and at a cash cost; TT's current programme is the former.

Direction and skew: the cost contribution is well founded and low variance for FY2026 and FY2027; the skew from FY2028 is modestly negative (inflation outruns a finished programme) unless the disposal or a further site exit is executed.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

C50

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: price will contribute about 1 to 1.5 points a year to reported revenue growth over the forecast period and roughly nothing to the margin percentage, because TT's pricing power is of the cost-recovery kind (indexed and negotiated pass-through that protects gross profit in pounds and dilutes the percentage) in about three quarters of the business, is real but small in the designed-in Power work (perhaps 1 to 2 points a year at contract renewal in Power, worth £2m to £4m of profit spread over three years), and is negative at the commodity edge (annual price-downs of 1% to 2% demanded by large OEMs in standard EMS and Components); the net of these is a margin contribution from price of plus 20bp to plus 40bp over four years, which the Consumer should treat as inside the noise of the volume and cost drivers rather than as a lever. The one period in which price matters more is a half-year of fast input inflation, when the three-to-six-month recovery lag costs 30bp to 60bp of gross margin before it is clawed back; the memory and PCB price rises now under way make H2 2026 or H1 2027 a candidate for exactly that.

The evidence, weighed. The best-documented episode is 2022: about £40m of input inflation was fully recovered, but Jefferies' decomposition shows £28m of that was zero-margin pass-through of raw materials and the organic growth of 14% included only 3 points of price against 11 of volume; pass-through revenue then ran £19.9m in 2023, £5.3m in 2024 and nil in 2025, so the 2023 to 2025 revenue decline overstates the volume decline by roughly 4 points and the margin recovery is partly the arithmetic of pass-through leaving the denominator. The second episode is Europe in FY2025: adjusted operating profit up 16.9% to £22.1m and the margin up 240bp to 15.3% on falling revenue, attributed partly to repricing underperforming legacy contracts; that is genuine margin pricing, but it is a one-time correction of mispriced contracts, not a repeatable annual increase, and most of it is in the FY2025 base. The third is Plano's last-time buys: £3.5m of H2 2025 profit at premium prices from distributors and end customers, proof of sole-source pricing power at end of life and also proof that such pricing is non-repeating. The fourth is the H1 2026 Components recovery, attributed partly to "strategic self-help pricing actions" alongside stronger distributor markets, which is small in pounds (the division made £1.0m). Against these, the limits: the contract architecture in EMS is multi-year with negotiated increases, so recovery lags six to twelve months in short-cycle fixed-price work; tariff co-terms pass duty to the customer and protect margin but cost volume (Asia organic revenue fell 9.2% in FY2025 on order deferrals, and the 24% Malaysian duty makes customers "reconsider where to place business"); Deutsche Bank's initiation notes that large OEMs require annual price-down concessions of 1% to 2% to maintain qualified-supplier status; and Volex's practice of bidding low and recovering at renewal sets the market's tolerance for price aggression in standard work.

By division, for the model. Power (43% of revenue, about 16% margin): sole-source, designed-in, 15-to-30-year platforms, requalification 18 to 24 months and $0.5m to $2m per component (the Medtronic director's figures for medical, which apply at least as strongly to defence); real price of 1 to 2 points a year is achievable at renewal and on new programmes, and the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement and MBDA letter of intent were won on capability, not price. The H1 2026 Power margin slip to 14.2% was attributed to delayed finalisation of customer agreements; the pricing report is right that the terms of those agreements are undisclosed and that a failure to restore Power above 15% in H2 2026 would be the first sign that price is being traded for volume. EMS (41%, 5% to 8%): cost-plus or negotiated multi-year; price recovers cost with a lag and does not add margin; Kitron's cost-plus model shows the same pattern at a larger peer (stable 9.5% margin, absolute profit protected, little leverage from price). Components (16%, 2% to 3%): price-taker through distribution, sub-20% second-source allocations; whatever pricing is achieved is given back at the next downturn. Against the sector digest's rule that only designed-in parts reprice while standard parts pass cost on late, TT's mix means about 40% to 45% of revenue can reprice and 55% to 60% passes through.

What it does to the line items. Revenue: about 1 point of price in FY2026 (mostly carry-over), 1 to 1.5 points in FY2027 and FY2028 as Power contracts renew and input inflation is recovered, so of my 5.0% and 4.0% growth, roughly 3.5 to 4 and 2.5 to 3 points are volume. Gross margin: roughly neutral over a year, minus 30bp to 60bp in a half of fast input inflation, recovered two quarters later; I have not put an explicit inflation dip into the half-year inputs because the timing is unknowable, but the Consumer should regard H1 2027 as the half most at risk of it. Operating margin: plus 10bp a year from Power mix and renewal pricing, offset by minus 5bp to 10bp from price-downs in standard work. The honest conclusion is that TT's forecast margin progression is a cost-and-volume story to which price contributes almost nothing, which is also what the pricing report concludes ("solely cost-out and volume driven rather than structurally enhanced by premium pricing") and what discoverIE's 13.8% margin against TT's 7.7% says about the difference between a design-in model and a mixed one.

Peers and persistence. discoverIE raises prices 3% to 5% a year because its parts are 3% to 5% of system cost; XP Power's 45.9% gross margin in H1 2026 (+450bp) came from "pricing and sales mix" on extended lead times, a restocking-upturn effect that TT's Components division will share in a smaller way for two to three halves; Solid State passed tariffs through with 10bp of gross-margin dilution; Senior's aerospace margin rose 190bp on pricing and settlements, which shows what a qualified aerospace supplier can do at renewal and is the nearest precedent for TT's Power division. TT's pricing power is persistent where it exists (Power) and structurally absent where it does not; the Components disposal would raise the repricing share of revenue from about 45% to about 55% and make the group's pricing behave more like Senior's than like Kitron's.

Skew and indicators. The skew on price is slightly negative over twelve months (input inflation lag) and slightly positive over three years (Power renewals, disposal). Indicators: the H2 2026 Power margin against 15%; any disclosure of price concessions at renewal; the gap between Components order intake growth (+26% in H1 2026) and its revenue growth (+5.8%), which if it widens further signals pull-forward on lead times rather than price.

P-05

Reinforcing or balancing feedback loops: which are at work, and how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C58

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: four reinforcing loops and five balancing loops are at work; over the next 18 months the reinforcing loops dominate because they are mechanical, dated and already visible in the H1 2026 numbers (utilisation to wins to margin; deleveraging to interest to dividend; US profitability to tax rate to EPS; defence design-in to follow-on orders), while the balancing loops dominate the width of the cone from months 18 to 48 because they are what stops a margin recovery turning into a compounding story (growth absorbing working capital; the restocking pulse reversing; inflation eroding a finished cost programme; scale loss in procurement; OEM payment terms). The net effect on the line items is an operating-profit path that compounds at 10% to 12% a year to FY2028 and then slows to 5% to 6%, an EPS path that compounds faster (17% a year to FY2028) because two of the reinforcing loops act below operating profit, and a cash path that lags both because the first balancing loop acts on working capital.

The reinforcing loops, how each works, the evidence it is running, and how far it runs. (1) Utilisation to reliability to wins to margin, at the sites. A profitable, reliable site with spare capacity (Cleveland: productivity 81%, rework 5%, consistently profitable in H1 2026, "existing capacity to handle increased demand") wins work because customers award allocations to suppliers who deliver, and the new work drops through at 30% to 40% into fixed cost already carried. Evidence: three new customers and six products at Cleveland in H2 2025 after a three-year drought, two new EMS logos in scientific and analytical instruments in H1 2026, group book-to-bill 1.12x, the sales transformation credited by management. Precedent: Volex's margin went from 2.8% to 10.2% over nine years on exactly this loop plus footprint consolidation; the loop ran until growth outpaced the footprint and working capital intervened. How far at TT: Cleveland can absorb perhaps £15m to £25m of incremental revenue without new fixed cost, worth £4m to £8m of profit over two to three years; Kuantan similarly once at full rate. This loop is worth 100bp to 150bp of group margin between FY2026 and FY2028 and is the core of my above-consensus EBIT. (2) Deleveraging to lower interest to higher free cash flow to dividend and investment. Net debt ex leases £126m to £50m in two years; adjusted finance cost £4.5m to £2.7m half-on-half; the RCF right-sized; the dividend decision promised for March 2027; capex raised from £8m to £10m to £13m. The loop is real but small at the interest line (P-02: about 1p of EPS by FY2028) and its main effect is to make the dividend and growth capex affordable without choosing between them. It runs until net cash, at which point it becomes a capital-allocation question rather than a loop. (3) US profitability to tax rate to EPS. The adjusted tax rate is 36% to 57% because US losses earn no tax credit and US deferred tax assets are unrecognised (£225.1m of £282.4m of gross unrecognised losses, with no expiry). As US sites turn profitable (Plano gone, Cleveland profitable), two things happen in sequence: first, US profits are sheltered by brought-forward losses and no unrelieved losses arise, so the adjusted rate falls mechanically toward the high 20s without any accounting decision; second, when the five-year plan supports recognition (management said the 2026 to 2030 plan "does not yet" do so at March 2026), a credit of up to £18.7m plus £7.9m is booked outside adjusted profit and the adjusted rate settles near the 25.4% normalised rate. The company's own sensitivity: recognition would have cut the H1 2026 rate from 36.3% to 26.7% and added 0.8p to a 5.7p half. This is the largest single EPS loop in the file, worth 15% to 20% of EPS, and it depends entirely on North American profitability recurring for two to three years. I model the first step (36%, 32%, 29%, 27%, 26%) and not the second; the second is a FY2028 or FY2029 event in the base and a FY2027 event in the upside. (4) Defence design-in to platform lifecycle compounding. Once designed in, a supplier earns production, spares, upgrades and next-generation variants for 15 to 30 years at margins that hold (Renk: aftermarket at three to four times initial equipment value on land platforms; Honeywell's 250 active platforms). Evidence at TT: Boxer, Challenger 3, CV-90, Typhoon in production; the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement for all large civil engines; the MBDA letter of intent; Collins Aerospace's $10.3m classified programme; FCAS and Tempest feasibility work; Power book-to-bill 1.14x; European defence book-to-bill 135% in 2025 against a sustainable 100% to 115%. The loop runs for the life of the platforms and is the reason Power's revenue can grow 4% to 6% a year with a mid-teens margin through the forecast period; its only weakness is timing (Power's H1 2026 revenue was flat on contract finalisation delays; Solid State's revenue swung £163m to £125m to £154m on award cadence), so it compounds in the backlog before it compounds in the P&L.

The balancing loops, how each works, and its size. (1) Growth to working capital. Net working capital was 12.6% of revenue at FY2025 (a trough from a £14.8m inventory release) and 19.7% at H1 2026 (a £12.7m build, £11.7m of it inventory, to protect supply through the Kuantan transfer); the historical range is 17% to 20%; management guides 70% to 80% conversion for FY2026 and 90%-plus in the medium term. Volex's conversion fell to 63.9% as data-centre growth absorbed $55m of inventory; Cicor's free cash flow was negative CHF 11.5m in H1 2026 on a working-capital build; discoverIE's held at 92% because its model is asset-light. I model working capital at 15.5% of revenue at FY2026 and 16% thereafter, which absorbs about £10m in FY2026 and £3m to £6m a year after; that caps free cash flow conversion at about 85% to 95% in the growth years and is the reason cash lags profit. The loop would turn reinforcing (releasing cash) only in a downturn, as it did in FY2024 and FY2025, which is the sector note's warning that strong conversion with falling revenue is the signal of a weak business, not a strong one. (2) Lead times to pull-forward to air pocket. Components (ten-week lead times, three months of visibility, 17% of group revenue through distribution) is the channel that reflects the restocking pulse first: order intake +26% in H1 2026 (+37% excluding Plano) against revenue +5.8%. XP Power's 20 October update showed the mechanism at its end state (orders +93%, book-to-bill 1.67x, "early customer bookings" on extended lead times, reported revenue down, shares fell); Morgan Stanley's October survey shows 42% of distributors pre-buying (29% a quarter earlier) while semiconductor channel inventory sits at its 56-day median, which says the restocking is distributor positioning more than end demand. The sector digest dates the pulse at six to eighteen months, plateauing by Q4 2026. For the model: Components revenue growth of 5% to 8% through H1 2027 and then flat to down in H2 2027 and FY2028; a disposal removes the loop from the group entirely. If the division is not sold, FY2028 group revenue growth carries about 1 point of downside from this loop that my 4.0% does not fully reflect. (3) Inflation on the fixed base against a finished cost programme (P-03): £3m to £4m a year against £6m of savings that stop growing after FY2027. This is the loop that turns my margin path from rising 70bp a year to rising 20bp to 40bp a year after FY2028. (4) Scale loss in procurement. Each exit (Albert's three sites, Plano, Mexicali, a Components sale) reduces purchasing volume with suppliers who allocate scarce parts by scale, at a time when memory, PCB and mature-node lead times are extending; smaller EMS groups "rely on sourcing partners" for allocations. The size is unquantifiable but the direction is clear: it raises the probability of a delivery miss on a programme and nudges gross margin down 10bp to 20bp a year in tight-supply periods. (5) OEM payment terms and inventory hubs. Blue-chip medical and aerospace customers demand customer-funded hubs and extended terms (the £21.3m H1 2024 outflow was partly the mix shift to longer-term customers); this works against working capital in the same direction as loop (1) and is the reason I do not take conversion back to the FY2025 level even in FY2029. A sixth, one-off balancing item: the £3.5m Plano last-time-buy profit in the H2 2025 comparator, which makes H2 2026 reported profit growth look muted (my H2 2026 adjusted operating profit of £23.0m is below H2 2025's £23.7m reported and 14% above the £20.2m clean comparator).

The net, and the interplay. For the next 18 months the reinforcing loops are worth about £8m to £10m of operating profit (utilisation and wins £4m to £5m, savings £3m, Power phasing and mix £1m to £2m) and 4 to 5 points of tax rate, against balancing loops worth about £3m to £4m (inflation, Plano comparator) and £10m to £15m of working capital; the P&L compounds, the cash flow does not yet. From months 18 to 48 the reinforcing loops slow (the sites fill, the savings stop, the tax rate normalises) and the balancing loops persist, so the base case is a company whose margin plateaus at 10% to 10.5% on a 3.5% to 4% growth rate unless the Components disposal, a further site exit or a bolt-on acquisition restarts a loop. That is the shape of a repair, not a compounder, and it matches the first set's judgement that the mechanical half of the improvement takes the margin to about 9.5% and the heroic half is needed beyond.

Peers as calibration. Volex is the precedent for the reinforcing loops running longest (nine years) and for the working-capital loop biting hardest in the growth phase; discoverIE is the precedent for order growth leading revenue by six months with a 0.72 correlation, which I apply to TT's £550m order book to say that H1 2027 revenue is largely written; XP Power is the live warning on loop (2); Solid State is the warning that loop (4) in defence compounds in the backlog before the P&L Cicor's doubling of margins at the former TT sites is evidence that loop (1) has further to run at TT's remaining estate than management has yet unlocked.

Indicators that decide which set of loops is winning: FY2026 cash conversion against 70% (loop 1 of the balancing set); the H2 2026 EMS margin against 8.5% (reinforcing loop 1); Components book-to-bill falling toward 1.0x by mid-2027 (balancing loop 2 ending); the FY2026 annual report's statement on the five-year plan and deferred tax (reinforcing loop 3); the conversion of the Rolls-Royce and MBDA frameworks into dated production orders (reinforcing loop 4).

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction to lean them

C55

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement: the forecasts for FY2026 deserve high weight and should be leaned up on profit; FY2027 deserves moderate weight and should be leaned up on operating profit and down on EPS; FY2028 and beyond deserve low weight and should be held at consensus on profit with the recognition that the fourth year rests on two brokers; across all years revenue should be held near consensus, cash flow held at consensus for FY2026 and leaned slightly up thereafter. The reason for the asymmetry is the one structural fact the delivery record establishes: TT's surprises follow the direction of the cycle, amplified by operating leverage and site execution, and the cycle is now up. The current management team's eighteen-month record (three beats, one raise, every operational milestone delivered on time) is what makes the near-term lean credible; the company's five-year record (three profit warnings, a 57% consensus EPS cut in a year, two withdrawn margin targets, a restatement, £93.6m of impairments) is what caps the weight the out-years can carry.

The record, read for what it predicts. In the 2021 to 2022 restocking upturn the company grew revenue 10% and 30% and beat; in the 2023 to 2025 destocking it missed badly because under-absorption met factory failure; in the last twelve months (FY2025 £37.2m against £34.5m consensus; H1 2026 £18.5m against £16.5m; EPS 5.7p against 4.0p) it has beaten as the cycle turned, before the volume has even arrived. The direction of surprise has therefore been set by the external cycle and the amplitude by internal execution. For the next 18 months the external cycle is up (order book +20%, book-to-bill 1.12x, cohort book-to-bill above 1.0, restocking live, defence structural) and internal execution is improving from a low base; that combination has historically produced beats, and the honest-tracker guidance posture ("ahead of market expectations" after a half is banked, no numeric upgrade, no FY2027 guidance) means consensus will keep lagging delivery. Hence the up-lean on FY2026 and FY2027 operating profit: I am at £41.5m and £47.0m against consensus £40.0m and £45.0m, 4% above in both years, which is within the 6% to 12% range of the last two beats and deliberately below the £42m to £43m that the H2 building blocks would support on a clean run.

Why the EPS lean for FY2027 is the other way. Consensus FY2027 EPS of 15.9p on consensus pre-tax profit of £39.0m implies a tax rate of about 26% to 27%; the company guides a mid-to-high 30s rate for FY2026 and gives no date for normalisation, and Jefferies explicitly models slower normalisation. The sell-side revision counts make the point sharply: FY2027 EPS is up 14% over three months while FY2027 EBITDA is up 2% and sales up 2%, so most of the EPS upgrade cycle of the last quarter has been brokers lowering the tax rate and the interest line, not raising profit. My 32% rate gives 15.5p on higher operating profit; if the rate were 35%, 14.8p. The record on tax is the weak part of the record: the FY2025 EPS miss (6.9p against 8.0p) was a tax miss on a profit beat, and the rate went to 57.1% against a prior 28.3%. So the forecast line with the worst delivery history is the one consensus has quietly become most optimistic about.

Weight by line item and year, as a steer.

  • Revenue: moderate weight, hold at consensus (FY2026 £482m, FY2027 £503m; my £480m and £504m). The order book covers FY2027 almost fully and the H2 2026 return to organic growth needs no end-market help, but Power timing (flat H1 2026 on finalisation delays) and the Kuantan ramp both sit in customers' hands. Revenue has been the least-surprising line in either direction (FY2025 £481.4m against £483m consensus; H1 2026 in line).
  • Operating profit: high weight for FY2026 (H1 banked, H2 built from dated items), lean up 3% to 5%; moderate weight for FY2027, lean up 3% to 5%; low weight beyond, hold.
  • Margin shape between halves: lean toward H2 weighting of 54% to 55% of the year in FY2026 and FY2027, consistent with the group's seasonality; the H2 2025 comparator contains £3.5m of non-repeating profit, so reported H2 2026 growth will look muted even on a beat, and the third-set Analyst should pre-empt that optical trap.
  • Cash: moderate weight for FY2026 at the guided 70% to 80% (I use about 72%), because management has met its cash commitments for two years running and the H1 build was described as deliberate; lean slightly up from FY2027 as restructuring cash ends.
  • Tax and EPS: low weight on any assumption of normalisation before FY2028; lean FY2027 EPS down toward 15p to 15.5p against 15.9p.
  • Dividends: consensus 2.5p for FY2027 is reasonable; a small FY2026 final (I carry 1.0p) is more likely than not but is not guided.
  • Out-years (FY2029, FY2030): low weight; consensus rests on two brokers for FY2029 and none for FY2030; the LTIP's own vesting target (12% EPS compound growth from 2026, about 16.7p by FY2029) is below consensus 19.9p, which tells the Consumer that management's internal stretch is below the street's, a point in favour of the street being too high in the out-years on EPS even if it is too low on near-term profit.

What the record says about the downside. The precedents for misses are all of the same type: a site execution failure meeting a volume shortfall (2024: Cleveland and Kansas City yields plus distributor destocking), announced in a trading update outside results (September and November 2024), followed by downgrades of 15% to 30% within a quarter. The equivalent now would be a Kuantan ramp problem or a Cleveland relapse meeting a Power award slip; the first set's correlated downside (margin toward 7% in FY2027, EPS about 9p) is the right stress case and its probability is moderate (I would say 15% to 20% over 18 months), not remote. The record also says the current management has so far named problems early (Plano closed within two months, Mexicali closed without a warning) rather than defending them, which argues that a miss would be smaller and earlier than the 2024 pattern.

Peers for calibration. Volex guides precisely and beats; discoverIE guides conservatively and delivers; both deserve higher weight on their out-years than TT. Solid State's forecasts carry low weight because award timing dominates; XP Power's carry moderate weight after its own 2024 reset. TT's forecasts now deserve the weight of a company one year into a credible repair: more than Solid State's, less than Volex's.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C60

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: the items below operating profit are, unusually, a larger source of EPS growth than operating profit itself over the next four years, and they turn from a persistent drain over the last six years into a tailwind: of the roughly 15p of adjusted EPS growth from 6.9p (FY2025) to 21.5p (FY2030) in my base, about 8p comes from operating profit, about 5p from the tax rate falling from 57% through 36% to 26%, about 1.5p from the finance line, and about minus 0.5p from share-count dilution; free cash flow gains a further £6m to £8m a year from the end of restructuring and transition cash by FY2028. The tax rate is the dominant and least certain of these, and the Consumer should treat it as the single input whose assumption most needs to be stated rather than inherited: between a 35% and a 27% FY2027 rate lies 1.8p, or 12%, of EPS.

Tax, in detail. FY2025's adjusted charge of £13.9m on a small base gave 57.1% (28.3% in FY2024), because North America's losses attracted no credit and £2.7m of US deferred tax assets were derecognised on top of £16.0m in FY2024; H1 2026 was 36.3%, guided mid-to-high 30s for FY2026. The gross unrecognised losses are £282.4m (£225.1m US), none of which expire, and the company puts the normalised rate at 25.4% (26.7% in H1 2026 terms). Cash tax has run below the charge throughout (£7.6m paid in FY2025 and FY2024 against charges of £13.9m and £20.0m; £9.1m, £5.9m and £4.7m in FY2023 to FY2021), and tax provisions of £12.2m (up to £16.0m in a worse case) sit on the balance sheet. My path: 36% (FY2026), 32% (FY2027), 29% (FY2028), 27% (FY2029), 26% (FY2030). The reasoning: with Plano gone and Cleveland profitable, North America should be profitable in FY2026 and clearly so in FY2027, at which point US profits are sheltered by brought-forward losses and the group rate falls mechanically toward 30% without any recognition decision; formal recognition (a credit of up to £18.7m, plus £7.9m, taken outside adjusted profit) would follow once the five-year plan supports it, most likely in the FY2027 or FY2028 accounts, and would settle the adjusted rate near 25%. This is more optimistic than the first set's recommendation to hold 35% for FY2027 and less optimistic than consensus's implied 26% to 27%; I have chosen the middle because the mechanical first step is near certain once the US is profitable, and the US was profitable in H1 2026 on the evidence of Cleveland and the EMS margin. Cash tax will stay below the charge throughout (I use £8m, £9.5m, £11m, £12.5m, £13.5m), a positive leak for free cash flow of £3m to £5m a year. The downside is a US relapse, which would hold the rate above 35% and keep the losses trapped; the upside is recognition in the FY2026 accounts, which would take FY2027 EPS toward 16.5p. Peer context: discoverIE's rate is stable at 23.5% to 24%, XP Power's unrecognised losses (£31.8m) begin to expire in 2029, Solid State absorbs small movements inside its reconciliation; TT is the outlier and the one with the most to gain.

Interest and other financial items. Covered in P-02: £5.5m falling to £3.2m, 84% fixed, lease interest £0.6m, pension credit disappearing as the UK scheme buys out, interest income negligible. The FY2025 net-debt reduction included an £11.2m pension surplus refund that will not recur; there is no further pension cash to come.

Adjusting items and the statutory-to-adjusted bridge. Over three years adjusting items exceeded £180m (FY2024 £60.6m; FY2025 £65.4m: £41.4m impairments, £15.2m restructuring including £7.0m Plano and £6.1m Cleveland, £2.6m acquired-intangible amortisation, £1.6m management transition); H1 2026 £8.8m (£3.8m Mexicali impairment, £1.0m restructuring, £1.3m amortisation, about £3m of programme cost). Cash within these: £7.9m in FY2025 (£4.2m restructuring, £3.7m acquisition and disposal costs including Cicor advisory fees), about £3.8m in H1 2026 and about £6m for FY2026 as a whole. For the forecast: acquired-intangible amortisation of about £2.5m a year continues (non-cash, outside adjusted EPS); restructuring and transition cash of about £6m in FY2026 and £2m in FY2027, then near zero; no impairments assumed (the North American goodwill is largely written off, £93.6m over two years); disposal costs of £1m to £2m if Components is sold. The statutory-to-adjusted gap should fall from £65m to about £10m to £12m in FY2026 and £4m to £5m thereafter, which means statutory EPS converges on adjusted EPS from FY2027 for the first time since 2022. That convergence is itself a catalyst for the fourth set: the sector digest's "first clean margins" test. The control history (the £5.7m FY2023 restatement at Cleveland, Deloitte's zero reliance on controls, internal audit co-sourced with PwC, the Provision 29 project) argues for keeping a small probability of a further prior-period adjustment in the risk register; I do not model one.

Share count. Basic weighted average 177.8m and diluted 181.3m in FY2025; 178.6m shares in issue; FactSet's 186.4m diluted appears to include all options and awards regardless of performance conditions and is the figure to avoid. Awards granted in 2026: 723,938 LTIP shares to the CEO (2 April), 576,419 LTIP and 472,304 buyout shares to the CFO (29 June; the buyout vests 31 March 2027); sharesave and US stock purchase issuance of about 0.1m a half. No buyback exists or is mooted, and executive ownership is too thin for one to be a personal priority. I carry diluted shares of 182.0m, 183.5m, 185.0m, 186.0m and 187.0m for FY2026 to FY2030 (about 1.5m a year of dilution from awards, no buyback, sharesave issuance of about £0.5m a year for cash), a drag of about 0.8% a year on EPS. The Value Creation Plan cited by Jefferies on 22 September 2026 (10% of value created above a 521p hurdle) cannot be TT's, since 521p is nearly three times the share price; I treat it as a mis-attribution in the research and exclude it. A Components disposal would not change the count unless proceeds were returned by buyback, which this board has not signalled.

Dividends. Nil for FY2024 (paid £12.2m in cash during 2024 for FY2023) and FY2025; the policy is under review with an update at the March 2027 results; leverage is already below the 1.5x threshold that preceded Senior's and Volex's reinstatements. I carry a 1.0p final for FY2026 (about £1.8m, paid mid-2027), 2.5p for FY2027, 3.5p, 4.5p and 5.5p thereafter, cover above 4x throughout; consensus carries nil, 2.5p, 3.1p and 4.3p. The cash cost is £0.7m in FY2026 (nil paid), £2.9m in FY2027, £5.4m in FY2028, £7.3m in FY2029, £9.2m in FY2030, well inside free cash flow. If the Components sale completes, a larger step (3p to 4p for FY2027) becomes likely.

Minority investments, associates, disposals. None in the base. The Components disposal (P-09) is the one item that would run through this part of the model: proceeds of £20m to £40m, a probable statutory loss or small gain on disposal depending on carrying value (Albert's precedent: £22.1m of net assets disposed for £20.8m after a £32.5m held-for-sale write-down), recycling of translation reserves through the income statement, and £1m to £2m of stranded central cost.

Currency. 79% of sales outside sterling; a 1 cent move in GBP/USD moves operating profit by about £0.1m; 75% of expected transaction exposures hedged over 12 months, 50% over 12 to 24; translation unhedged. Translation cut revenue by £38.1m cumulatively over FY2023 to FY2025 (£10.1m in FY2025). The Geography digest's instruction to model sterling separately stands: consensus expects sterling to drift to $1.31 to $1.34, which would add about £3m to £5m of revenue and £0.5m to £1.0m of operating profit in FY2027 against FY2026; the first set's reverse case (GBP/USD to 1.40) strips about £4.5m. I have put a neutral currency assumption into the base and note that the half-year revenue growth figures carry plus or minus 1.5 points of currency noise.

Net income and free cash flow, assembled. Adjusted net income: £23.0m (FY2026), £28.4m, £33.2m, £37.0m, £40.2m; consensus (one broker) £21.8m, £27.9m, £33.8m. Free cash flow after interest, tax, leases, capex, working capital and restructuring cash: about £8.5m (FY2026), £20m, £28m, £31m, £33.5m; consensus £9.0m, £18.9m, £26.7m, £30.4m. Against the last six years (FactSet free cash flow £7.0m, £15.4m, minus £15.7m, minus £16.4m, £18.7m, £21.5m, £34.2m for FY2019 to FY2025, with the best years coming from inventory liquidation), the forecast years are the first in which free cash flow is generated by profit rather than by shrinking.

P-09

The net likely impact of all the above on the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C62

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: the net impact is a company whose revenue grows modestly from a lower base, whose operating profit grows about twice as fast as revenue for three years because leverage, cost and mix all point the same way, whose EPS grows about twice as fast again because tax and interest normalise, and whose free cash flow becomes profit-led rather than liquidation-led; against the last four to six years this is a reversal on every line except revenue, where the forecast period merely stops the decline. The items that generate the differences are concentrated in five places (volume absorption at Cleveland and Kuantan, the £6m programme, Power's phasing and mix, the tax rate, the interest line), four of which are internal and dated, and that concentration is both what makes the next 18 months modellable and what makes the following 30 months more fragile than consensus's smooth 4% growth suggests.

Line by line, the forecast period against the last four to six years.

  • Revenue: £617m, £614m, £521m, £481m for FY2022 to FY2025 (compound minus 8% a year from FY2022, about minus 3% organic excluding Albert and pass-through); forecast £480m, £504m, £524m, £543m, £562m, compound plus 3.2% from FY2025 to FY2030 with the growth front-loaded in FY2027 (+5.0%) because the Kuantan and Plano effects lap and the order book converts. The difference comes from external forces (defence structural, restocking cyclical) and from the end of self-inflicted removals, not from share gain or new markets; the restocking element (1 to 1.5 points of FY2027 growth) is the fragile part.
  • Gross margin: 21.6% to 22.8% in the decline years (20.7% at the trough) against 25% to 27% in the forecast years; the step came from removing loss-making sites and scrap and will hold if volume lands without execution cost.
  • Adjusted operating profit and margin: £47.1m (FY2022, 7.6%), £46m to £53m (FY2023, 7.6% to 8.6% depending on basis), £37.1m (7.1%), £37.2m (7.7%); forecast £41.5m (8.6%), £47.0m (9.3%), £51.5m (9.8%), £54.5m (10.0%), £57.5m (10.2%). The FY2023 level is regained in FY2027 on £110m less revenue, which is the cleanest statement of what the repair has achieved. Decomposition of the 250bp of margin gain FY2025 to FY2030: volume absorption plus 200bp to 250bp, cost programme plus 120bp, mix plus 40bp, price plus 20bp to 30bp, inflation on the fixed base minus 150bp to 180bp, Plano last-time-buy comparator minus 70bp, central cost leverage plus 20bp.
  • Adjusted EBITDA: £49.8m (10.3%) to £72.0m (12.8%); consensus 10.9%, 11.5%, 12.1%, 12.3% for FY2026 to FY2029. Management's undated 12% operating-margin target (about 14.5% EBITDA) is not reached in the base without the disposal; with the disposal and £1m of stranded cost removed, FY2028 reaches about 10.7% to 11.0% operating margin on £445m of revenue and 12% comes into view for FY2029 or FY2030.
  • Net finance cost: £8.9m, £11.3m, £11.4m, £8.8m (FactSet FY2022 to FY2025) against £5.5m falling to £3.2m; pre-tax profit therefore grows faster than operating profit.
  • Tax: 57% to 26%; the largest single lever on EPS and the least guided.
  • Adjusted EPS: 19.0p (FY2023 brokers' basis), 10.9p, 6.9p; forecast 12.7p, 15.5p, 17.9p, 19.9p, 21.5p; consensus 11.9p, 15.9p, 17.8p, 19.9p. The FY2023 level is regained in FY2029 in the base, a year earlier with recognition of the US tax assets or the disposal. Compound EPS growth FY2025 to FY2030 is about 25% a year, but it is a recovery, not a compounding franchise; from FY2028 the growth rate falls to 10% to 11% and from FY2029 to 8%.
  • Free cash flow: liquidation-led in FY2023 to FY2025 (inventory release £14.8m in FY2025, pension refund £11.2m); profit-led from FY2027 at 85% to 95% conversion of adjusted operating profit (company definition) and about 70% of net income (FactSet-style); restructuring cash ends; capex rises from £8m to £13m to £14.5m; working capital absorbs £3m to £6m a year.
  • Balance sheet: net debt ex leases £50m to net cash £48m by FY2030 without a disposal; a dividend from 2027; no buyback; a capital-allocation question from FY2028 that this board has not yet answered.

The Components disposal as a dated scenario (not in the base; probability of completion within 18 months about 55%). Assumptions: completion mid-2027; net proceeds £25m to £35m (Jefferies' 5.0x EBITA sum-of-the-parts gives only £11.5m on its £2.3m FY2027 EBITA; the first set cited a £38.2m figure from the same broker on an earlier basis; a strategic buyer on 0.4x to 0.5x sales would pay £30m to £40m; I split the difference); the division removed at about £77m of revenue and £2.5m to £3m of profit; £1m of stranded central cost. Effects: FY2027 revenue £466m and adjusted operating profit £45.7m (9.8%); FY2028 revenue £444m and profit £47.5m to £48.5m (10.7% to 10.9%); interest saved about £1.2m a year; FY2028 EPS roughly unchanged at 17.5p to 18.0p (the lost profit offsets the interest saving, and the margin and quality improve rather than the earnings); net cash by end-FY2027; predictability improved by removing the shortest-visibility, highest-gearing division; group pricing mix moves toward the designed-in share. The third-set Analyst should carry this as the main source of upside to the multiple and of narrowing to the cone, not as a source of EPS.

Where I disagree with, or refine, the first set. (1) Tax rate for FY2027: the first set advised holding 35% unless recognition is modelled explicitly; I model 32% on the mechanical sheltering of US profits before any recognition, because the H1 2026 evidence (Cleveland profitable, EMS margin 7.9%, North America no longer loss-making) makes the first step probable; the difference is 0.7p of FY2027 EPS. (2) Working capital: the first set used about 17% of revenue; I use 15.5% to 16% because the Dynamo inventory work and the exit of distribution-heavy sites have lowered the structural level, and the H1 2026 19.7% was a deliberate transfer-related build; the difference is about £5m of FY2026 free cash flow. (3) FY2027 revenue: the first set held 4% as the central case; I take 5.0% because the Kuantan customer's £14m H1 2026 shortfall laps in H1 2027 and the order book is 13 months of revenue; the difference is about £3m of revenue, within noise, but it changes the shape (H1 2027 at +6.5%). (4) The Components valuation: the first set's £38.2m enterprise value and the P4 report's £11.5m are the same broker on different bases; the Consumer should treat proceeds as £20m to £40m and the disposal as margin- and quality-accretive but EPS-neutral, not as the £2m interest saving plus 100bp of margin the first set implied without netting the lost profit and stranded cost. (5) The first set's C-52 set the real cash test at the guided 70% for FY2026 and above 90% for FY2027; I would set FY2027 at 85% to 95% (company definition) because growth absorbs working capital, and treat 90%-plus as a FY2028 standard; a FY2027 print of 85% should not be read as a miss. (6) The Value Creation Plan dilution flagged in the P4 research is almost certainly mis-attributed (a 521p hurdle on a 186p share) and should not enter the share count. (7) On operating leverage the first set gave a two-to-one EPS range (12p to 19p) from a plus or minus 5% FY2027 revenue cone; I narrow that to about 12.5p to 18.5p because the tax rate and interest line move less than it assumed with volume, but I agree on the asymmetry (downside drop-through higher than upside).

Skew and width. Over twelve months the skew on operating profit is positive (consensus £40m is a floor; £41m to £43m is the range) and the skew on EPS is neutral (the tax rate caps it); over four years the skew on margin is neutral at 10% without the disposal and positive with it, and the skew on revenue is slightly negative after FY2027 because the restocking pulse and Power timing are both capable of taking a point off a year. The cone on FY2027 EPS is about 12.5p to 18.5p around 15.5p; on FY2028 about 13p to 22p around 17.9p. Concentration: five drivers explain 80% of the difference between the forecast and the past, which makes the forecast legible and makes any one of them (above all the tax rate and North American profitability, which are the same thing) capable of moving EPS by 10% or more alone.

P-10

The carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years

C60

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: my base case is a margin and cash repair that is about 40% complete and will be about 80% complete by the end of FY2027, delivering adjusted operating profit of £41.5m, £47.0m and £51.5m for FY2026 to FY2028 (8.6%, 9.3%, 9.8%) on revenue of £480m, £504m and £524m, adjusted EPS of 12.7p, 15.5p and 17.9p, free cash flow of about £8.5m, £20m and £28m, and net debt ex leases of about £42m, £24m and roughly nil; then a plateau at a 10% to 10.5% margin growing 3.5% a year with a 5p to 6p dividend and a net-cash balance sheet by FY2029 to FY2030, unless the Components disposal, a bolt-on or a bidder changes the perimeter. Against consensus the base is 4% above on FY2026 and FY2027 operating profit, 7% above on FY2026 EPS, 3% below on FY2027 EPS (tax), level on FY2028 and FY2029 EPS, and level on revenue and free cash flow. The case rests on things the company has already done (Plano, Cleveland, Suzhou, the programme) converting into things it has not yet shown (volume on the order book at a 25% to 30% drop-through, 85%-plus cash conversion with growth, two years of North American profit), and its predictability is high for the next two halves and moderate thereafter.

The key operational drivers, with the numbers I use.

  • Order book and book-to-bill: £550m (+20%), 1.12x group, 1.14x Power at June 2026; I assume the group book-to-bill stays above 1.05x through H1 2027 and drifts to 1.0x to 1.05x by FY2028 as restocking plateaus; discoverIE's six-month order-to-revenue lag implies H1 2027 revenue is largely written.
  • Divisional revenue (company basis, FY2025 restated to FY2028): Power £206m, £209m, £220m, £231m (flat in FY2026 on H1 phasing, then +5% a year on defence and the Rolls-Royce and MBDA frameworks); EMS £196m, £198m, £208m, £216m (down 8% in H1 2026 on the transfer, recovering to +5% in FY2027 as Kuantan reaches full rate and Cleveland fills, then +4%); Components £79m, £74m, £77m, £77m (Plano out, 6% underlying growth in FY2026 and FY2027 on restocking, flat in FY2028 as the pulse ends). Divisional margins: Power 16.2%, 15.5%, 16.0%, 16.2% (H1 2026's 14.2% is phasing and recovers in H2); EMS 4.9%, 8.2%, 8.8%, 9.3% (Cleveland and Kuantan absorption, the EMS margin cap in the high single digits per the first set); Components 2.3%, 3.0%, 3.5%, 3.5%; central costs £7.7m, £7.6m, £7.6m, £7.8m (the programme offsetting inflation). The sum: £37.2m, £41.5m, £47.5m, £52.4m before a haircut of £0.5m to £0.9m in FY2027 and FY2028 for wage, compliance and currency friction, giving £41.5m, £47.0m, £51.5m.
  • Half-year shape: H2 2026 revenue £252m (+3.3% reported; organic growth returns as guided), adjusted operating profit £23.0m (9.1%; 14% above the £20.2m clean comparator and 3% below the £23.7m reported one), EBITDA margin 11.6%; H1 2027 revenue £243m (+6.5%, the Kuantan lap), profit £21.6m (8.9%), EBITDA 11.6%; H2 2027 revenue £261m (+3.6%), profit £25.4m (9.7%), EBITDA 12.2%. The H2 weighting is 55% in FY2026 and 54% in FY2027.
  • Cost: £3m of net programme savings in FY2026, £6m-plus from FY2027, nothing further; inflation on the fixed base £3m to £4m a year from FY2027; restructuring cash £6m, £2m, then nil; no further footprint action in the base.
  • Price: 1 to 1.5 points of revenue a year, neutral to margin over a year, with a possible 30bp to 60bp gross-margin dip in a half of fast input inflation (H1 2027 the most exposed).
  • Capex: £12m, £13m, £13.5m, £14m, £14.5m (2.5% to 2.6% of sales, up from 1.8%), for Kuantan, Bedlington silicon carbide and winding automation; D&A ex acquired intangibles £12.5m rising to £14.5m.
  • Working capital: 15.5% of revenue at FY2026, 16% thereafter (£10m absorbed in FY2026, £3m to £6m a year after); company cash conversion about 72% in FY2026, 85% to 90% in FY2027, 90%-plus from FY2028.
  • Below the line: net finance cost £5.5m to £3.2m; adjusted tax rate 36%, 32%, 29%, 27%, 26% (first-step normalisation without recognition; recognition is upside); cash tax £8m to £13.5m; diluted shares 182m to 187m; dividend 1.0p (FY2026 final), 2.5p, 3.5p, 4.5p, 5.5p.
  • Currency: neutral; each cent on GBP/USD is £0.1m of operating profit.
  • Perimeter: no disposal in the base; the disposal scenario in P-09 (mid-2027, £25m to £35m net, EPS-neutral, margin plus 90bp to 110bp, net cash a year earlier).

Why this and not consensus. Consensus (four brokers) is a sound shape and I use it as a floor for operating profit in FY2026 and FY2027 for the reasons in P-06; I sit above it because the H2 2026 building blocks (the second £1.5m of savings, Kuantan volume into carried fixed cost, Power's deferred contracts, Components' continued recovery) add up to more than the £21.5m of H2 profit consensus implies, and because the Kuantan lap in H1 2027 is worth about 6 points of growth in that half that the brokers' 4.4% full-year growth appears to spread evenly. I sit below consensus on the FY2027 tax rate (32% against an implied 26% to 27%), which is why my higher profit produces a lower EPS, and level from FY2028 because by then the tax rate converges and my margin advantage is eroded by inflation. I sit level on revenue and cash because the evidence does not support leaning either.

Why this and not management's ambition. The 12% margin target is undated and arithmetically reachable only with the disposal (FY2028 ex-Components at about 10.7% to 11.0%) plus a further site exit or two more years of mix; on today's perimeter it would need about £63m to £65m of operating profit on £525m to £545m of revenue, which requires either a 35%-plus drop-through sustained for three years or revenue growth of 6% to 7%, neither of which the record supports. The LTIP's own 12% EPS compound target (about 16.7p by FY2029 from 11.9p) is below my 19.9p, which says management is planning conservatively and the street is not.

Skew, cone and the paths. Twelve months: positive skew on operating profit (£41m to £43m against consensus £40m; probability of a FY2026 print below £40m about 15%, mostly a Power timing or Kuantan risk, and above £42m about 30%), neutral on EPS, negative on sentiment (the multiple is at the 93rd percentile of its five-year range in a regime that punishes re-rated small caps). Eighteen months to four years: the cone on FY2028 EPS runs from about 13p (the correlated execution downside: Kuantan plus Cleveland plus a Power slip, margin toward 7% to 7.5%, tax rate stuck above 35%) to about 22p (Components sold at £35m, recognition of the US tax assets in the FY2027 accounts, Power at 17% on defence mix), around 17.9p; I weight the downside path at about 15% to 20%, the base at 55% to 60% and the upside at 20% to 25%. The three dated tests inside the next six months are discoverIE's interims in early December 2026 (the cohort restocking read-across), TT's own FY2026 results on 10 March 2027 (cash conversion against 70%, the H2 EMS margin against 8.5%, the dividend decision, the FY2027 framing, the five-year-plan statement on deferred tax, and the Components process) and the May 2027 AGM update (organic growth positive for a second half).

What would make me change the base. Upward: a Components sale announced at £30m or more; recognition of US deferred tax assets in the FY2026 accounts; H2 2026 Power margin above 16% with the deferred contracts landed; Cleveland winning a programme large enough to be named. Downward: a trading update before March 2027 (the 2024 pattern); H2 2026 cash conversion implying a full year below 65%; Components order intake falling below revenue (the XP Power air pocket); a second half of Power margin erosion with no phasing explanation; DBAY selling down or a bid rejected by DBAY (a ceiling on the equity even if the earnings are intact).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports three product divisions from 2026 (Power, EMS, Components) with FY2025 restated; divisional history is one year deep. Figures below are on the company's adjusted basis (revenue in GBP millions; adjusted operating margin in brackets); central costs are shown separately. H1 2026 actuals: Power £97.3m (14.2%), EMS £94.1m (7.9%), Components £36.7m (2.7%), central costs £3.7m; group £228.1m, £18.5m (8.1%).
