The divisional sums for FY2027 and FY2028 (£47.5m and £52.4m) exceed the group figures by £0.5m to £0.9m; the difference is a deliberate haircut for wage, compliance and currency friction not allocated to a division. Power: flat in FY2026 because H1 revenue was flat on delayed customer agreements, then +5% a year on European and NATO rearmament, the Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement and the MBDA letter of intent, with the margin returning to about 16% as H1 2026's 14.2% was phasing. EMS: down 8% in H1 2026 on the Suzhou-to-Kuantan transfer (the transferred customer was £14m lower year on year), recovering as Kuantan reaches full rate in H2 2026 and the lap arrives in H1 2027; the margin rises on Cleveland and Kuantan absorbing fixed cost already carried, capped in the high single digits by the division's cost-plus contract structure. Components: Plano removed from the base from Q4 2025 (about £8m of H2 2025 revenue and £3.5m of last-time-buy profit do not recur), 6% underlying growth in FY2026 and FY2027 on distributor restocking, then flat as the restocking pulse ends; kept in the base, with the disposal carried as a scenario below.

By end market (FY2025, old basis, for orientation only): Aerospace and Defence £152.8m growing low double digits; Automation and Electrification £140.1m recovering from -13%; Healthcare £107.8m flat to low single digits; Distribution £80.7m recovering with restocking then plateauing.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue. H2 2026 at £252m (+3.3% reported on H2 2025's £244m): organic growth returns as guided, Kuantan ships the transferred volumes, Power's deferred contracts land, Components grows about 6% on its ex-Plano base, EMS excluding the transferred customer grows about 4%; currency assumed neutral. H1 2027 at £243m (+6.5% on H1 2026's £228.1m): the Kuantan customer's £14m H1 2026 shortfall laps (I assume about £10m recovers), plus about 3% underlying growth; the £550m order book (13 months of revenue, book-to-bill 1.12x, 1.14x in Power) and discoverIE's six-month order-to-revenue lag mean this half is largely written. H2 2027 at £261m (+3.6%). FY2028 +4.0%: defence continues, restocking plateaus, the 4% to 6% medium-term target's low end. FY2029 and FY2030 +3.6% and +3.5%: below the market's 5% to 7% because TT holds no share-gain or new-market engine; the first set's judgement that standalone growth sits below the industry's. Price contributes 1 to 1.5 points a year throughout; volume the rest.

Margin and EBITDA. Adjusted EBITDA is adjusted operating profit plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation (FY2025: £37.2m plus £12.6m equals £49.8m, 10.3%, the brokers' basis). The operating-margin bridge from 7.7% (FY2025) to 9.8% (FY2028): volume absorption at a 25% to 30% drop-through on about £43m of revenue growth, plus 180bp to 220bp; the administrative cost programme (£3m net in FY2026, over £6m annualised from FY2027, nothing further), plus 120bp; Power mix, plus 30bp to 40bp; central cost leverage, plus 20bp; the Plano last-time-buy comparator, minus 70bp; wage, compliance (EO 14415, NIST) and energy inflation on a fixed base of about £110m to £120m at about 3% a year, minus 150bp cumulatively. H2 2026 adjusted operating profit of £23.0m (9.1%) is 14% above the clean H2 2025 comparator of £20.2m and 3% below the reported £23.7m that included £3.5m of Plano last-time-buy profit. H2 weighting 55% in FY2026 and 54% in FY2027. Depreciation and amortisation rises with capex from £12.5m to £14.5m.

Net financial items. £75m of private-placement notes at 3.65% (£37.5m due December 2028 and December 2031), a £105m revolving credit facility to June 2028 with £8.3m drawn, lease interest about £0.6m, a vanishing pension credit; adjusted net finance expense £2.7m in H1 2026. £5.5m in FY2026, falling as the RCF goes undrawn and cash builds, then £3.8m from FY2029 after the first note tranche is repaid from cash and the RCF is refinanced.

Tax. Adjusted effective rate 57.1% in FY2025, 36.3% in H1 2026, guided mid-to-high 30s for FY2026; normalised rate 25.4% once US deferred tax assets (£225.1m of £282.4m gross unrecognised losses, no expiry) are recognised. My path (36%, 32%, 29%, 27%, 26%) models the mechanical first step only: as North America is profitable (Plano closed, Cleveland profitable), US profits are sheltered by brought-forward losses and no unrelieved losses arise, so the rate falls toward the high 20s without an accounting decision. Formal recognition (a credit of up to £18.7m plus £7.9m, outside adjusted profit) is upside, most likely in the FY2027 or FY2028 accounts. Cash tax runs below the charge throughout (about £8m, £9.5m, £11m, £12.5m, £13.5m).

Dividend. Nil for FY2024 and FY2025; policy under review with an update at the March 2027 results; leverage already below 1.5x. I carry a 1.0p final for FY2026 (declared March 2027, about £1.8m paid mid-2027), then 2.5p, 3.5p, 4.5p and 5.5p, cover above 4x throughout. Consensus carries nil, 2.5p, 3.1p, 4.3p.

Share count. Basic 178.6m in issue, diluted weighted average 181.3m in FY2025. LTIP and buyout awards (CEO 723,938 shares; CFO 576,419 LTIP plus 472,304 buyout vesting 31 March 2027) and sharesave add about 1.5m a year, slowing to 1.0m; no buyback exists or is mooted; sharesave and US stock-purchase issuance of about £0.5m a year for cash is entered as positive issuance in Part 2.

Capital spending. Guided £10m to £13m for FY2026 (capex and development), up from £7.5m to £8.1m in FY2025 (1.8% of sales): Kuantan SMT lines and warehousing, Bedlington silicon-carbide capability, winding automation. I carry 2.5% to 2.6% of sales (£12m rising to £14.5m), including about £1m to £1.5m a year of capitalised development.

Working capital. Net working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables, FactSet basis) was 12.6% of revenue at FY2025 (a trough from a £14.8m inventory release) and 19.7% at H1 2026 after a deliberate £11.7m inventory build to protect supply through the Kuantan transfer; the historical range is 17% to 20%. I carry 15.5% at FY2026 (about £10m of working capital absorbed over the year on the company's cash-flow definition, consistent with guided cash conversion of 70% to 80%; I assume about 72%) and 16% thereafter (£3m to £6m a year absorbed at 4% growth). Company-definition cash conversion about 85% to 90% in FY2027 and 90%-plus from FY2028.

Leases. Lease liabilities £14.4m; principal plus interest about £4.5m a year; right-of-use depreciation is inside the D&A line, as adjusted EBITDA adds it back.

Restructuring and other cash outside adjusted profit (no input line in the form; deducted in my cross-check). About £6m in FY2026 (£3.8m paid in H1 for Mexicali, Plano and the programme; the balance in H2), about £2m in FY2027, then nil. Acquired-intangible amortisation of about £2.5m a year is non-cash and outside adjusted EPS. No impairments or acquisitions are assumed.

Currency. Neutral in the base. 79% of sales outside sterling; £0.1m of operating profit per cent on GBP/USD and per RMB 0.1; translation unhedged (cumulative revenue headwind £38.1m over FY2023 to FY2025). Consensus expects sterling to weaken to $1.31 to $1.34, worth about £0.5m to £1.0m of operating profit in FY2027 if it does; the half-year growth figures carry plus or minus 1.5 points of currency noise.

The Components disposal, carried as a scenario and not in the base (probability of completion within 18 months about 55%). Completion mid-2027 at net proceeds of £25m to £35m (Jefferies' 5.0x EBITA sum-of-the-parts gives £11.5m on £2.3m of EBITA; a strategic buyer on 0.4x to 0.5x sales would pay £30m to £40m); the division leaves at about £77m of revenue and £2.5m to £3m of profit; £1m of stranded central cost. Effects on the model if the Analyst chooses to overlay it: FY2027 revenue £466m and adjusted operating profit £45.7m (9.8%); FY2028 revenue £444m (+4.0% on the continuing base) and £47.5m to £48.5m (10.7% to 10.9%); net finance cost lower by about £1.2m a year from FY2028; "Acquisitions less disposals" of plus £30m in FY2027; adjusted EPS roughly unchanged (FY2028 17.5p to 18.0p); net cash by end-FY2027; a larger dividend step (3p to 4p for FY2027) likely.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 adjusted operating profit "ahead of current market expectations" (2 September 2026; the company-compiled range was then £32.6m to £38.5m). FactSet consensus is now £40.0m EBIT (four brokers: RBC, Jefferies, Investec, Stifel). My £41.5m is 4% above consensus and consistent with the H2 building blocks (H1 £18.5m banked; second £1.5m of savings; Kuantan volumes into carried fixed cost; Power's deferred contracts; Components' continued recovery).
  • FY2026 cash conversion 70% to 80%: I assume about 72%, with the H2 working-capital release modest (about £2m to £3m) because guidance is met largely by H2 profit and the end of the H1 build.
  • FY2026 adjusted tax rate mid-to-high 30s: I use 36%.
  • Cost programme £3m net in FY2026, over £6m annualised from FY2027, self-funded: taken at face value; no further savings assumed.
  • FY2026 capex and development £10m to £13m: I use £12m.
  • Return to organic revenue growth in H2 2026; Kuantan at higher volumes in H2; Power phasing benefits in H2: all assumed, with H2 revenue +3.3% and Power's H2 margin about 16%.
  • Medium-term targets (undated): 4% to 6% organic growth, 12% adjusted operating margin, mid-to-high-teens ROIC, 90%-plus cash conversion, leverage below 1.5x. My inputs sit at the low end of the growth range, reach 10.2% margin by FY2030 on today's perimeter (12% is reachable only with the Components disposal plus further mix or footprint action), reach 90%-plus conversion from FY2028, and take leverage to zero by FY2028.
  • No guidance exists for FY2027 or beyond; my FY2027 operating profit of £47.0m is 4% above consensus £45.0m (Jefferies £44.6m, 9.0%); my FY2027 EPS of 15.5p is 3% below consensus 15.9p because consensus's pre-tax profit of £39.0m and EPS imply a tax rate of about 26% to 27% that the company has not guided.
  • Dividend: decision deferred to March 2027; I carry a 1.0p FY2026 final where consensus carries nil.
  • The LTIP's internal target (12% compound EPS growth from 2026 at 95% cash conversion, about 16.7p by FY2029) is below my 19.9p and consensus 19.9p; management's own stretch on EPS is below the street's.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Adjusted operating profit: the company's headline measure (before acquired-intangible amortisation, restructuring, impairments, management transition costs and other adjusting items) is the same measure the four brokers submit to FactSet as EBIT; FY2025 £37.2m on both. FactSet's own history line (FF_EBIT_OPER £32.8m for FY2025) deducts items the company adjusts for and must not be used as the base for consensus growth.
  • Adjusted EBITDA: adjusted operating profit plus depreciation (including right-of-use depreciation) and amortisation of own intangibles, excluding acquired-intangible amortisation: FY2025 £37.2m plus £12.6m equals £49.8m (10.3%), which is FactSet's brokers'-basis actual. FactSet's operating EBITDA history (£47.5m, 9.9%) is a different basis. My EBITDA margins (11.2% to 12.8%) are on the brokers' basis and compare directly with consensus 10.9%, 11.5%, 12.1%, 12.3%.
  • Statutory to adjusted: FY2025 statutory operating loss of £28.2m after £65.4m of adjusting items (£41.4m impairments, £15.2m restructuring, £2.6m acquired-intangible amortisation, £1.6m transition costs and other); H1 2026 £8.8m of adjusting items (£3.8m Mexicali impairment, £1.0m restructuring, £1.3m amortisation, about £3m programme cost). I expect the FY2026 gap at about £10m to £12m and £4m to £5m a year thereafter, so statutory profit converges on adjusted profit from FY2027.
  • Net finance cost: the company's adjusted net finance expense (£2.7m in H1 2026) excludes items FactSet's net interest line (£8.8m for FY2025) includes; my inputs are on the adjusted basis.
  • Tax and EPS: adjusted EPS is adjusted profit after tax at the adjusted effective rate over the diluted weighted average share count (181.3m for FY2025); FY2025 6.9p company, 6.8p FactSet brokers' basis. FactSet shows 186.4m diluted shares, which appears to count all awards regardless of conditions; I use 182m to 187m. Any deferred-tax recognition credit would sit outside adjusted profit.
  • Free cash flow: the company's cash conversion is adjusted operating cash flow (after capex, before interest, tax and restructuring) over adjusted operating profit; FactSet's free cash flow is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex (before intangibles and leases); consensus free cash flow (£9.0m, £18.9m, £26.7m, £30.4m) appears to be after interest, tax and capex. My cross-check free cash flow is after interest, tax, leases, all capex, working capital and restructuring cash, before dividends.
  • Net debt: the company reports net debt excluding leases (£52.0m at H1 2026, covenant leverage 1.1x); FactSet includes leases (£68.0m). My figures are ex leases.
  • Revenue: no basis difference; organic growth excludes currency and disposals. FY2029 consensus rests on two brokers and FY2030 on none.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 13,820 words · written 24 October 2026, 04:40 to 04:59 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
TT Electronics (TTG-GB): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect

Written 2026-10-24 by the Investment Analyst for the Analyst who writes the investment memo.

How to read these answers, and the base they test (information, condensed)

  • The forecasts tested are the Financial Analyst's model (update 1), calculated from the second set's inputs: revenue £480m, £504m, £524m for FY2026 to FY2028; adjusted operating profit £41.5m, £47.0m, £51.5m (8.6%, 9.3%, 9.8%); adjusted diluted EPS 12.7p, 15.5p, 17.9p; free cash flow about £8.5m, £20m, £28m on the second set's cross-check (£5.3m, £17.7m, £25.4m in the model, explained below); net debt ex leases about £42m, £24m and nil. Consensus (FactSet median, four brokers, 22 October 2026): EBIT £40.0m, £45.0m, £50.0m; EPS 11.9p, 15.9p, 17.8p. Price 186p; 24-month-forward P/E 10.5x to 10.8x; the model's guideline fair multiple of 13.3x gives a total shareholder return of +46% to the 31 December 2027 column and +62% to the 30 June 2029 column.
  • The next 18 months run to April 2028 and contain, at the company: a probable trading update in November 2026 (one was issued on 22 November 2023, 14 November 2024 and 24 November 2025; none is announced), the FY2026 results on 10 March 2027 (the dividend decision, the first FY2027 framing, the annual report's deferred-tax statement), the AGM update in May 2027, the H1 2027 results in September 2027 and the FY2027 results in March 2028. Outside the company: Volex's interims in November 2026, discoverIE's in early December 2026, XP Power's fourth-quarter update in January 2027, Kitron's fourth quarter in February 2027, and the US continuing resolution that runs to 11 December 2026.
  • All profit figures are on the company's adjusted basis, which is the brokers' basis; EPS is adjusted and diluted; money in GBP millions, per-share figures in pence.

The model summary's cross-check: the gaps explained, and the one input changed

The summary flags no EPS gap (the model's 12.6p, 15.5p, 17.9p, 19.8p and 21.4p round to the second set's 12.7p, 15.5p, 17.9p, 19.9p and 21.5p) and a free cash flow gap in every year: the model is below the second set's cross-check by £3.2m, £2.3m, £2.6m, £1.6m and £0.9m for FY2026 to FY2030, when the second set expected the model to sit £6m above it in FY2026 and £2m above in FY2027 because the model has no line for restructuring cash. The true gap to explain is therefore about £9m in FY2026 and £4m in FY2027. Three mechanics explain all of it, to within £0.5m in every year. (1) Cash tax. The model has no cash-tax line, so it taxes cash at the adjusted P&L rate: 36% of £35.8m is £12.9m in FY2026 against the second set's £8m of cash tax (the company's own H1 2026 slides guided about £6m), a £4.9m difference; £13.4m against £9.5m in FY2027 (£3.9m); £13.5m against £11m in FY2028 (£2.5m); £13.7m against £12.5m in FY2029 (£1.2m); £14.1m against £13.5m in FY2030 (£0.6m). This is the whole of the gap from FY2028 onward and most of it in FY2027. It is not an input error: the tax rate is right for EPS, and lowering it to mimic the cash shelter would overstate earnings. The Consumer should carry the model's free cash flow and net cash as conservative by about £2.5m a year in FY2027 and FY2028 and by £1m a year thereafter, a cumulative understatement of net cash of roughly £7m by FY2030. (2) Restructuring cash. The model does not deduct the £6m (FY2026) and £2m (FY2027) of restructuring and transition cash that sits outside adjusted profit; this makes the model too generous by those amounts. In FY2026 the two structural omissions, (1) and (2), nearly cancel (+£6.0m less £4.9m). (3) Working capital. The model applies the working-capital percentage to FactSet's FY2025 base of £60.8m (12.6% of revenue). At 15.5% of £480m the FY2026 balance is £74.4m, an absorption of £13.6m, not the £10m the second set intended; thereafter 16% absorbs £6.2m in FY2027 and about £3m a year, as intended. The £3.6m of extra FY2026 absorption, plus £0.5m of higher cash interest, is the residual FY2026 gap (8.5 + 6.0 − 4.9 − 3.6 − 0.5 = 5.5, against the model's 5.3). This one is an input error in effect if not in intent: on the company's definition the model's FY2026 cash conversion falls to about 68% (EBITDA £53.8m less capex £12m less £13.6m, over £41.3m), below the guided 70% to 80% and below the second set's own 72%. I have changed the working-capital line to 15.0% for FY2026 and 15.5% thereafter (inputs-changed.md), which reproduces the second set's intended absorption (£11.2m, £6.1m, then about £3m a year), puts FY2026 conversion at about 73%, and stays inside FactSet's year-end range of 12.6% to 19.3%. The model's free cash flow should then read about £7.7m, £20.2m, £28.0m, £32.1m and £35.4m, and the model's net debt about £2.4m lower in FY2026 and about £13m lower by FY2030 than the summary shows. A fourth flag in the summary, "Phasing: FY2026: the periods were moved by +1.8 points to match the year", is a basis trap rather than an input error. The model's H1 2026 actual EBITDA appears to be FactSet's operating figure (£19.9m, 8.7%) rather than the brokers'-basis figure (£24.7m, 10.8%, which is adjusted operating profit of £18.5m plus £6.2m of depreciation and amortisation); to reach the year's 11.2% from the lower H1, the model lifted the H2 2026 margin from the input 11.6% to about 13.4%, which is the +1.8 points. The full-year FY2026 figures are right; any half-year figure the model shows for H2 2026 overstates EBITDA by about £4.5m and should not be quoted. The FY2027 halves (11.6% and 12.2%) reconcile to the year (11.9%) without adjustment. One further small item: the model's FY2026 year-end net debt of £38.9m sits about £3m below the second set's £42m before my change and I cannot reconcile the difference from the summary; the Consumer should carry the model's net-debt path with a tolerance of about £5m.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

C547 questions
D-0168D-0260D-0347D-0450D-0552D-0655D-0756

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts are predictable where it matters first and unpredictable where it matters most to the share price. The FY2026 operating profit is the most predictable number in the file: half of it is banked, the other half is built from dated items and an order book that covers the next 13 months, the cone is about plus or minus 5% around £41.5m, and the skew is above the £40m consensus. The FY2027 operating profit is moderately predictable (a cone of £41m to £51m around £47.0m, skewed above the £45m consensus with a fatter left tail), because the volume is in the order book but its conversion at Kuantan, Cleveland and in Power is in customers' hands and the drop-through is undemonstrated on the way up. Below operating profit the predictability falls away: the tax rate (the largest EPS lever, undated by the company and already assumed lower by consensus than the company guides), cash conversion with growth (never shown by TT) and the multiple (at the top of its five-year range, company-driven to an unusual degree, with a guideline fair value that requires a further re-rating) are the three lines where our assessment and the market's can diverge by 10% to 30% each. The earlier sets are congruent on all of this; the drivers are moderately concentrated, with half of the EPS uplift resting on North America (volume at Cleveland and the tax rate are one driver); the disclosure lets the group be monitored twice a year and the sites not at all; the internal trace is clean to EBIT and broken at tax; and the share price answers only to the company's own events. For the memo: present FY2026 and FY2027 operating profit as high-confidence, above-consensus numbers; present FY2027 EPS as a construction with the tax rate stated and a 12.5p to 18.5p cone; present FY2026 cash as the print most likely to disappoint relative to a good profit; treat the Components disposal and tax recognition as the two discrete events that would narrow the cone from FY2028; and set the risk to the share price as a company-event risk (March 2027, DBAY) rather than a sector one. The probability weights to carry are about 55% to 60% base, 15% to 20% correlated execution downside (margin toward 7% to 7.5%, FY2027 EPS about 12p to 13p), 20% to 25% upside (disposal at £30m or more, recognition in the FY2026 or FY2027 accounts, Power at 17%).

D-01

How congruent are the answers to the earlier sets: do they support and align with each other, or contradict or jar?

C68

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement: the two sets are highly congruent on everything that decides the next 18 months and jar in four places, three of which are differences of degree that the second set reasoned through and one of which is a genuine unresolved tension that the memo must hold rather than resolve. The congruence is structural rather than accidental: both sets describe the same company (a repair, not a compounder; a margin path from 7.7% to about 9.5% to 10% that is driven by identifiable internal items of known size; organic growth near 4% with no share-gain or new-market engine; the tax rate as the largest and least guided EPS lever; the Components disposal as a dated scenario that improves quality and narrows the cone but does not add EPS; a sale as the most likely end state with DBAY as arbiter; and the multiple, not the earnings, as where the near-term risk sits). The second set's numbers follow from the first set's mechanics without strain: the first set's C-71 and C-85 said FY2026 adjusted operating profit of £41m to £43m was reachable against £40m consensus and the second set took £41.5m; the first set's internal-change group said the EBIT path to £45m to £47m by FY2027 needs no demand and the second set built £47.0m from the same items (Plano gone, Cleveland profitable, £6m of savings, Kuantan restored, Power phasing, a falling interest line); the first set's economic-strength group asked for an explicit tax assumption, 70% to 80% cash conversion in FY2026, capex at £12.5m and the disposal held out of the base, and the second set did each of those.

The four places where the sets jar, and what the Consumer should do with each. (1) The FY2027 tax rate: the first set recommended holding 35% unless recognition was modelled explicitly; the second set used 32% on the ground that US profits are sheltered by brought-forward losses before any recognition decision, so the rate falls mechanically once North America is profitable. The second set is right about the mechanism (utilising previously unrecognised losses lowers the current tax charge without a deferred-tax entry), but the evidence that the mechanism has started is thinner than it reads: H1 2026's adjusted rate of 36.3% was still about ten points above the 26.7% the company says recognition would have produced, which means unrelieved US losses were still arising in H1 2026 even with Cleveland profitable and Plano closed; the AlphaSense tax report records that management states there is "no predefined calendar timeline" for reassessment and that Deloitte endorsed continued derecognition against the 2026 to 2030 plan. I keep 32% because it is the midpoint between what the company guides and what consensus has already assumed (26% to 27%), but the Consumer should treat the 0.7p of FY2027 EPS between 32% and 35% as the most contestable assumption in the model and the 1.8p between 27% and 35% as the width of the tax cone. (2) Working capital: the first set used about 17% of revenue, the second set 15.5% to 16%, and, as the cross-check section shows, the second set's percentages applied to FactSet's base absorbed more cash than the second set's own narrative said. I have resolved this in favour of the narrative (15.0% and 15.5%), which also sits closer to the guided cash conversion; the first set's 17% would take FY2026 conversion to about 50% and is inconsistent with management meeting its guidance, which both sets expect. (3) The restocking air pocket in Components: the second set's P-05 says the FY2028 revenue growth of 4.0% "does not fully reflect" about a point of downside from the Components pull-forward loop, while its P-10 holds Components flat in FY2028. This is a base-versus-skew statement rather than a contradiction (flat is the base, a fall is the skew), but it tells the Consumer that the second set's own author regards FY2028 revenue as skewed down, which matters when the memo's topic #6 presents FY2028 as a central case. (4) The genuine tension: the first set's risk group (C-82, C-83) concludes that sentiment risk is the largest risk to the share price, that the 24-month-forward P/E at 10.5x sits at the 93rd percentile of its own five-year range in a regime that "refuses re-ratings", and that a 20% to 35% de-rating on unchanged earnings (to 8.5x or 7.2x) is the sentiment trough; the model's return of +46% to December 2027 rests on a guideline fair multiple of 13.3x, which on the first set's own ten-year bands sits above "defendable" (12.5x) and far above "prudent" (9.8x). The template's own split attributes −15% to the multiple and +69% to earnings, so the return is earnings-led, but the earnings growth is capitalised at a multiple the first set would call optimistic for a stock in a turning-up phase. This is not a contradiction between the two sets (neither set chose the multiple) but it is the largest incongruence between the analysis in the files and the output of the model, and it belongs in the memo's topics #11 and #12 as the question of whether the second step of the clean-up re-rating (clean margins and cash in March 2027) is enough to carry the stock from the top of its own five-year range to the top of its ten-year range.

Smaller frictions, noted for completeness: the first set's figure of £38.2m for Components' enterprise value and the second set's £25m to £35m are reconciled by the AlphaSense disposal report (Jefferies' blended sum-of-the-parts value is £24.9m: £11.5m on 5.0x EBITA and £38.2m on 0.5x sales); the first set's reading of the LTIP (12% compound EPS growth from 11.9p, about 16.7p by FY2029) differs from the AlphaSense narrative report's account that the range was raised in September 2026 to 5% to 13% a year over three years from a deferred-tax-adjusted FY2025 base of 12.0p, which puts maximum vesting at about 17.3p of DTA-adjusted EPS by FY2028, at or below both our base and consensus on a like-for-like basis; both readings carry the same message, that management's own stretch on EPS is no higher than the street's. The earlier sets also agree on what they cannot know: Kuantan's utilisation, Cleveland's site margin, the Components proceeds, the deferred-tax threshold and the timing of defence awards are blind in both sets and in every AlphaSense report, which is itself a form of congruence and is the reason the research requests at the end target them.

D-02

To what extent does past and present delivery make the expected change plausible, and how far does the thesis rely on strengths the business demonstrably has rather than strengths it would need to acquire?

C60

Question asked To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

Judgement: the change expected over the next 18 months (adjusted operating profit from £37.2m to £41.5m and then £47.0m; margin from 7.7% to 9.3%; EPS from 6.9p to 15.5p) rests about three quarters on strengths the business has demonstrated in the last 18 months and one quarter on strengths it has not yet shown, and the undemonstrated quarter is concentrated in exactly the two lines (cash conversion with growth, and sustained North American profitability driving the tax rate) that the market doubts most; beyond FY2027, and above all for the 12% margin, the proportions reverse, and the thesis then relies on strengths (a low-cost footprint, scale in procurement, organic growth at the market rate, a North American business profitable through a full cycle) that TT has never had. The expected change is therefore plausible as far as the model takes it (a 9% to 10% margin business) and implausible on demonstrated strengths beyond that, which is the same conclusion the first set reached from the competitive-advantage side and the AlphaSense plausibility report reached from the precedents ("plausible but unproven"; Volex took four years to go from 2.0% to 8.1% and ten to reach 10.2%; XP Power's one-year jump needed a plant relocation TT is not undertaking).

What is demonstrated, and how well. (1) Removal of loss-making capacity and the cost programme: Plano closed within two months of the decision, Mexicali closed without a warning, the Suzhou transfer completed on plan, Cleveland's productivity from 57% to 81% and rework from 12% to 5%, the programme "substantially complete" with £1.5m banked in H1 2026. These are in the run rate; administrative programmes of this kind deliver on time at TT and at peers (discoverIE's £6.6m, Volex's site consolidations), so the £3m and £6m should be given full weight. (2) Guidance delivery: three beats and one raise in 18 months under the current chief executive, every operational milestone met on the date given; this is the strength that most changes the weight the near-term forecasts deserve, and it is the only thing in the record that would have been absent in April 2025. (3) The design-in moat: customers stayed through the 2024 crisis, Cleveland won three customers and six products in H2 2025 after a three-year drought, Rolls-Royce, MBDA and Collins were won since; the order book at £550m and 1.12x covers the next 13 months of revenue. (4) Cost-recovery pricing: £40m of inflation recovered in 2021 to 2023, tariffs passed through under co-terms, legacy European contracts repriced; this is demonstrated and is what the model needs, because the model asks price for only 1 to 1.5 points of revenue and nothing for margin. (5) Deleveraging: net debt ex leases from £126m to £50m in two years; the interest line is the most predictable input in the forecast.

What is not demonstrated, and what the model asks of it. (1) Volume absorption at a 25% to 30% drop-through while growing: TT's last growth year (FY2022, revenue +29.6%) delivered only 19% profit growth because £28m of the growth was zero-margin pass-through and price lagged cost; the high drop-through TT has shown is on the way down (Deutsche Bank's 88% on North American revenue losses in 2024), which is evidence of the mechanism's existence rather than of its upward operation; the sites that must absorb the volume (Cleveland, Kuantan) are the two whose capacity and utilisation the company does not quantify. The model's £11m to £13m of absorption by FY2028 is the largest single operating item and the one with the least precedent at TT; its saving grace is that the volume for the next two halves is in the order book rather than in a demand forecast. (2) Cash conversion with growth: TT's conversions above 100% (117% in FY2024, 150% in FY2025) came with falling revenue and an inventory release; the only growth half in the recent record (H1 2026, flat revenue with a Kuantan build) produced 42%. The model's 85% to 90% in FY2027 is what Volex (63.9%) and Cicor (negative free cash flow in H1 2026) failed to show in their growth phases, and the first set's precedent note (strong conversion with falling volume is the signature of a weak business) applies. This is the least demonstrated strength that the base case needs. (3) Two to three years of North American profitability: the region went from a £2.7m loss in 2024 to 0.7% in 2025 and a profitable H1 2026 at Cleveland, but the adjusted tax rate in H1 2026 (36.3% against 26.7% normalised) says US tax losses were still arising; the tax path (36%, 32%, 29%) and the 3.5p of EPS it carries by FY2028 depend on a run of US profitability that the company has not produced since before 2023, that its auditor has twice declined to underwrite (derecognitions of £16.0m and £2.7m), and that the AlphaSense tax precedents (Elastic, Vernalis) say typically takes three years out of a cumulative loss position. (4) Kuantan at full rate: the physical transfer is complete, the safety stocks are unwinding and orders are returning, but the site "has yet to achieve full-rate production" (Jefferies, 1 October 2026), the US duty on Malaysian imports (19% after the August 2025 agreement) is borne by customers under co-terms whose duration is undisclosed, and XP Power's Malaysian commissioning took twelve months; the H1 2027 revenue growth of 6.5% in the model is the Kuantan lap and is the single most Kuantan-dependent number in the forecast. (5) Organic growth at 4% to 6%: not shown since 2022, but the model's 5% in FY2027 needs no new strength because the order book and the lap supply it; the model's 4% in FY2028 and 3.5% thereafter assume no share gain and are below the market, which is the correct treatment of a strength the company lacks.

The proportions, sized. Of the £9.8m of adjusted operating profit growth from FY2025 to FY2027, about £6m (the programme) and the Plano and Cleveland removals already in the H1 2026 run rate are demonstrated; about £4m to £5m of volume absorption and £1m to £2m of Power phasing are order-book-backed but undemonstrated; the £3.5m Plano last-time-buy comparator and £3m to £4m of inflation are known headwinds. Of the 8.6p of EPS growth from FY2025 to FY2027, about 4.5p is operating profit (three quarters demonstrated), about 3p is the tax rate (undemonstrated, North America) and about 1p is interest (demonstrated). So the EPS forecast for FY2027 is roughly 65% to 70% demonstrated and the operating-profit forecast about 75% to 80%; the cash forecast is the least demonstrated of the three. For the 12% margin, the proportions invert: on today's perimeter it needs £63m to £65m of profit on £525m to £545m of revenue, which needs either a 35%-plus drop-through sustained for three years or 6% to 7% growth, neither of which TT has shown; with the disposal it comes into view for FY2029 or FY2030 through mix, which is a strength TT has (the ability to sell a division) rather than one it would have to acquire. The precedent the plausibility report adds that the earlier sets did not have is that "rework and yield crises often re-emerge during demand upswings" and that TT's own 2024 failure was triggered by a sudden volume increase at Cleveland and Kansas City; the strength the thesis most needs to acquire is therefore not a new capability but the maintenance of an existing one under load, and the first test of it is H2 2026.

What would change the judgement upward: a FY2026 cash conversion print at or above 75% with revenue growing (the first demonstration of cash with growth); an annual-report statement in March 2027 that the five-year plan now supports partial US recognition (the first external underwriting of North American profitability); Cleveland or Kuantan volume disclosed in a way that lets the drop-through be measured. Downward: an H2 2026 EMS margin below 7.5% on rising revenue (the leverage absorbed by execution cost, the 2024 pattern); a trading update before March 2027.

D-03

How diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years' financials?

❌ D47

Question asked In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

Judgement: the drivers are moderately concentrated, and more concentrated than they look, because three of the items the second set listed separately (volume absorption at Cleveland, North American profitability and the adjusted tax rate) are one driver expressed on three lines, and that one driver carries about half of the EPS difference between the forecast and the past; the other half is spread across four independent items (the administrative cost programme, the interest line, Power's defence-driven volume and mix, and the Kuantan lap) of which two are near-mechanical and two are customer-timed. The concentration is less damaging to predictability than it would be at most companies because the concentrated driver is observable twice a year in the EMS divisional margin and once a year in the tax note, and because its failure mode (a US site relapse) is a known pattern with a known early indicator (productivity and rework at Cleveland), but it means the forecast has a single point of failure that the diffuse-looking bridge conceals.

The decomposition, on the FY2028 forecast against the FY2025 base (EPS 17.9p against 6.9p, an 11p difference). Operating profit contributes about 6p (adjusted operating profit £51.5m against £37.2m, £14.3m, taxed and divided): volume absorption £11m to £13m, the cost programme £6m, Power mix £2m, central-cost leverage £1m, less inflation on the fixed base £7m to £8m and the Plano last-time-buy comparator £3.5m. The tax rate contributes about 3.5p (57.1% through 36% to 29%). The interest line about 1p (about £8m to £4.8m). Dilution about −0.5p. Now reassign by independent cause rather than by line. North America (Cleveland's volume into carried fixed cost, perhaps £4m to £6m of the absorption; the removal of Plano's losses already in the base; and the whole of the tax step, which under IAS 12 cannot occur without US profitability): about £5m of operating profit plus 3.5p of tax, roughly 5.5p of the 11p, or half. The cost programme: £6m gross, about 2.5p before the inflation that eats it, near-certain and independent of demand. Power and UK defence (mix, phasing recovery, £4m to £5m of absorption at Bedlington, Barnstaple and Manchester on the Rolls-Royce, MBDA, Typhoon and land-platform work): about 2p, independent of North America, dependent on procurement cadence. Kuantan (£2m to £3m of absorption, the H1 2027 lap): about 1p, independent of the others, dependent on one transferred customer. Interest: 1p, mechanical. Inflation and the comparator: about −4p, spread across all sites and near-certain. Against the AlphaSense concentration report's framing, which counted "three primary operational levers" (Cleveland, Kuantan, tax normalisation) and treated the Components disposal and the macro as diffuse secondary drivers, I count four independent drivers, of which one is compound and dominant. The report's most useful finding, that the tax rate and North American site profitability "represent a single connected driver rather than two independent levers" because deferred-tax recognition is explicitly dependent on forecast US profitability, is the point the second set's bridge does not make explicit and the memo must.

Why the concentration matters, and why it is survivable. If North America stalls (Cleveland productivity flattening, a complex-programme rework episode under rising volume, a Kansas City slip), the forecast loses the Cleveland absorption, the tax rate stays in the mid-to-high 30s, and FY2028 EPS falls toward 13p: that is the second set's downside case and it is produced by one geography. The cost programme, Power, Kuantan and interest would still deliver roughly half of the uplift, which is why the downside is a 25% to 30% EPS shortfall rather than a collapse, and why the company is not a single-driver story in the way Solid State (award timing) or XP Power (semiconductor capital equipment) is. The relevant comparison is discoverIE, whose improvement is spread across 30 businesses and two divisions and whose drivers are diffuse by design; TT sits between discoverIE and Solid State, nearer Solid State on the EPS line and nearer discoverIE on the operating-profit line. The disposal, if it happens, reduces concentration on the revenue side (it removes the ten-week-visibility, highest-gearing division) and increases it on the profit side (Power becomes a larger share of a smaller group), with the net effect of making the drivers fewer and better known.

The other concentration the Consumer should carry is temporal: almost all of the difference lands in two halves (H2 2026 and H1 2027), because the savings annualise, the Plano and Suzhou removals lap, Kuantan's volumes return and Power's deferred contracts land in that window. A forecast whose drivers are concentrated in time is more testable (the March and September 2027 prints settle most of it) and more fragile (a slip of a quarter in Power or Kuantan moves the half-year numbers by more than the annual numbers). What would widen the driver set and reduce concentration: a second site exit, a bolt-on acquisition, or recognition of the tax assets, which would convert the tax driver from a contingent item into a settled one.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

C50

Judgement: the cone is narrow on operating profit for FY2026, moderate on operating profit for FY2027, wide on EPS and cash for FY2027, and widest on the share price; it is skewed positive on profit against consensus, neutral to slightly negative on EPS against consensus (because the tax rate consensus has assumed is below what the company guides), negative on cash against the guided path in FY2026 and positive thereafter, and negative on price against the current multiple. The case is therefore moderately predictable: more predictable than TT's own history (consensus FY2026 EPS has ranged from 8.7p to 23.4p in 18 months), more predictable than Solid State or XP Power at the same stage, and less predictable than discoverIE or Volex, with the predictability concentrated in the next two prints and the width concentrated in the lines below operating profit and in the multiple.

The cone, line by line, with my probability weights (base about 55% to 60%, downside 15% to 20%, upside 20% to 25%, as the second set set them; I keep the weights and tighten the ranges where the evidence allows). FY2026 adjusted operating profit (reported 10 March 2027): £39.5m to £43.5m around £41.5m; consensus £40.0m. H1 of £18.5m is banked; H2 needs £23.0m against a clean H2 2025 comparator of £20.2m and a reported one of £23.7m; the group's 54% to 55% second-half weighting alone gives about £40m; the second £1.5m of savings is in hand; the open items are Power's deferred contracts (worth £1m to £2m), Kuantan's H2 shipments and Components' continued distributor demand. The one assumption in the half that is load-bearing is EMS revenue: the model's H2 2026 of £252m with Plano's £8m gone and Power up only 2% to 3% requires EMS to grow about 12% on H2 2025, almost all of it the transferred customer's volumes returning; consensus's implied H2 of £254m requires more. Probability of a print below £40m about 15%, above £42m about 30%. This is the narrowest part of the cone and the strongest statement in these answers: the FY2026 profit number is more likely to be beaten than missed, and the width is about plus or minus 5%. FY2027 adjusted operating profit (framed in March 2027, half-reported in September 2027, reported March 2028): £41m to £51m around £47.0m; consensus £45.0m. The width comes from volume (plus or minus 5% on revenue moves profit by £6m to £8m at the second set's drop-through), the H1 2027 Kuantan lap (about £10m of revenue and £2m to £3m of profit that depend on one customer's schedule), Power's award cadence (Solid State's precedent is a £38m swing in a year on award timing; TT's Power is better diversified across platforms, so I would put a half-year swing at £5m to £10m of revenue), an input-inflation dip of 30bp to 60bp of gross margin in the half when memory and PCB prices land (H1 2027 the most exposed), and the Components air pocket (a 10% fall in Components costs about £7m of revenue and £1.5m to £2m of profit). The downside drop-through is higher than the upside, so the left tail is fatter: a correlated execution failure (Kuantan plus Cleveland plus a Power slip) produces about £41m to £42m and a 7% to 7.5% margin, the second set's downside; the upside path (Power at 17% on mix, Kuantan early, Components holding) produces about £50m to £51m. Jefferies' published scenarios (90p on an 8.0% FY2027 margin with sales 10% below, 250p on 10.0% with sales 15% above) bracket the same range with a wider revenue swing than I think plausible given the order book. FY2027 EPS: 12.5p to 18.5p around 15.5p; consensus 15.9p. The operating cone above gives about 13.5p to 17p at a 32% rate; the tax rate adds about plus or minus 1p (27% to 35%); the skew against consensus is neutral to slightly negative because consensus's implied rate of 26% to 27% is below the company's guidance and below the evidence of H1 2026. Cash: FY2026 free cash flow (after restructuring) £0m to £12m around £8m; FY2027 £12m to £25m around £20m. The working-capital release in H2 2026 is the widest single item in the next print: H1 absorbed £12.7m on the company's basis, inventory was built in Power ahead of delayed revenue and in Kuantan ahead of transferred volumes, and the guided 70% to 80% needs most of the Power inventory to ship; Volex's 63.9% and Cicor's negative half are the precedents for growth absorbing more than planned. A conversion print below 70% is the most likely way for a good profit print to be received badly in March 2027. Price: the widest cone of all. The 24-month-forward P/E is 10.5x to 10.8x against a five-year median of 7.2x, a ten-year median of 9.8x and a guideline of 13.3x. The first set's sentiment trough (8.5x on unchanged earnings, about 148p, −20%; 7.2x, about 126p, −32%) and the model's 270p at December 2027 span −32% to +45% on a central earnings path. The restocking read-across (discoverIE in December, XP Power in January), the H2 comparator optics in March, DBAY's intentions and the UK small-cap regime move the price independently of the earnings cone; D-07 shows the price has moved 99% on its own news for two years, so these are company events rather than sector flows, but that does not narrow them.

The skew, summarised for the memo. On the fundamentals the skew is positive for the next two prints (profit more likely above than below consensus) and symmetrical thereafter; on the lines that convert profit into shareholder value (tax, cash, multiple) the skew is neutral to negative against what consensus and the price already assume. The historical dispersion the AlphaSense concentration report assembled supports this shape: one year into a site-led margin recovery, companies that depended on two or three internal items plus returning volume (XP Power 2025 to 2026, Volex 2017 to 2019, Kitron's defence transfers) met or beat their operating-profit consensus more often than not and missed on cash and timing more often than not, and the misses came in the second year, when working capital and transition friction arrived with the volume. The width of the cone is therefore a FY2027 property more than a FY2026 one.

The three dated tests that will collapse most of the cone: discoverIE's interims in early December 2026 (the restocking read-across, which moves the price but not TT's numbers); TT's own FY2026 results on 10 March 2027 (cash conversion against 70%, the H2 EMS margin against 8.5%, the Power H2 margin against 16%, the dividend, the FY2027 framing, the deferred-tax statement, the Components process); and the H1 2027 results in September 2027 (the Kuantan lap at +6.5%, the first half of growth with cash). After September 2027 the cone on FY2027 should be about half its present width.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and the key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C52

Question asked How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

Judgement: the disclosure is helpful on mechanisms and unhelpful on cadence and on the three site-level facts that will decide the next print; over the next six months the Consumer will get at most two readings (a probable but unannounced November trading update and the FY2026 results on 10 March 2027), the second of which is rich enough to settle most of the FY2026 cone and the first of which will be a sentence on trading against expectations. Against the question's standard, the disclosure lets the case be monitored at the group level with a half-year lag and at the divisional level with a one-year history, and does not let it be monitored at the level where the risk sits (Cleveland's utilisation, Kuantan's shipping rate, Power's contract terms, the US entities' taxable profit). The practical consequence is that for the next six months the case is monitored mainly through peers, distributors and the company's silence: the absence of a trading update before March 2027 is itself the most informative signal the company can give, because every one of its last three profit warnings came as an unscheduled update in September or November.

What the company discloses that is useful, and how to use it. (1) A company-compiled consensus range, published and guided against; "ahead of market expectations" was measurable (the range was £32.6m to £38.5m) and the next range will be published with the March results, so the Consumer can read the FY2027 framing against a number rather than a mood. (2) Divisional revenue, adjusted operating profit, margin and book-to-bill for Power, EMS and Components, with FY2025 restated, which allows the three divisional tests I set in D-04 (EMS H2 margin against 8.5%, Power H2 margin against 16%, Components order intake against revenue). (3) The group order book and book-to-bill (£550m, 1.12x), with underlying organic growth stated excluding the Plano and Suzhou effects, which strips the self-inflicted noise out of the growth line. (4) Cash conversion on a stated definition with its working-capital cause, the adjusted tax rate with its cause and its normalised level (25.4%, 26.7%), cash tax guided (about £6m for 2026), FX sensitivities per cent and per RMB 0.1, the cost programme sized gross and net with its restructuring cost, capex and development guided (£10m to £13m), net debt ex leases with covenant leverage and interest cover, and the dividend timetable (March 2027). (5) Named operational milestones with numbers attached (Cleveland productivity 57% to 81%, rework 12% to 5%; the Kuantan customer £14m lower year on year in H1; Plano's £3.5m last-time-buy profit), which is more than most small caps give and is why the H2 comparator trap is knowable in advance. (6) Named customer wins (Rolls-Royce lifetime agreement, MBDA letter of intent, Collins' $10.3m programme, two scientific-instrument logos at Cleveland), which allows the defence design-in thesis to be tracked by event.

What it does not disclose, and why each gap matters in the next six months. (1) No revenue or margin guidance for FY2026 or FY2027, no divisional margin guidance, no date for 12%; so the FY2027 framing in March will again be "in line with" a consensus the company itself publishes, and the Consumer will have to build the year from the blocks. (2) No quarterly reporting and no fixed interim trading-update date; the November update exists by precedent (2023, 2024, 2025) and not by policy, and in each of those years it carried bad news or back-ended risk (the 2025 update said £12m of profit had to land in two months). A November 2026 update that says trading is in line with the upgraded expectations would be the most useful disclosure of the six months; its absence would mean the company chose not to issue one, which under this management has been a signal that nothing has changed. (3) No site-level disclosure: Cleveland's spare capacity is "deliberate" but unquantified, its margin is not given, the two new logos have no contract values and "commercial volumes are unlikely to peak before 2027"; Kuantan's shipping rate against transferred volume, headcount and capacity are not given; so the two sites that carry the volume-absorption driver (D-03) cannot be monitored except through the EMS divisional line, which blends them with the UK sites. (4) No order-book conversion schedule (how much of the £550m ships within twelve months), no price and volume split, no statement of the deferred-tax recognition threshold (the annual report says the 2026 to 2030 plan "does not yet" support recognition and that timing depends on taxable profits at reassessment, with no quantitative test disclosed), no Components process timetable or perimeter, and no disclosure of whether the £225.1m of US losses sit in entities that would travel with a Components sale. (5) Divisional history is one year deep, so trend analysis of EMS and Components margins must be rebuilt from the retired regional data, and the FY2026 statutory-to-adjusted bridge (the "first clean margins" test) will be the first on the new basis.

What can be monitored from outside in the six months, in order of usefulness. Volex's interims (November 2026) for North American contract-manufacturing demand and the working-capital behaviour of a grower; discoverIE's interims (early December) for the restocking read-across (its orders lead revenue by six months at a 0.72 correlation, and a disappointment there would be read across to TT regardless of TT's trading); XP Power's fourth-quarter update (January 2027) for the semiconductor-equipment and order-inflation question; Kitron's fourth quarter (February) for European defence electronics award cadence; Morgan Stanley's semiconductor inventory tracker and distributor commentary (Avnet, Arrow) for channel inventory and lead times in passives, which bear directly on the Components order-intake gap (orders +26% against revenue +5.8%); memory and PCB contract prices for the gross-margin dip; GBP/USD for translation (each cent is £0.1m of profit); the US continuing resolution's expiry on 11 December 2026 for Kansas City award timing; UK Defence Investment Plan contract announcements for Power; TR-1 filings for any DBAY move above 30% or below 29%; and Companies House filings of TT's UK subsidiaries (the Fairford accounts filed in October 2026 gave site-level wage data), which arrive with a lag but carry site detail the group accounts do not.

Against peers: Volex guides revenue and profit floors and updates quarterly; discoverIE issues quarterly trading updates with organic order growth; XP Power reports quarterly orders and revenue; Solid State guides revenue "not less than"; Kitron reports quarterly. TT is the least frequent reporter in the set and the richest in mechanism per report; for a six-month monitoring window that combination is unhelpful, because the mechanisms it explains (Plano, Kuantan, Power phasing, tax) are the ones that will resolve inside the window, and the Consumer will learn the resolution only at the end of it. The improvement to ask for, and the thing a new Chair and CFO might add in March, is a dated margin target and a quantified Kuantan and Cleveland utilisation disclosure; neither is promised.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines, and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to EPS?

C55

Question asked How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

Judgement: the internal transmission is clear and sized from statement to operating profit for FY2026, clear in direction but unsized from statement to operating profit for FY2027, and breaks between operating profit and EPS in both years because the two items that convert one into the other (the adjusted tax rate beyond FY2026 and the diluted share count) are either undated or unguided; the external transmission is clear in mechanism for all four value-chain forces that matter (defence procurement, distributor restocking, input-cost inflation, sterling) and opaque in timing for the two that carry the most weight (defence award cadence and the restocking plateau). The result is a case that is highly traceable at the EBIT line over the next two prints and poorly traceable at the EPS line over the same period, which is the inverse of what the market prices (the share trades on a P/E) and is the structural reason the tax rate is where consensus and the company have quietly diverged.

The internal trace, statement by statement. (1) "£3m of net savings in 2026, over £6m annualised from 2027, strictly administrative": traces onto central costs and divisional overhead, sized to the pound, with the restructuring cost (about £3m) and its timing (H1 2026) given; the cleanest trace in the file, and the one the Consumer can verify in the central-cost line (£7.7m in FY2025, £3.7m in H1 2026) and in the EMS and Power overhead. (2) "A return to organic revenue growth in H2 2026, driven by improving order intake across divisions": traces onto group revenue (the lapping of Plano and Suzhou makes H2 growth arithmetic rather than demand-dependent) and, through the fixed base, onto operating profit, but the company gives no drop-through, so the size of the profit effect is the analyst's number (25% to 30%) not the company's. (3) "Kuantan positioned to support higher production volumes in H2": traces onto EMS revenue and margin, direction clear, size unknown (the £14m H1 shortfall is the only quantity given, and how much of it returns in H2 against H1 2027 is not said). (4) "Power's customer agreements finalised, phasing benefits in H2": traces onto Power revenue and margin, with the H1 margin slip quantified (14.2% against 15.4%) and the inventory built ahead of it noted (part of the £13m working-capital outflow), but the programmes, terms and pricing are undisclosed; the AlphaSense Power report adds that the then chief financial officer warned in March 2026 that Power margins would soften in 2026 on the non-repeat of 2025's favourable mix, which means the H2 2026 Power margin the model needs (about 16.7%, against 17.0% in H2 2025) is consistent with "softening" but not with any further slip. (5) "Cash conversion 70% to 80% for 2026, 90%-plus medium term": traces onto working capital, net debt and the interest line, sized; the trace from there to EPS is small (£0.1m of profit per £1m of debt at the blended rate). (6) "Adjusted tax rate mid-to-high 30s for 2026; 25.4% normalised; recognition when the plan supports it": traces onto FY2026 EPS exactly and onto FY2027 EPS not at all; the gap between 36% and 27% is 1.8p, or 12% of FY2027 EPS, and nothing the company has said dates the step. (7) "12% margin, undated; 4% to 6% growth; mid-to-high-teens ROIC; 90%-plus conversion; leverage below 1.5x": the medium-term frame traces onto nothing in the next 18 months, and the company has given no bridge from 9% to 12%; the LTIP's EPS range (5% to 13% a year from a DTA-adjusted 12.0p base) is the only quantified internal ambition and it is below the street. (8) The share count: the company gives the diluted weighted average (181.3m for FY2025) and the awards granted, but FactSet carries 186.4m, and a broker using the FactSet count would understate EPS by about 2.5%; this is a trace-break in the data rather than in the company's statements.

So the chain "statement → driver → line → EPS" is intact for FY2026 at every link except the last fraction of a penny, and for FY2027 it is intact to operating profit (the savings annualise, the lap is arithmetic, the order book covers the revenue) and broken at tax. The memo's topic #6 can present FY2027 operating profit as a traceable company-backed number and must present FY2027 EPS as an analyst's construction with a stated tax assumption.

The external trace, force by force. (1) Rearmament to Power revenue: budgets (UK Defence Investment Plan published 30 June 2026 with £15bn of extra equipment funding; European book-to-bill 135% in 2025) flow to primes, to platform awards (Boxer, Challenger 3, CV-90, Typhoon for Türkiye, Rolls-Royce, MBDA), to TT's order book (Power 1.14x), to revenue on platform schedules. Every link is identifiable and the final link's timing is not: the US continuing resolution to 11 December 2026 restricts new starts and delays sub-tier orders by months; the UK's three-per-cent target was shelved in August 2026 in favour of a £5bn equipment gap; MBDA's letter of intent has no production date; Kitron's third-quarter defence intake fell 30% below consensus in October 2026 on product transitions. The transmission is strong and slow, and a half year can disappoint while the backlog is intact (Solid State's £163m to £125m to £154m). (2) Restocking to Components and distribution revenue: distributor inventories (Morgan Stanley: 42% of distributors pre-buying, channel inventory at its 56-day median, total supply-chain inventory 30 days above median), lead times (microcontrollers 40 to 52 weeks, analog 60 to 70 weeks, PCBs booked into 2028) and order intake (TT Components +26%, XP Power +93% with £18.7m of it pull-forward) transmit to revenue in one to two quarters and reverse as fast; this is the clearest and fastest external trace and the one with the most negative skew into FY2028. (3) Input costs to gross margin: memory contract prices up several-fold, onsemi's 10% to 20% October increase, PCB capacity booked out; the trace is through the bill of materials with a three-to-six-month indexed recovery (80% to 90%) and six to twelve months in fixed-price short-cycle work; size 30bp to 60bp of gross margin in a half, then recovered; percentage margins dilute even when pounds are protected. (4) Sterling: 79% of sales outside sterling, translation unhedged, £0.1m of operating profit per cent on GBP/USD and per RMB 0.1; consensus expects sterling to weaken toward $1.31 to $1.34, a mild tailwind; the trace is exact and the Consumer can run it. (5) The macro and discount-rate regime: no trace to EPS worth modelling (no house forecasts recession, PMIs are expanding); the trace is to the multiple.

What is missing from the external trace that peers provide: discoverIE discloses the order-to-revenue lag statistic; Volex discloses end-market revenue by vertical quarterly; TT discloses end markets annually on a retired basis. The AlphaSense Power report's finding that the division's revenue and margin split by platform is "entirely confidential" and that the Rolls-Royce pricing and escalation terms are undisclosed means the defence trace stops at the division. The Consumer should therefore model the external forces at the group and divisional level, run the sterling sensitivity explicitly, treat defence timing as a half-year phasing risk of £5m to £10m of revenue in either direction, and treat the Components channel as a one-to-two-quarter leading indicator whose reversal would show first in Components order intake falling below revenue.

D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (rolling six months), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C56

Question asked To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

Judgement: the share price has moved almost entirely on its own news for two years, and the multiple is therefore company-driven to a degree that is unusual even for a small cap; over the latest six-month window the equal-weighted peer basket explains 1% of TT's daily variance (correlation 0.07, beta 0.13), TT rose 55.8% while the basket rose 3.7%, and in no month-end window of the last 24 has the basket explained more than 5%. The re-rating from about 5x to 10.5x 24-month-forward earnings happened on TT's own events (Volex's approach, the Cicor offer and its blocking by DBAY, the FY2025 beat, the H1 2026 beat and upgrade, the broker re-entries), not on a sector move, and the sector's own re-rating (Volex at the 92nd percentile of its ten-year P/E range, Senior at the 96th, AQ Group at the 86th, SKF and Trelleborg in the 80s) has run alongside TT's rather than pulling it. For predictability this cuts two ways, and on balance it is mildly positive: the price responds to things the Consumer can forecast (the company's own prints, the disposal, DBAY) rather than to sector flows it cannot, and a sector de-rating would not mechanically drag the multiple down; but there is no sector support in a company-specific disappointment, and a stock whose 95% rise carried an R-squared of 1% can fall the same way.

The evidence, read in order. (1) The latest window (28 April to 23 October 2026): correlation 0.07, beta 0.13, R-squared 1%, idiosyncratic share 99%. No single peer explains more than 2% (SFS Group 0.13 correlation; Senior shows a beta of 1.05 on a correlation of 0.10, which is a high-variance artefact rather than a link). discoverIE, the closest listed comparator and the cohort's restocking bellwether, has a correlation of 0.03. (2) The 24-month path: from November 2024 to April 2025 the correlation was negative (−0.15 to −0.18, beta −0.6 to −1.0): TT fell on its own profit warnings and the North American write-downs while the basket rose, which is anti-correlation driven by company events. From May 2025 the sign turned positive but stayed small (0.04 to 0.16), with the beta rising to about 1.0 in October and November 2025 during the Cicor offer period and the DBAY counter-moves, then falling back; the highest R-squared in the series (5%, June 2026) coincided with the cohort-wide restocking rally of May to August 2026, which is the only period in which TT traded with its sector at all, and the September 2026 upgrade broke the link again (R-squared 0% at end-September). (3) The multiple's own history: 24-month-forward P/E 10.5x against a five-year median of 7.2x (93rd percentile) and a ten-year median of 9.8x; the dashboard's current-P/E percentile of 53 on ten years says the stock is at the middle of its longer history and the top of its recent one. Against peers it is the cheapest in the cohort (discoverIE 17.3x, Volex 14.5x, Senior 21.8x, SFS 16.5x), and its discount to them has not closed as it re-rated, which is consistent with the co-movement data: the market has repriced TT's own earnings path, not its sector position.

Why the co-movement is so low, and the caveats. Three company-specific regimes dominated the window: a bid saga with a blocking shareholder (which detaches a price from fundamentals and from peers), a profit-warning cycle and its reversal (idiosyncratic by definition), and a thin market (about $1m a day, 427,000 shares) in which non-synchronous trading and bid-ask bounce depress measured daily correlation for any stock. The basket is also heterogeneous (MTU Aero Engines, Bekaert and Coats have little to do with high-mix electronics) and lacks SKF and Trelleborg prices. A weekly or monthly return window would show more co-movement than the daily one, and the restocking cohort (discoverIE, XP Power, Volex) would show more than the full basket; even so, the direction of the finding is robust, because TT's return over six months (+56%) against the basket's (+4%) cannot be explained by any beta.

What it means for the memo. (1) The fair multiple (topic #11) should be set from TT's own phase and its own range, not from peer parity; the cohort's 14.5x to 18.5x is not a gravitational pull on TT's multiple over 18 months, and the sector's own elevated percentiles are not a support. (2) The near-term price risk the first set identified (a 20% to 35% de-rating on sentiment) is a company-event risk: the H2 comparator optics in March, a cash print below 70%, a dividend not reinstated, a DBAY move; the sector's restocking test in December would affect TT through the narrative rather than through the beta, and the co-movement data say that channel has been weak. (3) The multiple is more trackable than at a sector-driven stock: the Consumer can map it to the company's own milestones (the first clean results, the disposal, recognition) and expect it to move on them. (4) The one sector linkage that does operate is corporate: Volex and Cicor have both bid, DBAY blocks, and the sector digest's rule that cleaned-up mid-caps are sold is the mechanism by which the sector's valuation (Cicor's 8.3x EV/EBITA offer; Volex at 14.5x earnings) reaches TT, through a bid rather than through co-movement. That is a discrete, company-timed event, which is the same conclusion in a different form.
